# 16. Aspire Orchestration & Service Defaults

This chapter covers the **hosting boundary**: how the distributed MMCA system is *composed and run*, locally as a single `dotnet run`, and in Azure Container Apps (ACA) as a set of independent revisions. Four distinct concerns live side by side here, and it pays to separate them up front. **AppHost-side** code (`MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting`, `MMCA.ADC.AppHost`) is the *orchestrator*: it declares the resource graph, containers, databases, broker, the four services, the gateway, the UI, and wires their dependencies. **Service-side** code (`MMCA.Common.Aspire`) is the *baseline every running process opts into*: OpenTelemetry, health checks, service discovery, HTTP resilience, Kestrel listener profiles, startup warm-up, Redis wiring, Serilog, a startup tripwire that fails a host whose configuration asks for a cloud-vendor capability no adapter call registered, and hardened security headers. **Edge-side** code (`MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway`) is the small kit a reverse-proxy host adds on top: correlation, an edge rate limiter, and per-downstream readiness. **YARP building blocks** (`MMCA.Common.Gateway`) are the fourth: cluster request profiles, destination health-check defaults, route/cluster trace headers, named per-route limiter policies, header hygiene on the proxied request, an opt-in connect-failure retry, and the forwarded-headers step, all applied on top of whatever route table the host loaded. The AppHost and service assemblies are deliberately kept apart so a running service never drags in the full `Aspire.Hosting` tooling graph (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:14-18`), and the service-side package carries exactly one first-party project reference, `MMCA.Common.Shared`, taken solely so the resilience constants have one home (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/MMCA.Common.Aspire.csproj:57-59`). The gateway package goes further: it has no project reference at all and one runtime package, YARP (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/MMCA.Common.Gateway.csproj:15`), because the host it serves has no application container to share. ADC has no app-local ServiceDefaults project: all four services, the Gateway, and the Blazor UI host consume `MMCA.Common.Aspire`'s `AddServiceDefaults()` directly (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:117`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:114`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:98`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:101`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:77`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:65`). Everything in this group is plumbing, but it is the plumbing that makes [ADR-006](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/006-database-per-service.html) (database-per-service), [ADR-008](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/008-service-extraction-topology.html) (service topology + YARP), [ADR-009](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/009-resilience-and-recovery-objectives.html) (resilience / RTO-RPO), and [ADR-041](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/041-observability-and-telemetry.html) (observability and telemetry) *real* at runtime rather than aspirational.

## The orchestrator: declaring the resource graph

When you run `dotnet run --project Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost`, Aspire executes `Program.cs` top to bottom, building a *resource model* rather than starting anything immediately. It declares a persistent SQL Server container (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:15`), four databases on it (`ADC_Identity` / `ADC_Conference` / `ADC_Engagement` / `ADC_Notification`, `Program.cs:37-40`), Redis (`:44`), a broker chosen in one call at `:98`, and a MailDev SMTP container (`:106`), then the four service projects, each wired by one `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` call (`:137`, `:159`, `:197`, `:222`), the YARP Gateway with its HTTPS endpoint pinned to port `6001` (`:364`), and the Blazor UI pinned to `6002` (`:394`). All of the cross-cutting wiring vocabulary lives in one reusable, cross-app namespace, `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting`, whose core is the [Extensions](#extensions) static class (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:24`). It supplies the fluent helpers, grouped into C# `extension(T)` blocks by the resource type they attach to (primer [§4](00-primer.md#4-c-build-and-code-style-conventions)): the three provisioning helpers `AddMailDev` (`:201`), `AddMessageBroker` (`:220`) and `AddServiceBusEmulatorBroker` (`:261`); the two `WithBroker` overloads (`:312` for RabbitMQ, `:341` for the emulator); `WithJwksDiscovery` (`:370`); the five CI and local-run switches `WithE2eRsaKeys` (`:414`), `WithE2eRegistrationThrottleLift` (`:456`), `WithE2eForceServer` (`:490`), `WithE2eForceWebAssembly` (`:514`) and `WithE2eGatewayRateLimitLift` (`:548`); and the per-engine data-source helpers `WithSQLServerDataSource` (`:587`), `WithPostgreSQLDataSource` (`:617`), `WithCosmosDataSource` (`:646`) and `WithSqliteDataSource` (`:671`), the last three added for polyglot persistence ([ADR-018](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/018-polyglot-persistence.html)).

`.WithSQLServerDataSource(db, "Conference")` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:587`) is the AppHost manifestation of **[ADR-006](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/006-database-per-service.html)**: in one fluent chain it adds `.WithReference(database)`, `.WaitFor(database)`, and injects a single connection-string env var, `DataSources__{logicalName}__SQLServerConnectionString` (`Extensions.cs:594-597`). That one entry is the whole configuration: with no top-level connection string the single database a host declares this way also becomes its `Default` source, so the framework's own tables and the readiness health check both resolve from it, and the logical name collapses onto `Default`, leaving one context, one change tracker and one migration set per service (the method's own doc comment, `:571-582`). The routing types that consume those env vars, [DataSourceResolver](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#datasourceresolver) and [EntityDataSourceRegistry](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#entitydatasourceregistry), live in the persistence group. The ADC AppHost does not call it directly: it is the first link of the shared per-service chain below (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:74`), which ADC applies once per service (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:137,159,197,222`). This paragraph is where **`[Rubric §8, Data Architecture]`** (each service owns its store, with no shared write path) meets **`[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`** (the topology is a declaration in one file, not a rewrite).

The per-service chain is itself framework code, and so is the front door. [ServiceInfrastructureExtensions](#serviceinfrastructureextensions) (Level 2, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:16`) exposes one method, `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure(database, logicalName, cache, mailServer, attachBroker, profile, probeEndpointName)` (`:57`), which applies, in order, the service's own database, a reference to the cache, the selected broker through the delegate `AddSelectedBroker` returns, a wait on the cache and a wait on the mail server (`:73-78`), then the startup probe its [ServiceEndpointProfile](#serviceendpointprofile) argument picks (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceEndpointProfile.cs:7`): `Http2Only` (`:13`) gets `WithH2cHealthCheck` on the `http` endpoint unless another is named, and `Mixed` (`:19`) gets Aspire's stock `WithHttpHealthCheck`, both on `/alive` (`ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:80-84`). Its remarks fix three rules every host inherits (`:27-43`): service endpoints stay internal because a backend is reached through the gateway, the probe is always liveness and never readiness, and no project resource is referenced, so the project-to-project edge set is exactly what the host declares itself. [GatewayUiExtensions](#gatewayuiextensions) (Level 2, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/GatewayUiExtensions.cs:16`) is the front-door twin: `WithMmcaGateway(httpsPort, services)` (`:41`) references and waits for each service, marks the gateway's endpoints external, probes `/alive` and pins its `https` port (`:48-57`); `WithMmcaUi(gateway, httpsPort, services)` (`:82`) does the same for the UI head over the gateway but gates on `/health/ready` (`:91-103`), which is safe only because nothing waits for the UI (`:70-76`); and `WithGatewayApiEndpoints(gateway)` (`:122`) sets both `Api__ApiEndpoint` and `Api__WasmApiEndpoint` to the gateway's `https` endpoint (`:127-131`), the second because it is the URL the browser is handed and a browser cannot resolve a service-discovery name, the first because a SignalR `HubConnection` builds its own message handler and never sees the discovery handler (`:110-118`). [IdentityLinkExtensions](#identitylinkextensions) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/IdentityLinkExtensions.cs:23`) hands Identity the addresses it cannot know itself: `WithUiLinks(ui)` (`:74`) roots the OAuth redirect base and the password-reset, email-confirmation and sign-in links at the UI's `https` endpoint (`:79-91`), and `WithIssuer(gateway)` (`:100`) sets `Jwt__Issuer` to the gateway's (`:105`). This package references none of the packages that own those settings, so the section and property names are internal mirrors (`:25-50`) that the hosting tests cross-assert against their owners (`:12-17`), and `EnvironmentKey` (`:61-62`) turns a section and property into the `__` form configuration binds. [ServiceTokenExtensions](#servicetokenextensions) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceTokenExtensions.cs:22`) does the same for the local gRPC service-token signing key (ADR-061, `:8-16`): `AddServiceTokenSigningKey()` (`:46`) adds a secret parameter that, when configuration supplies none, is generated (64 letters and digits) and persisted to the AppHost's user secrets (`:51-55`) under the slot existing AppHosts already used (`DefaultSigningKeyParameterName`, `:28`), and `WithServiceTokenSigningKey(signingKey)` (`:67`) hands that parameter to one service as `Grpc__ServiceToken__SigningKey` (`:72-74`). [MmcaReplicas](#mmcareplicas) (Level 4, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/MmcaReplicas.cs:24`) is the local multi-replica opt-in: `FromEnvironment(variableName)` (`:33`) reads a count that falls back to 1 when unset, unparsable, or 1 or less (`:37-43`), and `WithMmcaReplicas(replicas, distributedRateLimiting)` (`:63`) is a no-op at 1 and otherwise runs the service at that many replicas, adding `RateLimiting__Distributed=true` when asked so a per-user limit does not multiply by the replica count (`:67-76`). Its remarks state the limit of what such a run proves: Aspire's proxy balances per TCP connection, so pooled HTTP/2 connections tend to pin to one replica, and a run proves correctness under concurrency, not capacity (`:14-18`). ADC applies all of them: the token key to Identity, Engagement and Notification (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:244-247`), `ADC_REPLICAS` to the four services with the distributed limiter and to the gateway without it, whose edge limiters stay per replica (`:449-454`), and the gateway API endpoints and the UI links last (`:469`, `:477`).

The remaining AppHost helpers complete that vocabulary. `AddMessageBroker()` (`Extensions.cs:220`) provisions RabbitMQ with the management plugin in a single call (`:224`), defaulting the resource name to the `DefaultBrokerResourceName` constant, `rabbitmq` (`:29`); `WithBroker(broker)` (`:312`) attaches a service to it with `.WithReference(broker)`, `.WaitFor(broker)` and `MessageBus__Provider=RabbitMq` (`:318-321`), the env var that the consuming service's `AddBrokerMessaging` reads to select RabbitMQ over [InProcessMessageBus](group-04-events-outbox.md#inprocessmessagebus) (selection driven by [MessageBusProviders](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebusproviders) / [MessageBusSettings](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebussettings), [ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html)). `AddMailDev()` (`:201`) provisions the local SMTP sink from an image pinned to `3.0.0` (`MailDevImageTag`, `:178`) with a persistent lifetime so the captured inbox survives an AppHost restart (`:207`), on two *fixed* host ports, `1080` for the web UI and `1025` for SMTP (`:208-209`), because a developer opens the first by hand and every consumer's `Smtp:Port` setting names the second (`:187-191`). The two numbers live in [MailDevDefaults](#maildevdefaults) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Build/MailDev/MailDevDefaults.cs:17`, `HttpPort` at `:20`, `SmtpPort` at `:23`), re-exposed as `MailDevHttpPort` and `MailDevSmtpPort` (`Extensions.cs:163,169`): one internal source file compiled into both this package and `MMCA.Common.Testing.E2E` through a linked `Compile Include`, because a shared reference would make the E2E package depend on the Aspire hosting SDK, or the hosting package on Playwright, for two integers (`MailDevDefaults.cs:10-16`). `WithJwksDiscovery(identity, gateway?)` (`Extensions.cs:370`) references and waits for Identity (`:377-379`) and sets `Authentication__JwtBearer__Authority` (`:396`) so the consuming service validates RS256 tokens against Identity's published keys ([IJwksProvider](group-08-auth.md#ijwksprovider) / [RsaJwksProvider](group-08-auth.md#rsajwksprovider)). The non-trivial part, captured in a long inline comment (`:382-392`), is that it prefers the *gateway* endpoint over Identity's (`:393-395`): the three REST services run **HTTP/2-only on cleartext (h2c)** so gRPC clients can use prior-knowledge negotiation, but the default `JwtBearer` backchannel `HttpClient` speaks HTTP/1.1, which Kestrel rejects on an HTTP/2-only endpoint. Routing the JWKS and `/.well-known/*` fetch through the gateway (which terminates TLS and supports both protocols via ALPN, [ADR-008](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/008-service-extraction-topology.html)) makes the default backchannel work end-to-end without weakening the services (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:373-375`). The other half of that contract is `identityService.WithIssuer(gateway)` (`Program.cs:385`, the [IdentityLinkExtensions](#identitylinkextensions) helper above): the issuer Identity stamps into every token it signs is derived from the same gateway endpoint, so an Aspire run cannot mint wrong-issuer tokens if the pinned port ever moves. That is the runtime embodiment of the **cross-service token validation** boundary in [ADR-004](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/004-authentication-dual-fetch.html), with the transport caveat spelled out in [ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html).

The E2E switches are the CI-shaped siblings, and every one is a no-op unless its trigger variable is set, so ADC calls all five unconditionally (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:231,409,417,423,430`). `WithE2eRsaKeys()` (`Extensions.cs:414`) forwards an ephemeral RSA keypair from `E2E_JWT_PRIVATE_KEY_PEM` / `E2E_JWT_PUBLIC_KEY_PEM` (read at `:418-419`) onto Identity's `Jwt__RsaPrivateKeyPem` / `Jwt__RsaPublicKeyPem` / `Jwks__RsaPublicKeyPem` (`:425-428`), returning untouched when either variable is absent (`:420-423`), so locally and in production user-secrets or Key Vault supply the keys. `WithE2eRegistrationThrottleLift()` (`:456`) raises Identity's per-IP registration cap to the `E2eRegistrationsPerIpPerHour` constant of 1000 (`:36`) and its per-IP auth limiter to `E2eAuthIpPermitLimit`, 100000 (`:94`), injected as `LoginProtection__MaxRegistrationsPerIpPerHour` and `RateLimiting__AuthIpPermitLimit` (`:462-470`): a Playwright suite registers far more than ten accounts from one localhost IP, and with passkeys offered every visit to the login page spends one auth-ip permit before the login spends another, so without the lift the anti-abuse controls refuse the suite and the failures look like broken registration (`:432-447`). `WithE2eGatewayRateLimitLift()` (`:548`) is the gateway-side twin: it raises the edge limiter to 100000 permits and 10000 concurrent requests and the named `auth-tight` route policy to 100000 (`:75`, `:81`, `:87`, injected at `:559-568`), because the whole suite arrives from one loopback IP and both the per-IP window and the anti-credential-stuffing policy read it as the flood they exist to stop (`:531-537`). Both lifts fire on `E2E_LIFT_REGISTRATION_THROTTLE` (`:43`) or on a forced-WebAssembly run, which implies the same volume (`:460`, `:552`). Note what those env-var keys are built from: the package's own internal constants (`RateLimitingSection` at `:102`, `GatewayRateLimitingSection` at `:112`, `MmcaGatewaySection` at `:119`, `AuthTightPolicyName` at `:126`) rather than the settings types, which this AppHost-tier package does not reference. The UI pair picks the render mode for a run. `WithE2eForceServer()` (`:490`) sets `E2E__ForceServer=true` (`E2eForceServerConfigurationKey`, `:55`) when `E2E_FORCE_SERVER` (`:49`) reads true, pinning the UI host to InteractiveServer because under InteractiveAuto the post-login page load switches to the background-downloaded WASM runtime, whose boot on a shared 2-core CI runner exceeds every suite wait (`:477-481`). `WithE2eForceWebAssembly()` (`:514`) is the local-run opposite: it sets `E2E__ForceWebAssembly=true` (`:69`) when `E2E_FORCE_WASM` (`:63`) is true, so every interactive page boots the WebAssembly runtime and the client render path is exercised end to end (`:502-506`, written at `:518-520`). When both are set, forced WebAssembly wins, because the force-server switch then writes nothing (`:494`). The PostgreSQL, Cosmos and SQLite siblings (`Extensions.cs:617,646,671`) inject the equivalent per-engine connection-string env vars ([ADR-018](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/018-polyglot-persistence.html)) and are layered on top of, not instead of, the SQL Server source; the ADC AppHost wires none of them.

## Broker parity: RabbitMQ by default, the Service Bus emulator on demand

RabbitMQ is a different broker from the one production runs, so anything that only misbehaves on Azure Service Bus (entity-name limits, topic and subscription provisioning, scheduled redelivery pacing) is invisible in the inner loop. `AddServiceBusEmulatorBroker(sqlServer)` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:261`) closes that divergence for a host that opts in: it declares a [ServiceBusEmulatorResource](#servicebusemulatorresource) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceBusEmulatorResource.cs:32`) from the official image pinned to tag `2.0.1` (`Extensions.cs:150,274-275`), publishes both of the emulator's planes (AMQP on container port 5672 and the HTTP management plane on 5300, `ServiceBusEmulatorResource.cs:41,44`, wired at `Extensions.cs:276-282`), accepts the EULA the image demands (`:287`), points it at the **existing** SQL Server resource for its own state rather than starting a second engine (`:288-293`), and waits for that SQL Server because the emulator's first act is to create its schema (`:295-297`). The image tag floor is not cosmetic: the HTTP management plane shipped in emulator 2.0.0, and the Azure Service Bus transport provisions its own topology when its receiver starts, so a broker with no admin plane is not usable at all (`ServiceBusEmulatorResource.cs:11-15`). The resource's `ConnectionStringExpression` ends in `UseDevelopmentEmulator=true` (`:66-68`), the marker `MMCA.Common.Azure`'s Service Bus transport detects to take its emulator branch, which a real connection string never carries (`:18-23`), and `AdminEndpointExpression` (`:81-83`) is the second address that transport needs to build its administration client on the emulator (`:70-74`). The matching `WithBroker` overload (`Extensions.cs:341`) sets `MessageBus__Provider=AzureServiceBus` plus those two settings as `MessageBus__ConnectionString` and `MessageBus__EmulatorAdminEndpoint` (`:347-352`). One deliberate gap is recorded in the resource's own doc comment (`ServiceBusEmulatorResource.cs:25-30`): it declares no health check, so a `WaitFor` on it gates on the container running rather than on the emulator finishing warm-up, and the consuming service absorbs the remainder because the transport's receiver provisions and connects in the background, retrying, rather than failing host startup.

The choice itself is framework code as well. [BrokerSelection](#brokerselection) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/BrokerSelection.cs:25`) owns everything except the variable's name, which stays in the app's AppHost (`:13-19`). `IsServiceBusSelected(environmentVariableName)` (`:36`) answers true only when that variable reads the `ServiceBusSelection` constant, `servicebus` (`:28`), compared case-insensitively (`:40-43`), so unset or any other value keeps RabbitMQ. Because the two `WithBroker` overloads take different resource types, the choice cannot be one variable handed to one call, so `AddSelectedBroker(environmentVariableName, sqlServer)` (`:58`), in an `extension(IDistributedApplicationBuilder)` block, provisions the selected broker once (the emulator over the existing SQL Server at `:65-69`, otherwise `AddMessageBroker()` with a persistent container lifetime so the broker survives an AppHost restart, `:71-73`) and returns an attach delegate, which `WithSelectedBroker(attach)` (`:82`), in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>)` block, simply invokes (`:86`). ADC makes the choice in one line, `builder.AddSelectedBroker("ADC_BROKER", sqlServer)` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:98`, rationale in the comment block from `:69`), and hands the delegate to every service's `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` call (`Program.cs:137,159,197,222`), which applies it as the single `.WithSelectedBroker(attachBroker)` link of the shared chain (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:76`); that is what stops a fifth service from quietly being wired to the wrong broker. **`[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`** (environment parity available on demand, at a cost the everyday inner loop does not pay).

## Startup ordering, health-gated waits, and the gRPC deadlock-avoidance trick

Aspire's `WaitFor` builds a startup dependency graph from the resource model: a service does not start until the resources it waits on report healthy. What "healthy" means for a project resource is the health check that resource carries, and this is where the h2c profile bites a second time. Aspire's stock `WithHttpHealthCheck` probes with a default `HttpClient`, which sends HTTP/1.1, and an `HttpProtocols.Http2` Kestrel endpoint answers that with GOAWAY `HTTP_1_1_REQUIRED` rather than the health payload, so the check never turns healthy and every `WaitFor` edge into that resource degrades to "the process started". [H2cHealthCheckExtensions](#h2chealthcheckextensions) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/H2cHealthCheckExtensions.cs:42`) fixes that with `WithH2cHealthCheck(path, endpointName)` (`:111`), which registers an [H2cEndpointHealthCheck](#h2cendpointhealthcheck) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/H2cEndpointHealthCheck.cs:23`) against the named endpoint and then associates it with the resource (`:128-137`, both halves being required for a `WaitFor` to gate). Three details carry the design. The default path is `/alive`, not `/health/ready` (`:54`): a startup gate must probe liveness, because a readiness endpoint aggregates downstream and warm-up checks and gating startup on it can deadlock the graph. The probe budget is 2 seconds (`:67`), short because Aspire polls for as long as a dependent resource is waiting. And the endpoint URL is resolved on every check rather than captured at registration (`H2cEndpointHealthCheck.cs:71-72,108`), with an unallocated endpoint reported as the failure status rather than thrown or passed (`:110-120`), because Aspire only allocates the endpoint once the resource starts and a pre-allocation poll must not release the wait. The shared probe client's keep-alive ping trio (`:46-52`) is load-bearing rather than tuning: Aspire's endpoint proxy listens before the target Kestrel does, so a first probe can open a connection that is accepted and never answered, and without the pings every later probe queues onto that one zombie connection forever. [H2cHealthCheckRegistry](#h2chealthcheckregistry) (Level 0, same file `:154`) makes a repeat registration a no-op: `IServiceCollection` offers no way to ask which health checks exist and a duplicate key is a startup exception, so `GetOrAdd` (`:185`) attaches one ledger to the AppHost's own collection and `TryClaim` (`:168`) skips a key an earlier call took.

ADC applies both probe shapes through that `ServiceEndpointProfile` argument and explains the split in a long comment block (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:325-347`): Identity, Conference and Engagement pass `Http2Only` (`:222`, `:197`, `:159`) and so take `WithH2cHealthCheck()`, because they run Kestrel Http2-only on cleartext, while Notification passes `Mixed` (`:137`) and takes the stock `WithHttpHealthCheck("/alive")`, because it runs the mixed `Http1AndHttp2` profile. Every *service* gate is `/alive`, never `/health/ready`, and the reason is a startup cycle: service readiness includes the warm-up gate, whose OIDC task fetches discovery through the gateway, but the gateway waits for the services (`:342-345`). The gateway resource itself stays on `/alive` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/GatewayUiExtensions.cs:56`, applied by `WithMmcaGateway` at `Program.cs:364`) in the sharpest form of the same rule, because its readiness aggregate includes the per-downstream probes and one unreachable downstream would stop `ui.WaitFor(gateway)` from ever releasing (`GatewayUiExtensions.cs:28-35`). Only the UI, which nothing waits for, gates on `/health/ready` (`GatewayUiExtensions.cs:102`, applied by `WithMmcaUi` at `Program.cs:394`). One further subtlety worth internalizing: Conference and Engagement form a **bidirectional gRPC pair**, and a reciprocal `WaitFor` would deadlock, so the AppHost deliberately omits the reverse edge (`Program.cs:276`, reasoning at `:259` and `:275`) and lets transient "peer not ready" failures self-heal via the Polly pipeline baked into `AddTypedGrpcClient`. The same judgement is applied to the other reverse-direction edges: Engagement to Notification carries no `WaitFor` because the publish is fire-and-forget (`:284`, reasoning from `:281`), Identity's two export edges and its feedback edge to Conference carry none because Identity is the leaf the other services already wait on (`:294-295`, `:300`, reasoning at `:271` and `:289-291`), and Notification's edge to Conference deliberately carries none either (`:304-308`). The typed-client and interceptor machinery, [JwtForwardingClientInterceptor](group-13-grpc-contracts.md#jwtforwardingclientinterceptor) and [GrpcResultExceptionInterceptor](group-13-grpc-contracts.md#grpcresultexceptioninterceptor), lives in the gRPC group ([ADR-007](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/007-grpc-extraction.html)).

## The service baseline: AddServiceDefaults()

Every running host calls one method early in `Program.cs`: `AddServiceDefaults()` from the single framework-grade [Extensions](#extensions-1) in `MMCA.Common.Aspire` (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:23`, a `partial` class whose health-check and telemetry halves live in `Extensions.Health.cs` and `Extensions.Telemetry.cs` beside it). There is no ADC-local copy. `AddServiceDefaults()` (`Extensions.cs:35`, declared in an `extension<TBuilder>(TBuilder builder)` block constrained to `IHostApplicationBuilder` at `:24-25`) is four lines of composition, `ConfigureOpenTelemetry()`, `AddDefaultHealthChecks()`, `AddWarmupReadiness()`, `AddServiceDiscovery()` (`:35-38`), then the cloud-adapter tripwire described below, registered as an options validator behind `ValidateOnStart` so it runs with the other startup validators (`:40-46`), followed by one `ConfigureHttpClientDefaults` block (`:50`) that applies to *every* `HttpClient` the host later creates: typed clients, named clients, the YARP forwarder, and the gateway's own downstream probe clients alike. That block installs the standard Polly pipeline with its values read from `HttpResilienceDefaults` (`:60-65`) and switches its one retry off for `POST` and `PATCH` (`:74`): an attempt that timed out may still be running server-side, and most hops carry no idempotency key, so replaying one risks a duplicate write. `GET`, `PUT`, `DELETE` and the other idempotent verbs keep their retry, which is why the code names the two verbs rather than calling `DisableForUnsafeHttpMethods`, a helper that would also strip it from `PUT` and `DELETE` (`:67-73`). It then wraps the retry's `ShouldHandle` so a `429` whose `Retry-After` asks for longer than the client is willing to wait is not retried at all (`:76-84`, decided by `RetryAfterHeader.ExceedsRetryBudget`, below): left alone, the standard handler honors the header, and a rate limiter sets it to the rest of its window, so one refused action would hold a request for up to a minute. The block then re-adds service discovery to the handler chain (`:86`) and replaces the primary handler with a tuned `SocketsHttpHandler` (`:99-107`, rationale comment at `:88-98`). The handler is the clearest **`[Rubric §31, Cost & FinOps]`** decision in the codebase: recycling pooled connections picks up ACA replica DNS rollover without a restart, holding idle connections in the pool avoids paying TCP and TLS handshakes on every low-traffic inter-service call, and socket-level keep-alive pings keep the TCP connection warm *without* generating HTTP traffic, so an idle replica stays on idle-vCPU billing (documented in-code as roughly 8x cheaper than active). `EnableMultipleHttp2Connections = true` (`:106`) keeps a single multiplexed HTTP/2 connection from becoming the bottleneck for the gRPC edges above.

## One source of truth for resilience numbers

The numbers that pipeline uses are not literals in the Aspire package. They live in [HttpResilienceDefaults](#httpresiliencedefaults) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/HttpResilienceDefaults.cs:12`), a static class of read-only properties in `MMCA.Common.Shared`: a 30 second per-attempt timeout (`:16`), a 60 second circuit-breaker sampling window (`:19`), a 90 second total request timeout including retries (`:22`), **one** retry beyond the initial attempt (`:33`), an 8 second ceiling on how long a client waits out a `429`'s `Retry-After` before retrying (`MaxRetryAfterWait`, `:41`, the longest step of the UI retry backoff), a 10 minute pooled-connection lifetime (`:47`), a 5 minute pooled idle timeout (`:50`), and 60 second / 30 second keep-alive ping delay and timeout (`:53,56`). It sits in `Shared` for a layer reason: `MMCA.Common.Aspire`, the UI service base classes and `MMCA.Common.Grpc` may only depend on `Shared`, and before this type existed each package hand-mirrored the values and drifted (10s/30s library defaults on the gRPC side against the tuned 30s/90s on the HTTP side, stated in the doc comment at `:3-12`). The retry budget is the interesting constant: it is pinned at one deliberately (`:24-32`) because the UI service base classes already own user-facing retries (their policy makes up to 4 attempts), and a full retry budget re-applied at every hop turns a backend brownout into an up-to-16x request storm at exactly the wrong moment. The same remarks state the mutation rule both clients honor: the HTTP clients refuse the retry for POST and PATCH, and the gRPC clients (all POST) retry only a failure to reach the service (`:30-31`).

The same file carries the east-west gRPC path's own values under a `Grpc` prefix (`HttpResilienceDefaults.cs:57-93`), which is what the typed gRPC clients in `MMCA.Common.Grpc` read (group 13), so one file answers "what timeout does this client get" for every HTTP-based client. The gRPC timeouts are sized from measured latency rather than inherited: an 8 second per-attempt timeout (`GrpcAttemptTimeout`, `:73`, about ten times the slowest measured east-west p99 of 784ms) and a 20 second total covering two attempts plus the backoff (`GrpcTotalRequestTimeout`, `:76`), because an in-cluster call answers in well under a second and the 30s/90s outbound budget would hold a caller on a hung peer for a minute and a half (`:58-62`). The retry budget and the sampling window are shared with the HTTP path, while the breaker shape is stated explicitly, a 0.5 failure ratio (`:82`), a minimum throughput of 10 calls (`:88`) and a 10 second break, about one container-replica restart (`:94`), because an east-west call addresses a peer directly and bypasses the gateway's active health checks, so the breaker is the only thing that notices a peer going bad (`:62-65`). A consumer whose edge is legitimately slower overrides these per client through `AddTypedGrpcClient`'s `configureResilience` callback (`:65-66`).

[RetryAfterHeader](#retryafterheader) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/RetryAfterHeader.cs:15`) sits beside it and owns both ends of the `Retry-After` contract on a `429` (`:6-14`). The server half, `Format` (`:28`), turns a rejected lease's retry-after hint into whole seconds, rounded up so a client never retries before the window has moved on, and never negative (`:30-31`); it takes the `TimeSpan` hint rather than the lease because `System.Threading.RateLimiting` ships in the ASP.NET Core shared framework, which this package (also loaded by Blazor WebAssembly) does not reference, so a lease without the hint, a concurrency-limiter rejection for one, sends its 429 without the header (`:17-24`). The client half, `TryGetDelay` (`:43`), reads the header in either form, a delta in seconds or an HTTP date measured against an injected `TimeProvider`, clamping anything already past to zero (`:48-63`), and `ExceedsRetryBudget` (`:75`) is the predicate the `ConfigureHttpClientDefaults` retry consults: true only for a `429` that carries the header and asks for more than `MaxRetryAfterWait` (`:76-78`), so a refused user action costs one request rather than a held connection. Keeping it in `Shared` on pure BCL types is what lets the API, Aspire and UI.Web limiters and the UI and Aspire retry policies apply one rule (`:10-12`); the gateway's edge limiter below writes its header through it directly, and `MMCA.Common.Gateway`, which references no MMCA package, carries a test-pinned internal copy of the server half (`:12-13`).

[BrokerResilienceDefaults](#brokerresiliencedefaults) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/BrokerResilienceDefaults.cs:25`) is the third member of the folder and does the same job for the *other* outbound transport: the circuit breaker guarding the outbox's broker-publish path. Four properties, a 0.5 failure ratio (`:32`), a minimum throughput of 10 attempts (`:40`), a 30 second sampling window (`:47`), and a 15 second break duration (`:55`), are read by [OutboxProcessor](group-04-events-outbox.md#outboxprocessor) in `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure`. Its doc comment carries the two decisions worth reading (`BrokerResilienceDefaults.cs:8-23`): a breaker is needed at all because a broker outage makes each of a 50-row batch's publishes wait out its own transport timeout, so one cycle can spend minutes discovering nothing; and it is deliberately **not** paired with a Polly retry, because the outbox already owns retry (`RetryCount`, exponential backoff with jitter, then dead-letter) and a second policy would make a row's effective attempt count an accident of two independent budgets. Two constants files and one header rule, every outbound transport, no drift: **`[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`** ([ADR-009](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/009-resilience-and-recovery-objectives.html)) and **`[Rubric §29, Resilience, Reliability & Business Continuity]`**.

## Listeners and probes: one Kestrel profile per host shape

Before a service can answer a health probe it has to be listening on a protocol the prober speaks, and that is not free when the same port serves inbound gRPC. [KestrelEndpointExtensions](#kestrelendpointextensions) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Kestrel/KestrelEndpointExtensions.cs:24`) turns both deployed profiles into a single call, `ConfigureEndpointsWithHealthProbe(defaultProtocols, redeclareCleartextEndpoint, cleartextPort)` (`:77`). It applies the requested protocols to every Kestrel endpoint default and then declares the explicit listeners computed by `BuildListenerPlan` (`:84-98`, plan at `:113`). The plan is empty when `HealthProbe:Port` (`:31`) is unconfigured (`:119-122`), which is exactly the local and integration-test case: no explicit `Listen` call, so Aspire's dynamic ports keep working and two co-hosted services cannot collide. When the deployment injects the port, the plan is one or two [KestrelListenerSpec](#kestrellistenerspec) records (`:132`), each a `(Port, Protocols)` pair: an `Http1`-only listener on the probe port, preceded by a re-declaration of the main cleartext listener (default `8080`, `:37`) when `redeclareCleartextEndpoint` is left at its default (`:124-126`), because an explicit `Listen` call otherwise overrides the container's `ASPNETCORE_HTTP_PORTS` binding entirely. The reason for the second listener is operational and documented at the top of the file (`:8-22`): ACA `httpGet` probes speak HTTP/1.1, which an Http2-only endpoint rejects with GOAWAY `HTTP_1_1_REQUIRED`, which is why those probes used to be TCP-only and never consulted the real dependency-aware health checks. A dedicated Http1 listener on a port that is never published through ingress gives the platform a real target, since `MapDefaultEndpoints()` maps the health routes on every listener. ADC's three REST services pass `HttpProtocols.Http2` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:88`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:86`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:70`) and the SignalR-hosting Notification service passes `Http1AndHttp2` with `redeclareCleartextEndpoint: false` because its endpoints come from configuration (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:73`). This is [ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html) made concrete, and it tags **`[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]`**.

## Health checks: liveness, readiness, and the "optional" tag

`MapDefaultEndpoints()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.Health.cs:143`) exposes the three-probe surface the platform reads: `/health` (every check, for humans and dashboards, `:134`), `/alive` (only checks tagged `live`, `:138-141`), and `/health/ready` (`:154-156`). None of those three paths is a literal at the mapping site: they come from [HealthEndpointPaths](#healthendpointpaths) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/HealthEndpointPaths.cs:8`), a constants holder carrying `Health` (`:11`), `Alive` (`:14`) and `Ready` (`:17`) plus one predicate, `IsProbePath` (`:29`), which answers case-insensitively for `/alive`, `/health`, and anything below `/health/`, so a sub-route a host adds later is covered without a second edit (`:30-33`). The type exists to keep the mapping, the probe telemetry filters and the gateway's downstream probe from drifting apart (`:3-7`). Every ADC host maps it: the four services through the shared `UseMmcaServiceHostAsync` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:376`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:316`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:248`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:145`), which calls `MapDefaultEndpoints()` once the schema is migrated (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.ServiceHost/ServiceHostApplicationExtensions.cs:56`), the gateway directly (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:195`), and the UI host through `UseMmcaBlazorWebPipeline` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:128-130`, mapped at `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI.Web/Hosting/BlazorWebHostExtensions.cs:199`). The tag vocabulary is a named type rather than string literals, [HealthCheckTags](#healthchecktags) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/HealthCheckTags.cs:6`), with three constants: `Live` (`:12`), `Ready` (`:18`), and `Optional` (`:32`). Read the readiness predicate carefully, because it encodes a hard-won operational rule: it excludes both `live` and `optional` (`Extensions.Health.cs:176`). Excluding `live` keeps a downstream SQL outage from restarting the container. Excluding `optional` is the subtler half, argued at length in both the tag's own doc comment (`HealthCheckTags.cs:20-31`) and the endpoint comment (`Extensions.Health.cs:168-173`): a dependency the app degrades gracefully without (a distributed cache behind an in-memory fallback, a broker behind a retrying outbox) must not gate readiness, because making it readiness-fatal converts a partial degradation into a total outage when every replica goes unready at once. Those checks still surface on `/health`, so the degradation stays visible without being self-inflicted.

The two aggregating probes are not computed per request. `/health` and `/health/ready` are both mapped through `MapCachedHealthChecks(path, predicate)` (`Extensions.Health.cs:192-220`), which answers exactly as `MapHealthChecks` does (the status name as `text/plain`, `503` only when unhealthy, `:182-189`) but serves the report from [CachedHealthReportProvider](#cachedhealthreportprovider) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/CachedHealthReportProvider.cs:25`), keyed by path so the full report and the readiness subset never share a result (`Extensions.Health.cs:189`, `:179`). The window comes from [HealthReportCacheOptions](#healthreportcacheoptions) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/HealthReportCacheOptions.cs:29`): one property, `CacheSeconds`, defaulting to 5 and range-validated to 0-300, with `0` restoring probe-per-request behavior (`:37-38`), bound from the `HealthChecks` section (`:31`). Read the reason in its remarks (`:10-22`), because it is an availability finding rather than a performance tweak: both endpoints are anonymous and sit on the always-bypassed rate-limit prefix, and executing every registered check per request turned N anonymous requests into N x (services + database + cache) backend operations, a `SELECT 1` on a pooled SQL connection, a Redis `PING`, a broker check, and on a gateway one outbound `/alive` per fronted service, which exhausts the SQL pool without an account. Caching the *report* bounds that amplification at one probe round per window regardless of inbound rate, which a bypass list alone cannot do. `/alive` is deliberately left uncached (`Extensions.Health.cs:152-153`, `HealthReportCacheOptions.cs:20-23`): it runs only the `self` check, costs nothing, and is what the platform restarts a container on, so it must answer for the current instant. The provider keeps one private `Entry` (`CachedHealthReportProvider.cs:33`) per endpoint key, a cached report, its expiry, and a `SemaphoreSlim` refresh slot (`:35-41`), and the single-flight rule is the subtle part: a caller that cannot take the slot serves the **stale** report rather than queueing (`:75-81`), so a slow dependency cannot convert a probe flood into a request backlog; only the very first call after startup, with nothing to serve, waits for the round in progress (`:83-85`), and the winner re-checks for a freshly published report before probing again (`:89-93`). The clock is an injected `TimeProvider` (`:28`, registered with `TryAddSingleton(TimeProvider.System)` at `Extensions.Health.cs:37`) so the window is testable without waiting.

`AddDefaultHealthChecks()` (`Extensions.Health.cs:25`) registers the baseline `self` check tagged `Live` (`:28`) and, alongside it, the cache: the options bound with `ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` (`:33-36`) and the provider as a singleton (`:38`). `AddInfrastructureHealthChecks(requireDatabase)` (`:76`) adds the dependency probes, every one registered with the per-check timeout read from `HealthChecks:CheckTimeoutSeconds`, 5 seconds when absent (`:79`, `:287-294`). The relational half is engine-agnostic and multi-source: `AddDatabaseHealthChecks` (`:237`) asks `RelationalSources` (`:326`) for every distinct database the host declares on each of the three relational engines, SQL Server, PostgreSQL and SQLite (`:243-245`), across both the top-level `ConnectionStrings` section and every named `DataSources` entry, then registers one untagged check per database (`:255-284`). Untagged means they *do* gate readiness: readiness for a host that owns several databases means every database it serves from is reachable. The `requireDatabase` asymmetry is deliberate and documented (`Extensions.Health.cs:57-75`): a host that cannot resolve its own database is misconfigured, so no relational engine at all throws at startup (`:247-253`), the same fail-fast posture as [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html); Redis and RabbitMQ are optional per host and are added only when their connection string is present, tagged `Optional` (`Extensions.Health.cs:83-100`, `:103-123`), so the same binary runs unchanged in an integration-test environment where those containers are absent. Every one of those checks is Common's own code rather than a health-check package registration. [SqlServerHealthCheck](#sqlserverhealthcheck) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/SqlServerHealthCheck.cs:16`) and [SqliteHealthCheck](#sqlitehealthcheck) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/SqliteHealthCheck.cs:15`) open a connection and run `SELECT 1` under the check's cancellation token (`SqlServerHealthCheck.cs:37-46`, `SqliteHealthCheck.cs:36-45`), rethrow only a cancellation the caller asked for and report anything else as the failure status (`SqlServerHealthCheck.cs:52-56`); they hold no state, because the driver's pool keyed by the connection string carries the reuse, so one instance is shared across probes (`SqlServerHealthCheck.cs:9-14`, created once per database at `Extensions.Health.cs:259`, `:282`). The SQL Server check uses the same `Microsoft.Data.SqlClient` build Infrastructure connects with, so a connection string that authenticates for the contexts authenticates here too (`SqlServerHealthCheck.cs:12-13`). [PostgreSqlHealthCheck](#postgresqlhealthcheck) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/PostgreSqlHealthCheck.cs:26`) is the one with a twist: `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure` registers a keyed `NpgsqlDataSource` singleton, keyed by the connection string, once a data-source configurator is added (RDS IAM tokens, for one), and a check built from the bare connection string would authenticate without that password provider and report a healthy database as down (`:12-17`). This package cannot reference Infrastructure, so the check decides per probe, because the two registrations can arrive in either order: it probes the shared data source when the keyed lookup finds one, and otherwise a data source of its own, built on first use, kept for every later probe and disposed with the check, never disposing the shared one (`:20-24`, the probe at `:55`). It is registered as a keyed singleton per check name so that owned data source is built once (`Extensions.Health.cs:265-277`). The broker check splits the same way between a probe and a holder. [RabbitMqHealthCheck](#rabbitmqhealthcheck) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/RabbitMqHealthCheck.cs:15`) opens and closes one channel on the connection that [RabbitMqHealthConnection](#rabbitmqhealthconnection) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/RabbitMqHealthConnection.cs:22`) hands it (`RabbitMqHealthCheck.cs:36-39`) and never opens or disposes that connection itself (`:9-12`). The holder exists because opening a connection per probe round would leave one AMQP connection on the broker every successful round: it keeps at most one, replaces it only when it is gone or no longer open, creates it single-flight under its own token on the check-timeout budget so a port that accepts TCP and never answers cannot hang the handshake, and closes it when the host stops (`RabbitMqHealthConnection.cs:6-16`, registered as a singleton at `Extensions.Health.cs:116` from the `rabbitmq` or `messaging` connection string, `:103-106`). All four ADC services pass `requireDatabase: true` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:222`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:158`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:152`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:137`).

The Redis branch is worth reading as a case study in why the tag rules exist, because it was learned in production. [RedisCachingExtensions](#rediscachingextensions) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Caching/RedisCachingExtensions.cs:39`) is the framework-owned way to wire a host to Redis: `AddRedisCaching(connectionName)` (`:69`) registers the distributed cache and the `IConnectionMultiplexer` client with `DisableHealthChecks = true` on both (`:76-77`), and `AddRedisOutputCaching(connectionName)` (`:103`) backs the ASP.NET Core output cache with the same instance so tag eviction crosses replicas (`:111`). Both are no-ops when the connection string is absent (`:71`, `:106`). The reason for the wrapper is stated in its own doc comment (`:10-24`): Aspire's Redis integrations register the `AspNetCore.HealthChecks.Redis` check with **no tags**, an untagged check silently gates readiness, and that check issues `CLUSTER INFO` against anything StackExchange.Redis 3.x detects as clustered, which is how it sees Azure Managed Redis (Enterprise tier). The server refuses that command outside admin mode, so every probe threw against a healthy cache, every replica reported not ready, and the platform stopped routing traffic the applications could serve. The fix is structural rather than a tag patch on someone else's registration: the Aspire checks are switched off at the source and Common contributes [RedisPingHealthCheck](#redispinghealthcheck) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Health/RedisPingHealthCheck.cs:27`), which issues nothing but `PING` (`:53`), resolves the multiplexer from DI (`:48`) and only lazily builds its own behind a semaphore when the host registered no client (`:31`, `:102-105`), reports rather than throws on any failure (`:61-73`), and is registered once as a singleton tagged `Optional` (`Extensions.Health.cs:92-100`). The two connections this package opens outside Aspire's Redis client, the output-cache store (`RedisCachingExtensions.cs:118`) and that fallback multiplexer (`RedisPingHealthCheck.cs:102`), both go through [RedisConfigurationOptionsPipeline](#redisconfigurationoptionspipeline) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Caching/RedisConfigurationOptionsPipeline.cs:19`). Its `CreateConfigured` (`:29`) parses the connection string and runs every registered `IPostConfigureOptions<ConfigurationOptions>` over it (`:33-45`), the same post-configure steps Aspire's client options go through, and returns null when none is registered so the caller keeps its plain connection-string path (`:34-37`). That is how a Redis configurator, the Entra token configurator of `MMCA.Common.Azure` for one, reaches those connections without this package referencing Infrastructure: both sides name only the StackExchange.Redis type and the options abstractions (`:12-16`). ADC's four services call `AddRedisCaching()` (`.../Conference.Service/Program.cs:185`, `.../Identity.Service/Program.cs:130`, `.../Engagement.Service/Program.cs:108`, `.../Notification.Service/Program.cs:111`), and Conference additionally calls `AddRedisOutputCaching()` (`:196`). This is **`[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]`** (the probe contract the deployment platform consumes) and **`[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`** made concrete; the readiness revision is recorded in [ADR-025](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/025-startup-warmup-readiness.html).

The optional-tag rule is also proven against real outages, in an opt-in ADC tier that runs the whole AppHost. [AdcOutageAppHostFixture](#adcoutageapphostfixture) (Level 3, `MMCA.ADC/Tests/Integration/MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests/AdcOutageAppHostFixture.cs:17`) boots the real ADC AppHost over the shipped `AppHostFixtureBase` and runs only with the opt-in variable, a container runtime and a valid developer certificate, a missing precondition making every test skip with the gate's sentence rather than hang (`:10-16`, `:60-63`). It clears `ADC_BROKER` so the stack selects RabbitMQ, since the Service Bus emulator has no stop-and-start story this tier can assert (`:52-57`), allows 12 minutes to start and 8 to turn healthy (`:69-70`), awaits only Identity, Notification and the gateway, all liveness-gated so the wait cannot deadlock on a dependency a test takes away (`:72-77`), and shortens Identity's outbox retry, lease and polling so the outbox outlasts an outage (`:93-96`). [AdcOutageCollection](#adcoutagecollection) (Level 4, `MMCA.ADC/Tests/Integration/MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests/AdcOutageCollection.cs:9`) turns parallelization off, because an outage test stops a container the whole stack shares (`:3-8`). [AdcDependencyOutageTests](#adcdependencyoutagetests) (Level 5, `MMCA.ADC/Tests/Integration/MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests/AdcDependencyOutageTests.cs:22`) derives from the shipped `DependencyOutageTestBase` and probes Notification through two scenarios: a Redis outage (`:26`) and a broker outage (`:45`) each fail `/health` but keep readiness, because those checks are tagged optional, and recover once the container starts again (`:7-12`). The broker scenario also registers an attendee through the gateway while RabbitMQ is down, so the `UserRegistered` event parks in Identity's outbox, which the base asserts pending and, after the broker returns, drained (`:65-70`, `:73-96`). There is deliberately no SQL Server scenario: an Aspire start recreates a stopped container with empty databases and the services migrate only at start, so a "recovered" readiness would be asserted over a missing schema (`:14-19`).

## Telemetry: what gets exported, and what it costs

`ConfigureOpenTelemetry()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.Telemetry.cs:70`) wires logging with formatted messages and scopes (`:72-76`), metrics, and tracing (`:78-80`). Metrics are configured by a private `ConfigureMetrics(metrics, configuration)` (`:239`), which first hands the ASP.NET Core instrumentation to `ConfigureAspNetCoreMetrics` (`:241`, defined at `:351`) and then adds ten framework meters by **literal name**, `MMCA.Common.Outbox`, `MMCA.Common.Cqrs`, `MMCA.Common.Idempotency`, `MMCA.Common.Scheduler`, `MMCA.Common.Broker`, `MMCA.Common.OutputCache`, `MMCA.Common.BestEffort`, `MMCA.Common.InternalCommands`, `MMCA.Common.Persistence`, and the optional AI package's `MMCA.Common.AI` (the `AiTelemetryName` constant at `:43`) (`:305-314`), because the Aspire package has no reference to the assemblies that define them (its only project reference is `Shared`), plus Polly's own `Polly` meter (`:321`, the `PollyMeterName` constant at `:30`). Tracing adds the host's own application-name source plus the `MMCA.Common.Outbox`, `MMCA.Common.InternalCommands` and `MMCA.Common.AI` activity sources, again as literals (`:82-85`). Several of those meters are inert until a host opts into the feature behind them, the scheduler in a host that never enables `Scheduler:Enabled`, the broker meter in a host that stays on the in-process bus, and the AI meter until a host adds that package and enables `Ai:Enabled`, which the in-code comment states explicitly (`:293-304`). Four cost levers hang off this method, and all four fail safe; a fifth, `Telemetry:EnablePollyDurationMetrics` (the key is a named constant at `:24`, read by `IsInstrumentationEnabled` at `:217-218`), inverts the default the other way and drops the two Polly duration histograms unless a host asks for them (`:323-339`). First, [OutboxPollFilterProcessor](#outboxpollfilterprocessor) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Telemetry/OutboxPollFilterProcessor.cs:17`) is registered via `.AddProcessor(...)` (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:124`): a `BaseProcessor<Activity>` whose `OnEnd` (`OutboxPollFilterProcessor.cs:32`) walks each ending span's in-process parent chain (`:37`), matches on *both* operation name and source name so an unrelated span called `OutboxPoll` is not swept up (`:39-40`, the two literals pinned as private constants at `:23-24`), and clears `ActivityTraceFlags.Recorded` (`:45`) so the batch exporters skip it. It must be registered before the exporters, including any vendor adapter's exporter added after `AddServiceDefaults()`, so its `OnEnd` runs first (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:117-123`), and it returns rather than throws on a null activity (`OutboxPollFilterProcessor.cs:29-33`), because a telemetry callback must never take the process down. Real per-message `OutboxProcess` work restores an explicit parent context and is never a poll descendant, so genuine outbox telemetry survives (the poll machinery, [OutboxProcessor](group-04-events-outbox.md#outboxprocessor), [IOutboxSignal](group-04-events-outbox.md#ioutboxsignal), and [OutboxMessage](group-04-events-outbox.md#outboxmessage), lives in the events and outbox group, [ADR-003](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/003-outbox-dual-dispatch.html)). Second, `TryGetTraceSampleRatio` (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:182`) reads an optional `Telemetry:TracesSampleRatio` and, only when it parses inside the open interval `(0,1)` (`:186-190`), installs a `ParentBasedSampler(TraceIdRatioBasedSampler(...))` (`:139-140`) for head-based sampling; a typo, a blank, or an out-of-range value falls back to sampling everything, so a mistake can never silently blackhole all telemetry, and `ParentBased` keeps a sampled-in trace intact across service boundaries. Third, `IsInstrumentationDisabled` (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:205-206`) backs three opt-in kill switches, `Telemetry:DisableAspNetCoreMetrics` (`:353`), `Telemetry:DisableHttpClientMetrics` (`:250`) and `Telemetry:DisableRuntimeMetrics` (`:278`), which drop the highest-volume metric families on a low-traffic multi-service deployment. The ASP.NET Core family (`http.server.*`, `kestrel.*`, `aspnetcore.*`, `signalr.server.*`) is the largest of the three: its doc comment records it as 73% of both production workspaces' ingestion over one week, mostly gauges re-emitted on replicas serving no traffic, with no alert reading any of it, since request latency and failures alert off the request traces (`:342-347`). Note *how* they drop them: skipping the `Add*Instrumentation` call is not enough, because a deployed host may also wire the Azure Monitor distro through the `MMCA.Common.Azure` adapter, and that distro adds the `System.Net.Http` and `System.Runtime` meters itself, so each branch installs a `MetricStreamConfiguration.Drop` **View** over the whole meter instead (`:266`, `:285`, and for ASP.NET Core one `Microsoft.AspNetCore.` prefix match at `:358-361`; reasoning from `:254` and at `:355-357`). A View applies to the MeterProvider regardless of which component added the meter, which is what makes the toggle authoritative rather than advisory. Unset keeps everything, so a host that does not opt in sees no behavior change. Fourth, and unlike the other three on by default, `Telemetry:FilterProbeTelemetry` (the key is a named constant, `Extensions.Telemetry.cs:18`, read by `IsProbeTelemetryFilterEnabled` at `:229-230`) keeps health-probe traffic out of trace export: absent, blank or unparseable all mean filter, and only an explicit boolean `false` turns it off for a host debugging its own probes (`:220-230`). It takes two passes, because a probe request and its dependency children are sampled independently. [ProbeTelemetryFilter](#probetelemetryfilter) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Telemetry/ProbeTelemetryFilter.cs:27`) supplies the two instrumentation predicates, wired onto the **default-named** ASP.NET Core and HttpClient options (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:100-109`) so they also govern the instrumentation a vendor distro such as Azure Monitor adds, which is what makes them authoritative without a View. `ShouldCollectRequest` (`ProbeTelemetryFilter.cs:40`) refuses an inbound probe request and on its way out stamps an `mmca.probe` tag (`:33`, `:51`) on the current server activity; `ShouldCollectOutgoing` (`:62`) refuses the outbound probe calls that have no inbound parent to inherit from, the gateway's [DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck) calls to each backend's `/alive` and YARP's active health checks, both driven by background timers (`:20-25`). The marker exists because refusing the request makes the instrumentation return before it writes `url.path`, leaving descendants with no way to recognize their probe ancestor (`:12-19`). [ProbeTelemetryFilterProcessor](#probetelemetryfilterprocessor) (Level 2, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Telemetry/ProbeTelemetryFilterProcessor.cs:20`) is the second pass, registered only when the knob is on and, like the outbox one, ahead of the exporters (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:126-132`): `OnStart` (`:29`) and `OnEnd` (`:40`) run the same walk up the in-process parent chain (`:52`) and, on a probe ancestor, clear both `ActivityTraceFlags.Recorded` and `IsAllDataRequested` (`:59-60`), so the database check's `SELECT 1`, the Redis `PING` and the gateway's `/alive` call never reach an exporter. A parent counts as a probe when it carries the marker tag (`:68`) or is a **server** span whose `url.path`, `http.route`, or route-suffixed display name is a probe path (`:76-79`); server kind only, so a normal request never loses its whole subtree because one dependency happened to be a probe (`:73-75`). Both passes return rather than throw on a null activity (`:44-48`), and running at end as well as at start is deliberate, since a client span is started before its instrumentation has written any attribute (`:32-39`). Metrics are untouched by design, so probe traffic stays on dashboards while leaving the trace bill (`:14-17`); the volume is concrete, probe requests accounted for every `AppRequests` row in both production workspaces and their children for most of the `AppDependencies` volume (`ProbeTelemetryFilter.cs:8-11`, `ProbeTelemetryFilterProcessor.cs:10-12`). Exporters are equally conditional, and the core ships only the vendor-neutral one: `AddOpenTelemetryExporters` (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:164`) enables **OTLP** when `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` is present (the local Aspire dashboard, `:165-171`). A vendor exporter is an adapter package's registration made *after* `AddServiceDefaults()`, `AddAzureMonitorExporter()` from `MMCA.Common.Azure` for one, so the filter processors above still run ahead of its batch processor, and both exporters can be active at once (`:152-162`). `ConfigureOpenTelemetry()` leaves a `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` [HostIntegrationMarker](#hostintegrationmarker) behind (`:145-147`), the receipt that adapter checks for so it refuses to run ahead of this call. ADC's services and Gateway each add the Azure Monitor exporter just after `AddServiceDefaults()` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:117,122`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:114,119`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:98,103`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:101,106`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:77,82`). The whole shape is [ADR-041](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/041-observability-and-telemetry.html): instrument where auto-instrumentation is blind, then expose cost knobs whose defaults never go dark. **`[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`** and **`[Rubric §31, Cost & FinOps]`**.

One logging detail belongs to this boundary rather than to the telemetry pipeline itself. [SerilogHostExtensions](#seriloghostextensions) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Logging/SerilogHostExtensions.cs:27`) carries the bootstrap every service host used to repeat by hand: `AddCommonSerilog(logFilePath, configure)` (`:48`) builds the framework configuration (`CreateLoggerConfiguration` at `:98`: minimum level Debug in Development and Information elsewhere via `ResolveMinimumLevel` at `:76`, EF Core and ASP.NET Core overridden to Warning at `:108-109`, console always at `:110`, a daily rolling file sink everywhere except Production via `ShouldWriteFileSink` at `:87`), publishes it as the global `Log.Logger`, and registers it with `builder.Logging.AddSerilog(...)` (`:54-55`). That last line is the load-bearing part, stated in the type's doc comment (`:16-21`): `UseSerilog()` replaces the whole `ILoggerFactory` and silently bypasses every other provider, including the OpenTelemetry one that carries logs to Azure Monitor once `AddAzureMonitorExporter()` is wired, so a host that calls it publishes no application log line to App Insights at all. `AddSerilog` adds Serilog alongside the others instead. `CreateBootstrapLoggerFactory()` (`:67`) covers the startup diagnostics a host needs before the DI container exists, module discovery above all. All four ADC services call `AddCommonSerilog` ahead of `AddServiceDefaults()`, with only the `MMCA.Common.Azure` vault configuration source between them (`.../Conference.Service/Program.cs:103,117`, `.../Identity.Service/Program.cs:101,114`, `.../Engagement.Service/Program.cs:85,98`, `.../Notification.Service/Program.cs:88,101`).

## Warm-up: defeating ACA cold-start

The warm-up subsystem exists for one concrete failure mode: the "first request fails, second succeeds" pattern on a CPU-throttled idle ACA replica, where lazy initialization (OIDC discovery fetch, connection-pool establishment, JIT) stretches past a client timeout. `AddWarmupReadiness()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:130`, folded into `AddServiceDefaults` at `:34`) registers four cooperating pieces. [IWarmupTask](#iwarmuptask) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Warmup/IWarmupTask.cs:9`) is the unit of startup work: a `Name` for logs (`:12`) plus `ExecuteAsync` (`:15`). [WarmupHostedService](#warmuphostedservice) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Warmup/WarmupHostedService.cs:28`) is a `BackgroundService` that runs *all* registered tasks in parallel exactly once (`:53`) and then opens the gate **in a `finally` block** (`:56-60`), so even when a task throws, the replica is never wedged permanently out of rotation. Each task additionally runs under a 120 second ceiling applied with `WaitAsync` over an injectable `TimeProvider` (`:42,44-45,69`): a task that neither completes nor throws used to leave `Task.WhenAll` pending forever and the gate closed with it, and the timeout turns that case into the same log-and-continue path as a failure (`:77-82`), which closes the one gap [ADR-025](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/025-startup-warmup-readiness.html) originally recorded as open. A per-task `catch` logs a genuine failure at Warning and lets the others proceed (`:84-88`), while a real host-shutdown cancellation is rethrown (`:73-76`). [WarmupReadinessGate](#warmupreadinessgate) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Warmup/WarmupReadinessGate.cs:10`) is a thread-safe one-shot flag (`Volatile.Read` at `:15`, `Interlocked.Exchange` at `:18`, over an `int`), consumed by [WarmupReadinessHealthCheck](#warmupreadinesshealthcheck) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Warmup/WarmupReadinessHealthCheck.cs:9`), which is registered tagged `HealthCheckTags.Ready` (`Extensions.cs:137-138`) and reports `Unhealthy` until the gate opens (`WarmupReadinessHealthCheck.cs:14-16`). That is exactly what makes `/health/ready` hold ingress traffic off a still-warming replica.

Two task shapes ship with the framework. [OpenIdConnectMetadataWarmupTask](#openidconnectmetadatawarmuptask) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Warmup/OpenIdConnectMetadataWarmupTask.cs:25`) is registered unconditionally by `AddWarmupReadiness` (`Extensions.cs:135`): it reads `Authentication:JwtBearer:Authority` (`:30`, the same key the AppHost's `WithJwksDiscovery` injects), returns quietly when it is unset (`:31-34`), warns and returns when it is not a valid absolute URI (`:36-43`), and otherwise GETs `{authority}/.well-known/openid-configuration` through the shared `IHttpClientFactory` (`:45-46`) to warm DNS, TCP, TLS, and the connection pool. Its remarks state the honest caveat (`:14-20`): the `JwtBearer` middleware caches discovery state separately and still performs its own first fetch, but now over a warm connection. [SelfHttpWarmupTaskBase](#selfhttpwarmuptaskbase) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Warmup/SelfHttpWarmupTaskBase.cs:28`) is the abstract base for the deeper variant: after awaiting `ApplicationStarted` (`:102`, implemented at `:191`, because the warm-up runner starts before Kestrel is listening) it replays a subclass-supplied list of `WarmupPaths` (`:59`) against this host's own bound cleartext port, resolved from the server's addresses feature with `ASPNETCORE_URLS` and port 8080 as fallbacks (`ResolveWarmupPort`, `:157`, default at `:39`). Three `virtual` members are the extension points that make it usable on every host shape: `RequestVersion` and `RequestVersionPolicy` default to HTTP/2 pinned exactly (`:70,77`), because an Http2-only cleartext endpoint rejects a silently downgraded HTTP/1.1 request, and `RequireSuccessStatusCode` (`:90`) can be turned off so an intentional 401 on an `[Authorize]` route still counts (the refusal traverses Kestrel, routing, and authentication, which is the JIT cost being paid down). The whole task is skipped under the `Testing` environment (`:46,95-98`), where `WebApplicationFactory`'s in-memory server never opens a socket. Services register their own tasks via `AddWarmupTask<TTask>()` (`Extensions.cs:153`): ADC wires [SelfHttpOutputCacheWarmupTask](group-20-conference-api-grpc.md#selfhttpoutputcachewarmuptask) in Conference (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:320`) and a [SelfHttpWarmupTask](group-22-engagement-module.md#selfhttpwarmuptask) in Engagement and Identity (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:166`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:182`). This whole subsystem is the decision recorded in **[ADR-025](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/025-startup-warmup-readiness.html)**: warm-up is wired into `AddServiceDefaults` so every host gets it, the gate opens even when a task fails or times out (availability over strict warmth), and the missed work is re-paid as a lazy retry on the first real request through the resilience pipeline. Tag-wise it leans on **`[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`** and **`[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]`**.

## Cloud adapters and the startup tripwire

Connection strings, RSA signing keys, OAuth client secrets, the Application Insights exporter and a multi-replica DataProtection key ring all have to reach a deployed process, and none of them is wired by this group: the core packages reference no cloud vendor SDK, and each vendor capability is one registration call in an adapter package (`MMCA.Common.Azure`, `MMCA.Common.Aws`, `MMCA.Common.Gcp`), documented in the cloud provider adapters chapter ([AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions](group-28-common-cloud-provider-adapters.md#azurekeyvaultconfigurationextensions), [AzureDataProtectionExtensions](group-28-common-cloud-provider-adapters.md#azuredataprotectionextensions)) under [ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html). What stays here is the check that the adapter call was made. Without it, a deployment that still sets `KeyVault:Uri` but no longer calls `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration()` would start cleanly with every vault secret missing, or ship no telemetry at all, and look healthy while doing it (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:6-12`).

The mechanism is four small types in `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Integrations`. [HostIntegrationMarker](#hostintegrationmarker) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationMarker.cs:10`) is a registration receipt: a sealed class carrying one `Name` (`:13`) that an adapter's host-level registration adds to the service collection, because the core never names an adapter type and so can only check a well-known string (`:3-8`). [HostIntegrationNames](#hostintegrationnames) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationNames.cs:8`) is that vocabulary: `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` (`:14`), the one the core registers itself from `ConfigureOpenTelemetry()` (`Extensions.Telemetry.cs:148`), and eight adapter names, Azure's `AzureMonitorExporter`, `AzureKeyVaultConfiguration` and `AzureDataProtection` (`HostIntegrationNames.cs:17,20,23`), AWS's Secrets Manager source and key ring (`:26,29`), and Google Cloud's Secret Manager source, key ring and telemetry exporter (`:32,35,38`). [HostIntegrationTripwire](#hostintegrationtripwire) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:15`) is an internal `IValidateOptions` that collects every marker's name into a set (`:56`) and, in `Validate` (`:59`), pairs eight configuration keys with the receipt each requires (`:63-102`): `APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING` (`:20`) with the Azure Monitor exporter, `KeyVault:Uri` (`:23`) with the vault source, `DataProtection:BlobStorageUri` (`:26`) with the Azure key ring, and the AWS and Google Cloud equivalents (`:29-46`). A key that is set without its receipt adds a failure naming the missing call and its consequence (`Check` at `:117-123`, where set means non-whitespace, `:125`). One rule is not about a missing call: setting more than one of the three key-ring gates (`:49-54`) also fails, because whichever registration runs last owns the repository, and services that disagree on the order mint cookies the others cannot decrypt (`:104-112`). Every failure is returned together rather than only the first (`:114`). [HostIntegrationTripwireOptions](#hostintegrationtripwireoptions) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwireOptions.cs:8`) is an empty internal options type whose only job is to carry the tripwire through `ValidateOnStart` (`:3-7`): `AddServiceDefaults()` registers it with `AddOptions<HostIntegrationTripwireOptions>().ValidateOnStart()` plus a `TryAddEnumerable` of the validator (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:50-52`), so the check runs with the other startup validators, before any hosted service starts. This is the [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html) fail-fast posture applied to the vendor boundary. ADC's four services, Gateway and Blazor UI host call `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration()` ahead of `AddServiceDefaults()`, because that call reads configuration eagerly itself and must see vault values too (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Conference.Service/Program.cs:115`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:112`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Engagement.Service/Program.cs:96`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Notification.Service/Program.cs:99`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:75`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:63`), and the two hosts that mint cookies and antiforgery tokens, Identity and the UI, call `AddAzureDataProtection()` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Services/MMCA.ADC.Identity.Service/Program.cs:127`, `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:72`). **`[Rubric §11, Security]`** and **`[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]`**.

## Security headers and CORS at the host edge

The next boundary in this group hardens the request and response edge. The shared response-header middleware is defined in `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Security/SecurityHeaders.cs`. [SecurityHeadersSettings](#securityheaderssettings) (Level 0, `:19`) holds the strongly-typed values bound from the `"SecurityHeaders"` section (`:22`): `FrameOptions` defaulting to `DENY` (`:25`), `ReferrerPolicy` (`:28`), `PermissionsPolicy` (`:31`), HSTS opt-out and value (`:34,51`), an enforce-versus-report-only switch (`:72`), and a complete hardened **CSP baseline** (`:67-69`) that ships `script-src 'self' 'wasm-unsafe-eval'` and `style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'` at exactly the strength Blazor and MudBlazor require, so an HTML host that never registers a provider still gets a functional policy rather than one silently missing both directives, while the JSON, WebSocket and static responses of API and gateway hosts are unaffected (`:53-66`). It also carries `CredentialPathPrefixes` (`:48`), defaulting to `/reset-password` and `/auth/oauth-complete`: the pages a credential arrives on, whose URL must not travel onward in a `Referer` header or sit in a browser or proxy cache after the token is spent (`:36-46`). That list is guarded by [LeadingSlashPathPrefixesAttribute](#leadingslashpathprefixesattribute) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Security/LeadingSlashPathPrefixesAttribute.cs:13`), an internal `ValidationAttribute` that rejects every non-blank entry not starting with `/` and names all the offenders in one message (`:25-31`), skipping blanks as the matchers do. The reason is in its doc comment (`:5-11`): `PathString` refuses a non-empty value without a leading slash, so an entry such as `"hubs"` would otherwise throw on every request that reaches the matcher instead of failing once at startup, the [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html) posture.
