[ICspPolicyProvider](#icsppolicyprovider) (Level 1, `SecurityHeaders.cs:86`) is the per-host CSP extension point, with [StaticCspPolicyProvider](#staticcsppolicyprovider) (Level 2, `:93`) as the default that resolves the configured policy once in its constructor (`:97-104`) and returns it for every request (`:106`); a Blazor host registers its own dynamic implementation *before* calling `AddCommonSecurityHeaders`, which is exactly what ADC's UI host does: its `AddMmcaBlazorWebHost` call (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:26`) registers the shared [BlazorCspPolicyProvider](group-15-common-ui-framework.md#blazorcsppolicyprovider) through `AddCommonBlazorCsp()` immediately before `AddCommonSecurityHeaders` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI.Web/Hosting/BlazorWebHostExtensions.cs:156-157`). That host keeps HSTS on for its own origin, because an HSTS pin is per-origin and the Gateway's header says nothing about the UI's sibling hostname (`Program.cs:195-200`). [CspPolicy](#csppolicy) (Level 0, `SecurityHeaders.cs:78`) is the resolved `(Value, Enforce)` record that decides between `Content-Security-Policy` and `Content-Security-Policy-Report-Only`, keeping a policy and its enforcement mode inseparable. [SecurityHeadersMiddleware](#securityheadersmiddleware) (Level 2, `:147`) stamps `X-Content-Type-Options`, `X-Frame-Options`, `Referrer-Policy`, and `Permissions-Policy` on every response (`:190-195`), switching `Referrer-Policy` to `no-referrer` on a credential path (`:192-194`). A credential path is matched by whole segments, case-insensitively, so `/reset-password` covers `/reset-password/anything` but never `/reset-passwords` (`:178-182`), and its response also leaves with `Cache-Control: no-store, no-cache, must-revalidate, max-age=0` plus `Pragma: no-cache` (`:156`, `:246-250`). Those are applied once up front and again from a response-start callback (`:197-212`): the Razor Components endpoint writes its own weaker `Cache-Control` while it renders, and a callback registered before the rest of the pipeline runs after every downstream one, so the strict value settles last. HSTS is added only outside Development (decided once in the constructor at `:175`, applied at `:214-217`), and the middleware emits whichever CSP header the provider's `Enforce` flag selects (`:233-240`). Between those two steps sits the nonce path: a policy carrying the literal `{nonce}` token gets a fresh 16-byte value per request (`:150,153,226-231`), stashed raw in `HttpContext.Items` *before* the rest of the pipeline runs so the page render can read it, and substituted into the header as the quoted `'nonce-<value>'` source-list form. [CspNonce](#cspnonce) (Level 0, `:124`) is the tiny public surface for that: the `ItemKey` constant (`:127`) and a `Get(context)` helper (`:134`) a layout calls to stamp the value onto its own script tags. It is the supported path off `'unsafe-inline'`, and a policy with no placeholder generates nothing (`:110-122`). [SecurityHeadersExtensions](#securityheadersextensions) (Level 3, `:258`) supplies the two registration halves: `AddCommonSecurityHeaders` (`:268`), which validates the options with `ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` so a malformed credential prefix fails the host once rather than every request (`:274-277`), binds the config section when one is supplied (`:278-281`), and registers the default provider through `TryAddSingleton` so a pre-registered custom provider wins (`:288`), and `UseCommonSecurityHeaders` (`:296`). The stated point is to centralize what each client-facing host previously hand-rolled, eliminating drift; this is **[ADR-023](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/023-security-response-headers.html)**.

One sibling completes this boundary. [GatewayCorsExtensions](#gatewaycorsextensions) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/GatewayCorsExtensions.cs:16`) exposes `AddCommonGatewayCors` (`:24`), which registers the reverse proxy's *default* CORS policy: allow-any origin in Development (`:34-41`) but in every other environment restricting **origins** to `Cors:AllowedOrigins` while allowing any header and method plus credentials (`:44-52`), because the CORS spec forbids combining allow-any-origin with credentials. It is the gateway half of the two-tier posture in [ADR-082](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/082-two-tier-cors-posture.html), deliberately looser than `MMCA.Common.API`'s per-service allow-listed `AddCommonCors`, because a proxy must pass arbitrary client headers through; ADC's Gateway wires it alongside the header middleware (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:135,140,196`). The multi-replica half of the same story, a DataProtection key ring every replica shares so an auth cookie or antiforgery token minted by one can be decrypted by another ([ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html)), is a cloud-adapter call rather than part of this package ([AzureDataProtectionExtensions](group-28-common-cloud-provider-adapters.md#azuredataprotectionextensions)), guarded at startup by the tripwire above. Relevant tags across this boundary: **`[Rubric §11, Security]`** and **`[Rubric §26, Front-End Security]`** (defense-in-depth headers and CSP with a nonce path), and **`[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`** (one shared middleware and one shared CORS policy instead of N hand-rolled copies).

## The gateway edge kit: correlation, rate limiting, downstream readiness

A YARP gateway is the one process every client request passes through, and it is also the one host that has *no* application container: no DbContext, no module loader, no `ICorrelationContext`. That is why the three edge behaviors live in their own namespace, `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway`, with no dependency beyond ASP.NET Core itself. The decision, including the three responsibilities the edge explicitly declines, is **[ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html)**.

[GatewayCorrelationMiddleware](#gatewaycorrelationmiddleware) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayCorrelationMiddleware.cs:30`) is the twin of `MMCA.Common.API`'s [CorrelationIdMiddleware](group-12-api-hosting-mapping.md#correlationidmiddleware) and exists precisely because that one cannot run here: it writes onto a scoped [ICorrelationContext](group-12-api-hosting-mapping.md#icorrelationcontext) that only a host with the Common application services registered owns (`:11-16`). This one takes `RequestDelegate` and nothing else (`:29`), which is what makes it safe to drop into a bare proxy. `InvokeAsync` (`:51`) reads `X-Correlation-ID` (the header name is a public constant deliberately duplicating the API package's literal, `:36`, since the two packages share no reference) and applies the service's own rule to a caller-supplied value, mirrored in a private `Sanitize` (`:87-91`): cut to `MaxLength`, 64 characters, the stored width (`:43`), and discarded when any character falls outside printable ASCII, because Kestrel refuses to echo a non-ASCII or control character (`:21-23`). When nothing usable arrived it mints one from `Activity.Current?.TraceId` with `TraceIdentifier` as the fallback (`:62-64`), and it writes the result onto the **request** headers whenever it differs from what the caller sent (`:66-69`). Writing the request side is the whole point: the proxied request carries the ID downstream, so the service-side middleware adopts it instead of minting a second one, and because the echo runs after the forwarder has copied the service's headers, the edge must forward and echo exactly the value the service keeps (`:57-59`). The response echo is registered through `HttpResponse.OnStarting` (`:72-76`) so it survives a response whose headers the forwarder writes. [GatewayCorrelationExtensions](#gatewaycorrelationextensions) (Level 1, same file `:99`) is the one-line `UseGatewayCorrelation()` pipeline call (`:108`).

The edge limiter is two types and a mode. [GatewayRateLimitingSettings](#gatewayratelimitingsettings) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayRateLimitingSettings.cs:48`) binds the optional `GatewayRateLimiting` section (`:51`): `PermitLimit` 120 (`:60`), `WindowSeconds` 60 (`:64`), `GlobalConcurrencyLimit` 200 (`:74`), extra `BypassPathPrefixes` (`:85`, guarded by the same [LeadingSlashPathPrefixesAttribute](#leadingslashpathprefixesattribute) at `:84`, so a bad entry fails registration instead of every request), two header-and-secret pairs, the synthetic-traffic pair (`:94`, `:118`) and the trusted-internal-caller pair (`:128`, `:156`), and the `TrustedCallerMode` that decides what the second pair buys (`:166`). Three properties of the design are load-bearing and stated in its remarks: the limiters count **in this replica's memory**, so the effective allowance multiplies by replica count, a trade taken because an edge limiter must answer in microseconds and a shared counter would put a network round trip in front of every request; partitioning is by `Connection.RemoteIpAddress`, so forwarded headers must be applied *before* the limiter or every request is attributed to the ingress; and a scheduled capacity proof drives its whole load from one runner IP, which the per-IP window cannot tell from a flood, so a run presenting the configured synthetic-traffic value takes the same no-limiter partition a bypassed path takes. The trusted-caller pair exists for a server-rendered UI host, which makes every back-end call from one address on behalf of every signed-in visitor; both secrets are off unless configured, and the trusted one identifies a deployed component, never a browser. [TrustedCallerMode](#trustedcallermode) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/TrustedCallerMode.cs:8`) decides how such a caller is counted. The default, `PerVisitor` (`:17`), counts a trusted-caller request that carries the end visitor's address in `X-Forwarded-For` against that visitor's own per-IP window and every named route policy keyed on the visitor, exactly as the visitor's direct request would be, and gives only a request with no visitor address, a background call, the no-limiter partition (`:10-16`); `Exempt` (`:23`) gives every trusted-caller request the no-limiter partition whatever address it carries (`:19-22`). [GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions) (Level 2, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:50`) composes them: `IsBypassed` (`:121`) matches whole path segments case-insensitively against the always-exempt `/health`, `/alive`, `/.well-known` (`:109`) plus the host's configured prefixes, `IsSyntheticTraffic` (`:148`) and `IsTrustedInternalCaller` (`:184`) both go through one `PresentsSecret` (`:199`) that compares the header with `CryptographicOperations.FixedTimeEquals` (`:215`), and `IsExemptFromLimiters` (`:227`) is the union of the three. `ClientIpPartition` (`:245`) decides a trusted caller first. Under `PerVisitor` it reads the visitor from the last `X-Forwarded-For` entry (`TrustedCallerVisitorIp`, `:302-319`), stamps that address into `HttpContext.Items` under `TrustedCallerVisitorIpItemKey` (`:78`) for the named per-route policies that run later and cannot see these settings, and counts the request in the visitor's window (`:269-272`); with no visitor, under `Exempt`, or on a bypassed path or synthetic run it stamps `TrustedInternalCallerItemKey` (`:69`) instead and returns the no-limiter partition (`:259-267`). Any other exempt request also gets the no-limiter partition (`:275-278`), so does an unresolvable IP (fail open rather than collapsing every unattributable request into one bucket, `:280-286`), and everything else gets a fixed window per IP with no queue (`:288`, `:322-337`). That window is a wrapper, `RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter`, because the framework limiter's `Retry-After` is always the whole window and the wrapper reports the time actually left in it (`:326-328`). `ConcurrencyPartition` (`:348`) is one process-wide bucket that also rejects immediately rather than queueing (`:355-362`), because the failure it guards against is a slow downstream backing requests up until the edge runs out of threads. `AddGatewayRateLimiting` has two overloads: the configuration one binds with `ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` (`:374-387`), and the settings one runs `Validator.ValidateObject` at registration (`:396`, `:404`) because the limiter closes over an eagerly bound copy rather than resolving `IOptions` per request, which honors [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html) on both paths at the cost of no hot reload. The two limiters are chained (`:417`), so a request must satisfy both, and rejection is 429 (`:408`). Every rejection also passes through `WithRetryAfterHeader` (`:92`, installed at `:412`), which writes a `Retry-After` header in whole seconds through the shared [RetryAfterHeader](#retryafterheader) whenever the rejecting lease carries a retry-after hint (the fixed window does, a concurrency rejection does not) and then runs whatever `OnRejected` callback was configured before it, because that callback is one slot shared with the route policies below (`:410-412`). `UseGatewayRateLimiting()` is the pipeline half. Note the deliberate inversion of [ADR-019](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/019-rate-limiting.html): the service-side global limiter exempts anonymous callers, and the edge does not, because the output cache that justified that exemption sits behind the proxy. **`[Rubric §11, Security]`** and **`[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`**.

Readiness is the third piece, and it is the one that learned the most. [GatewayHealthCheckExtensions](#gatewayhealthcheckextensions) (Level 5, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:71`) exposes `AddGatewayDownstreamHealthChecks` in two overloads (`:131`, `:149`) that funnel into one private `Register` (`:162`), which for each Aspire service name registers a named `HttpClient` whose base address is the service-discovery form `http://{name}` (`:190-196`, resolved by the `AddServiceDiscovery` that `AddServiceDefaults` already wired, so nothing hard-codes a host or port) and a `downstream-{name}` health check over it (`:237-242`). Two choices carry the design (`:114-130`): the checks are tagged `Ready`, never `Live`, because restarting the gateway fixes nothing about a downstream outage; and their failure status is `Unhealthy` rather than `Degraded`, because `/health/ready` treats Degraded as passing, so a Degraded check would report a problem while still taking traffic the gateway cannot serve. The probe budget is 2 seconds (`:85`), short because it runs per downstream on every readiness poll, and two registration details protect it. The probe client strips every `ResilienceHandler` that `AddServiceDefaults()` put on it (`:204-221`), because the standard pipeline's one retry lands inside the two-second budget and turns a healthy downstream into a timed-out one, and a refused HTTP/2 attempt must reach the check at once so it can fall back. And the check is one keyed singleton per downstream (`:223-235`), because the health-check service runs the registration factory on every poll and a fresh instance each time would drop the latched HTTP version described below. [DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/DownstreamServiceHealthCheck.cs:41`) is the hand-rolled check itself, a GET and a status comparison (`:143-175`), with a narrow catch list plus a cancellation guard so host shutdown is never reported as a dependency fault (`:75-87`). It probes `/alive`, not `/health/ready`, taking the path from the shared `HealthEndpointPaths.Alive` constant rather than a literal of its own (`:46`), and the reason is in its remarks (`:17-21`): the gateway needs to know whether the service exists and is reachable, and probing readiness would make every rolling downstream deployment register as a gateway failure. The protocol question is settled per downstream rather than configured: [DownstreamProbeVersion](#downstreamprobeversion) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:11`) offers `Auto`, `Http2` and `Http11` (`:19,26,33`), and [GatewayDownstreamHealthCheckOptions](#gatewaydownstreamhealthcheckoptions) (Level 1, same file `:41`) defaults to `Auto` (`:60`) because neither fixed answer is right for every head: an h2c-only endpoint refuses HTTP/1.1, and a mixed `Http1AndHttp2` cleartext endpoint without ALPN answers `HTTP_1_1_REQUIRED` to HTTP/2 forever (`:43-58`). Under `Auto` the check asks for HTTP/2 first and, on a *protocol* refusal only (`IsProtocolRefusal` at `DownstreamServiceHealthCheck.cs:184` matching `VersionNegotiationError` or `HttpProtocolError`), retries once as HTTP/1.1 inside the same poll (`:98-131`), then latches the version that answered with `Interlocked.CompareExchange` (`:192`) so the fallback is a one-time cost per downstream rather than a per-poll one. Nothing latches on a connectivity fault, because a transient outage says nothing about which protocol the endpoint speaks. [GatewayDownstreamRegistry](#gatewaydownstreamregistry) (Level 0, `GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:255`) is the small ledger that makes the registration idempotent, the same shape as the H2c one above: `GetOrAdd` (`:287`) attaches one instance to the collection and `TryClaim` (`:270`) skips a name an earlier call already registered.

## The YARP building blocks: cluster profiles, ejection, trace headers, route policies

The route table itself is **configuration**, not code ([ADR-089](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/089-gateway-topology-owned-by-configuration.html)), and `MMCA.Common.Gateway` deliberately does not load it: `LoadFromConfig` (or any other `IProxyConfigProvider`) stays the host's call, and this package only adds behavior on top of whatever source the host chose (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:24-29`). [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings) (Level 2, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettings.cs:12`) is its whole configuration surface, bound from the `MmcaGateway` section (`:15`): cluster request defaults and per-cluster overrides (`:22`, `:29`), destination health-check defaults (`:33`), trace headers (`:36`), named route policies (`:42`) and the opt-in connect retry (`:49`), duplicating nothing from the YARP `ReverseProxy` section. [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions) (Level 4, `GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:35`) is the single entry point, `AddMmcaGateway`, in a configuration overload that binds with `ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` (`:56-66`) and a settings overload for a host that composes in code (`:82`, supplying an explicit `IOptions<>` instance at `:90`). Both funnel into one private `Wire` (`:100`) that validates the settings and registers the named policies (`:105-106`), installs the connect retry when it is enabled (`:108-111`), post-configures Kestrel's header encoding (`:113-114`), and attaches two config filters plus two transform providers (`:116-120`). The validation step exists because `ValidateDataAnnotations` reads only the attributes on the properties of `GatewaySettings` itself and does not recurse into the objects it holds, so a blank trace-header name would pass startup and then throw from every proxied request (`GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:102-104`). [GatewaySettingsValidator](#gatewaysettingsvalidator) (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettingsValidator.cs:19`), an internal `IValidateOptions<GatewaySettings>`, closes that gap over four nested objects, `TraceHeaders`, both halves of `HealthCheckDefaults`, and `Retry` (`NestedObjects` at `:50-55`), and it is used the two ways the route policies are checked. `ThrowIfInvalid` (`:23`) runs over each and fails registration for both overloads (called from `Wire` at `GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:105`, the [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html) posture), and the configuration overload also registers the validator through `TryAddEnumerable` (`GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:70`) so the value the options pipeline binds is checked again on start, where `Validate` (`GatewaySettingsValidator.cs:32`) collects every failure rather than stopping at the first.

Four building blocks hang off that. First, [GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter](#gatewayclusterprofileconfigfilter) (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Configuration/GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter.cs:25`) resolves each cluster's forwarder request profile from three sources, **per property** rather than per block (`Resolve` at `:59`, the four most-specific-wins helpers at `:82-122`): the cluster's own `HttpRequest`, then [GatewayClusterRequestProfile](#gatewayclusterrequestprofile) (Level 0, `GatewaySettings.cs:81`) under `ClusterRequestOverrides[clusterId]`, then `ClusterRequestDefaults`. That ordering is the point: a gateway fronting services that all speak h2c declares the shared activity timeout once instead of repeating an identical block per cluster, while the one cluster that genuinely differs still says so locally. The profile expresses the version pair as text (`:58,64`) so a mistyped value fails at startup with a message naming the cluster (`ParseVersion` at `:76`, `ParseVersionPolicy` at `:96`) instead of binding to a silent default, and `IsSameAs` (`:131`) returns an unchanged cluster by reference so YARP's config-change detection sees no churn on reload. Second, [GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter](#gatewayhealthcheckdefaultsconfigfilter) (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Configuration/GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter.cs:17`) fills in destination health checks for clusters that declare none (`:25-47`), additively: an operator's explicit `Passive` or `Active` block is kept verbatim (`:29-33`). [GatewayHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewayhealthcheckdefaults) (Level 1, `GatewaySettings.cs:148`) splits into [GatewayPassiveHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewaypassivehealthcheckdefaults) (Level 0, `:132`), on by default with the built-in `TransportFailureRate` policy and a 60 second reactivation period (`:135-142`) because passive checks watch the responses the gateway is already forwarding and cost no extra traffic, and [GatewayActiveHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewayactivehealthcheckdefaults) (Level 0, `:150`), off by default with `ConsecutiveFailures`, a 10 second interval, a 5 second timeout and a `/alive` path (`:153-171`) chosen for the same reason the gateway's own probes use it: readiness on a downstream flips during its own rolling deployment, and ejecting a destination for that is the gateway reacting to a healthy deployment as if it were an outage. Third, [GatewayTraceHeaderTransformProvider](#gatewaytraceheadertransformprovider) (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Transforms/GatewayTraceHeaderTransformProvider.cs:18`) stamps the matched route id and target cluster id onto every proxied request from [GatewayTraceHeaderSettings](#gatewaytraceheadersettings) (Level 0, `GatewaySettings.cs:207`, defaults `X-MMCA-Route` and `X-MMCA-Cluster` at `:181,184`), capturing the ids once at build time (`:51-54`) and **removing** any inbound value before writing its own (`:60-61`), because a header a service trusts must be one only the gateway can set. Fourth, [GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions](#gatewayroutepolicyextensions) (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/RateLimiting/GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:29`) registers one named fixed-window policy per entry in `RateLimiterPolicies` (`AddGatewayRoutePolicies` at `:50`), the policies a YARP route references through its own `RateLimiterPolicy` property. Each [GatewayRoutePolicySettings](#gatewayroutepolicysettings) (Level 1, `GatewaySettings.cs:241`) states a [GatewayRoutePolicyPartition](#gatewayroutepolicypartition) (Level 0, `:199`, `ClientIp` or `Global`), a permit limit defaulting to 30, a 60 second window and a queue limit of zero (`:215-230`), and its `PartitionKey` returns null for an unresolvable IP so `Partition` (`GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:113`) hands back a no-limiter partition rather than one shared bucket. The edge limiter's trusted-caller decision carries over through the two `HttpContext.Items` keys it stamps first: a request it counted against a forwarded visitor's window is keyed on that visitor's address here too (`TrustedCallerVisitorIpItemKey`, `:49`, read at `:122`), and a request it exempted takes a no-limiter partition here as well (`TrustedInternalCallerItemKey`, `:40`, checked at `:127-129`), so a server-rendered UI's sign-ins, which all leave from one address, are never pooled into one per-IP bucket. Values are validated at registration, not at the first throttled request (`:60-63`), again [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html). These policies are **additive** to any global limiter the host installs (`:16-21`): ASP.NET Core evaluates the global limiter and the route's named policy independently, so a request must satisfy both, and the two packages stay independent of each other. Both registrations write to the one shared `OnRejected` slot, so the route policies install the same `Retry-After` writer the edge limiter does and chain whatever callback came before (`GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:80-82`). That writer is [GatewayRetryAfter](#gatewayretryafter) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/RateLimiting/GatewayRetryAfter.cs:13`), an internal copy of `RetryAfterHeader.Format` (`:21-25`) plus the `WithRetryAfterHeader` wrapper (`:36-46`), which sets the header only when the rejecting lease carries a `MetadataName.RetryAfter` hint and then runs the previous callback (`:40-45`). It is a copy rather than a reference because this package deliberately references no MMCA package, and a test pins it to the Shared original so the two cannot drift (`:7-12`).

The fifth type in this package is not about YARP configuration at all. [ForwardedHeadersExtensions](#forwardedheadersextensions) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/ForwardedHeadersExtensions.cs:23`) supplies `UseCommonForwardedHeaders()` (`:36`) and the shared `CreateForwardedHeadersOptions()` (`:55`) that applies `X-Forwarded-For`, `-Proto` and `-Host` with the known-proxy and known-network allow-lists **cleared** (`:59-63`). Clearing them is load-bearing: cloud reverse proxies front the app from internal IPs that are in neither default allow-list, so leaving the defaults in place makes the middleware ignore every forwarded header and report the ingress IP as the client. It lives here because a service host gets this step from `UseCommonMiddlewarePipeline` in `MMCA.Common.API`, and a gateway takes none of that package (`:9-17`); without it a gateway hand-rolls the same five lines, and getting them wrong is invisible until production, where an unforwarded gateway collapses the per-client-IP rate-limit partition into one shared window for every real user.

Three more pieces harden the forwarding path itself. [GatewayHeaderEncoding](#gatewayheaderencoding) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayHeaderEncoding.cs:14`) lets the gateway's Kestrel read `X-Correlation-ID` (`:20`) as Latin-1 instead of ASCII. Kestrel answers a non-ASCII header byte with an empty 400 and `Connection: close` before any middleware runs, so without it [GatewayCorrelationMiddleware](#gatewaycorrelationmiddleware) would never get to replace the unusable value with a generated id (`:6-13`). `Apply` (`:27`) chains the rule in front of whatever selector the host set, which keeps deciding every other header (`:31-35`), and `Wire` post-configures it so it wraps the host's own `ConfigureKestrel` (`GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:113-114`). [GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider](#gatewaybypassheadertransformprovider) (Level 0, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Transforms/GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider.cs:20`) removes the edge limiter's trusted-caller and synthetic-traffic headers from every proxied request (`:50-64`), so the secrets they carry end at the gateway and no downstream service, sidecar or header log receives them, while the limiter, which reads both before the proxy runs, is unaffected (`:7-11`). Because this package takes no reference to the Aspire one, it reads the header names from the `GatewayRateLimiting` section by name and falls back to the limiter's defaults, `X-Internal-Caller-Key` and `X-Synthetic-Traffic-Key` (`:23-29`, `:66-81`). The third is an opt-in connect-failure retry. [GatewayRetrySettings](#gatewayretrysettings) (Level 0, `GatewaySettings.cs:58`), bound from `MmcaGateway:Retry` (`:49`, `:52-57`), is off by default (`Enabled`, `:64`), allows one to three extra attempts defaulting to one (`:67-68`), and waits at most five seconds before each, defaulting to 200 milliseconds (`:71-72`). When it is enabled, `Wire` replaces YARP's forwarder client factory with [GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory](#gatewayretryforwarderhttpclientfactory) (Level 3, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory.cs:12`, registered at `GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:153-154`), and fails registration rather than silently replacing a factory the host registered itself (`:130-146`). Its `WrapHandler` (`GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory.cs:64-68`) installs [GatewayConnectRetryHandler](#gatewayconnectretryhandler) (Level 1, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayConnectRetryHandler.cs:20`) as the outermost handler of every cluster client, so a retried attempt goes through the whole inner pipeline again (`GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory.cs:7-11`). The handler retries only what never reached the destination: a body-less `GET` or `HEAD` (`GatewayConnectRetryHandler.cs:36-38`) whose attempt failed with a name-resolution, connection or TLS error (`:41-44`), logging a Warning per retry (`:61-67`) and waiting the configured delay on an injected `TimeProvider` (`:70-73`). Any response, any other exception and a cancellation pass through untouched, and a spent budget rethrows the last exception, so YARP still answers 502 and the passive health policy counts one failure (`:5-15`).

ADC's Gateway wires all of this in an order the comments call out as contractual (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:67-151` for registration, `:160-234` for the pipeline): `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration()` first (`:68`), then `AddServiceDefaults()` (`:70`) and `AddAzureMonitorExporter()` (`:75`), the edge limiter (`:76`), one call registering all four downstream checks on the `Auto` default (`:90`), shared security headers (`:120`) and CORS (`:125`), then `AddReverseProxy().LoadFromConfig(...).AddMmcaGateway(...).AddServiceDiscoveryDestinationResolver()` (`:148-151`). The pipeline is forwarded headers first so the limiter sees the real client IP (`:160`), then correlation (`:165`) so even a 429 from the limiter carries an ID, then security headers (`:170`), `MapDefaultEndpoints` (`:172`), CORS (`:173`), the limiter after CORS so a rejected preflight is still a CORS answer (`:180`), static files and the `/privacy` alias (`:185-193`), and finally `MapReverseProxy()` (`:234`) so a throttled request never reaches a backend. Its `GatewayRateLimiting` section adds `/hubs` to the bypass list (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/appsettings.json:25`) because a SignalR connection is long-lived and its negotiate and reconnect traffic must not be throttled, and it names the trusted-caller header while the secret itself arrives from Key Vault (`:26-32`); the UI head presents that secret on its server-side calls through `AddTrustedCallerHeader`, which stamps it only on requests to the configured gateway origin (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/UI/MMCA.ADC.UI.Web/Program.cs:139-144`). Its `MmcaGateway` section declares the shared 100 second forwarder activity timeout, the one-hour override for the `notification-hub` cluster, active destination probing explicitly left off (`:57-61`), and the `auth-tight` per-route policy at 30 permits per 60 second window per client IP (`:62-68`), all within `appsettings.json:43-70`. **`[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`**, **`[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`**, and **`[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`**.

## How it all fits at runtime

Putting the pieces in sequence: the AppHost declares the graph, picks a broker once, and injects per-service env vars (`WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` for each service's database, cache, broker and mail waits, `WithJwksDiscovery`, `WithIssuer`, `WithUiLinks`, `WithServiceTokenSigningKey`, the E2E switches, and the gRPC `WithReference`s), attaching an `/alive` health check to every service resource in the shape its `ServiceEndpointProfile` names (`WithH2cHealthCheck` for the three h2c services, the stock probe for Notification) so each `WaitFor` edge gates on a real answer. Each service boots, sets its Kestrel protocol profile and optional Http1 probe listener with `ConfigureEndpointsWithHealthProbe`, calls `AddCommonSerilog`, then `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration()` from the Azure adapter so everything that binds settings afterwards (service defaults included) reads vault-backed secrets, then `AddServiceDefaults()` to opt into telemetry, health, resilience, warm-up and the adapter tripwire, then `AddAzureMonitorExporter()` behind the trace filter processors, `AddRedisCaching()` (and `AddRedisOutputCaching()` where the output cache must cross replicas), `AddInfrastructureHealthChecks(requireDatabase: true)` for its dependency probes, `AddAzureDataProtection()` where cookies must survive scale-out, and `AddCommonSecurityHeaders` plus `UseCommonSecurityHeaders` at the edge. The Gateway boots on the same vault source and `AddServiceDefaults()` and adds the edge kit (`AddGatewayRateLimiting`, `AddGatewayDownstreamHealthChecks`, `AddCommonGatewayCors`, `UseCommonForwardedHeaders`, `UseGatewayCorrelation`, `UseGatewayRateLimiting`) plus `AddMmcaGateway` over a route table it loads from configuration. Aspire withholds traffic via `WaitFor` and the `/health/ready` predicate until each replica is past its warm-up gate and its non-optional dependencies are healthy, and the gateway's own readiness additionally reflects whether it can reach the four services, on whichever HTTP version each of them latched. The warm-up tasks pre-pay the cold paths, bounded by the 120 second per-task ceiling. Once live, outbound calls ride the shared `HttpResilienceDefaults` pipeline over the idle-cost-tuned socket handler, east-west gRPC rides the `Grpc`-prefixed values in the same `HttpResilienceDefaults` file, integration events flow through the broker selected by the env vars behind the `BrokerResilienceDefaults` circuit breaker, YARP ejects a failing destination on the package's passive health-check defaults, and telemetry streams to the OTLP endpoint or Azure Monitor minus the suppressed outbox-poll and health-probe spans, any dropped metric family, and any head-sampled trace fraction. The same env-var-and-abstraction contract is what lets a module run in-process or as an extracted service without code changes ([ADR-007](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/007-grpc-extraction.html) and [ADR-008](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/008-service-extraction-topology.html)): the AppHost decides topology by what it wires, and the service code stays transport-agnostic. The per-type sections that follow document each of these classes in ascending Level order; the module-registration and message-bus types they reference are in the **module system** (group 14) and **events and outbox** (group 04) chapters.

### GatewayCorsExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/GatewayCorsExtensions.cs:16` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: Registers the shared **default** CORS policy for the reverse-proxy Gateway via a single `AddCommonGatewayCors` extension method.
- **Depends on**: `Microsoft.Extensions` CORS / Configuration / Hosting (ASP.NET Core, BCL). No first-party types.
- **Concept introduced**: `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §26, Front-End Security]`, `[Rubric §9, API & Contract Design]`. §11 and §26 assess a cross-origin policy that stays safe while still allowing credentials. The doc comment (`GatewayCorsExtensions.cs:7-15`) draws the distinction from a *service* host's CORS: a reverse-proxy gateway must pass arbitrary client headers through to the services it fronts, so, unlike `MMCA.Common.API.AddCommonCors`'s allow-listed headers and methods, the production gateway policy allows **any header and method** while restricting **origins** to `Cors:AllowedOrigins`. That origin restriction is load-bearing: the CORS specification forbids combining `AllowAnyOrigin()` with `AllowCredentials()`, so to let cookies and `Authorization` headers flow, the policy must name explicit origins. **[ADR-082](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/082-two-tier-cors-posture.html)** is the record for exactly this two-tier posture (allow-listed service policies plus an any-header gateway policy). This is the browser-facing edge of the Gateway topology ([ADR-008](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/008-service-extraction-topology.html)).
- **Walkthrough**: `AddCommonGatewayCors(configuration, environment)` sits in an `extension(IServiceCollection services)` block (`GatewayCorsExtensions.cs:18`, method at `:24`). It null-guards the receiver and both arguments (`:28-30`), then registers a **default** policy inside `services.AddCors` (`:32`). In Development it allows any origin, header, and method (`:37-40`), with a scoped `#pragma warning disable S5122` (`:36`, restored at `:41`) whose comment records that allow-any-origin is confined to Development. Otherwise it reads the `Cors:AllowedOrigins` string array, defaulting to `[]` (`:45-47`), and builds `WithOrigins(origins).AllowAnyHeader().AllowAnyMethod().AllowCredentials()` (`:48-52`). Because it is registered as the **default** policy, hosts pair it with a bare `app.UseCors()` (no named policy).
- **Why it's built this way**: Credentialed cross-origin traffic requires enumerated origins, so the non-Development branch reads them from configuration rather than allowing everything; the any-header and any-method latitude reflects the gateway's pass-through role. One shared method keeps the ADC and Store gateways from drifting apart.
- **Where it's used**: The YARP Gateway hosts: `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:140` and `MMCA.Store/Source/Hosts/MMCA.Store.Gateway/Program.cs:77`, each followed by a bare `app.UseCors()` (ADC `Program.cs:137`, Store `Program.cs:162`). Note the pipeline order in both hosts: `UseCors()` runs **before** [GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions)' `UseGatewayRateLimiting()` (ADC `:137`, `:144`; Store `:162`, `:170`), and both hosts carry a comment saying why (ADC `:139`, Store `:164`): a rejected preflight is still a CORS answer, and a throttled cross-origin caller still receives the headers it needs in order to read the 429.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: Outside Development, an unset or empty `Cors:AllowedOrigins` yields an empty origin list, which blocks all cross-origin credentialed calls. That is fail-closed (a misconfiguration denies rather than permits), but the browser client cannot reach the gateway until origins are configured.

### HealthCheckTags

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/HealthCheckTags.cs:6` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: The three tag-name constants (`live`, `ready`, `optional`) that the standard health endpoints mapped by `MapDefaultEndpoints()` filter on.
- **Depends on**: Nothing. Three `const string` fields. Consumed by the [Extensions](#extensions) registration and endpoint-mapping code, by [RedisPingHealthCheck](#redispinghealthcheck)'s registration, and by [GatewayHealthCheckExtensions](#gatewayhealthcheckextensions).
- **Concept introduced, health-check tags as a routing vocabulary.** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §17, DevOps]`. ASP.NET Core health checks each carry a set of string tags, and an endpoint maps a `Predicate` over those tags. That means the tag vocabulary *is* the operational contract between the app and the platform (Azure Container Apps or Kubernetes probes). Getting it wrong is not cosmetic: a liveness probe that fails restarts the container, and a readiness probe that fails removes the replica from traffic. Naming the three tags once, in a shared constants class, is what stops a consumer from tagging a check `"Ready"` or `"live "` and silently landing it on the wrong endpoint.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `Live = "live"` (`HealthCheckTags.cs:12`): liveness only. The check runs on `/alive` and is **excluded** from readiness. Its doc comment (`:8-11`) reserves it for self checks, "so an external dependency outage never restarts the container".
  - `Ready = "ready"` (`HealthCheckTags.cs:18`): the readiness gate. The check runs on `/health/ready` and holds traffic back until it passes. This is the tag the warm-up gate uses, and the tag the gateway's downstream checks use.
  - `Optional = "optional"` (`HealthCheckTags.cs:32`): a dependency the application degrades gracefully without. It is reported on `/health` but **excluded** from `/health/ready`. The long doc comment (`:20-31`) is the teaching part and is worth reading in full: a distributed cache sitting behind an in-memory fallback, or a broker behind a retrying outbox, can fail without the app losing the ability to serve. Gating readiness on such a dependency converts a partial degradation into a **total** outage, because every replica goes unready simultaneously and the platform stops routing traffic the app could still handle. A check is left untagged only when the app genuinely cannot serve correct responses without it, its own database being the standard example.
- **Why it's built this way**: Constants rather than an enum, because the health-checks API takes `string` tags; a `const` also lets the values appear in collection expressions such as `tags: [HealthCheckTags.Optional]` with no conversion. The three-way split (live / ready / optional / untagged) encodes a deliberate blast-radius policy: liveness restarts, readiness withholds traffic, optional only reports. That policy is recorded in **[ADR-025](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/025-startup-warmup-readiness.html)**, whose revision added the `optional` exclusion to `/health/ready` for exactly the total-outage reason above, and it is what [ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html) applies to a dependency rather than to a startup task.
- **Where it's used**: The service-defaults [Extensions](#extensions): the warm-up check is registered with `[HealthCheckTags.Ready]` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:138`), the `"self"` check with `[HealthCheckTags.Live]` (`:238`), and the conditional Redis and RabbitMQ checks with `[HealthCheckTags.Optional]` (`:289`, `:301`). The endpoint predicates read them back: `/alive` requires `Live` (`:376-379`) and `/health/ready` excludes both `Live` and `Optional` (`:392-395`). The relational database checks are deliberately left untagged (`:462`, `:467`), which is what makes them readiness-fatal. [GatewayHealthCheckExtensions](#gatewayhealthcheckextensions) tags every downstream probe `Ready`. The tagging is asserted by [InfrastructureHealthChecksTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) and, for the Redis path specifically, by [RedisReadinessSafetyTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup).

### HealthEndpointPaths

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/HealthEndpointPaths.cs:8` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: The three health-probe route constants (`/health`, `/alive`, `/health/ready`) plus one predicate, `IsProbePath(string?)`, that answers whether a request path addresses any of them. Where [HealthCheckTags](#healthchecktags) names the tag vocabulary, this names the *routes*.
- **Depends on**: Nothing first-party beyond a doc-comment reference to [HealthCheckTags](#healthchecktags) (`HealthEndpointPaths.cs:13`). Three `const string` fields and a static method over `string.Equals` / `string.StartsWith`.
- **Concept introduced, one owner for the probe route vocabulary.** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`, `[Rubric §31, Cost / FinOps]`. The probe routes are read in two very different places: the code that *maps* them ([Extensions](#extensions)`.MapDefaultEndpoints()`) and the code that *recognises* them after the fact, namely the trace filters that keep probe traffic out of telemetry export ([ProbeTelemetryFilter](#probetelemetryfilter) and [ProbeTelemetryFilterProcessor](#probetelemetryfilterprocessor)) and the gateway's own downstream probe ([DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck)). A literal `"/alive"` retyped on the recognising side is the classic silent break: the endpoint keeps working, the filter quietly stops matching, and the only symptom is a telemetry bill. Naming the routes once makes the mapping side and the matching side provably the same strings. The cost half of that is recorded in **[ADR-041](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/041-observability-and-telemetry.html)**.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `Health = "/health"` (`HealthEndpointPaths.cs:11`), `Alive = "/alive"` (`:14`), `Ready = "/health/ready"` (`:17`): the full report, the liveness probe, and the readiness probe respectively, each with a one-line doc comment naming which checks run there (`:10`, `:13`, `:16`).
  - `HealthPrefix` (`:19`) is private and composed from `Health + "/"`, so it cannot drift from the constant it extends.
  - `IsProbePath(path)` (`:29`) returns `false` for null or empty, then matches `Alive` or `Health` exactly and anything under `HealthPrefix` (`:30-33`). The prefix arm is what makes `/health/ready` match without being listed, and it also covers any sub-route a host maps later. Every comparison is `OrdinalIgnoreCase`, which the doc comment (`:21-28`) ties to ASP.NET Core routing being case-insensitive.
- **Why it's built this way**: Constants rather than an options type, because these routes are a platform contract (the Container Apps probe configuration and the availability web test point at them) and are not per-host configurable. `IsProbePath` lives beside them rather than inside either filter because both filters and the gateway probe need the same answer; putting the predicate next to the strings keeps the "what counts as a probe" definition in one file. The prefix rule is deliberately broader than the three constants, so adding a `/health/live` style route later needs no change on the recognising side.
- **Where it's used**: [Extensions](#extensions)`.MapDefaultEndpoints()` maps all three (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.Health.cs:154`, `:138`, `:154`). [ProbeTelemetryFilter](#probetelemetryfilter) calls `IsProbePath` for the inbound request path (`Telemetry/ProbeTelemetryFilter.cs:42`) and for an outgoing request URI (`:63`). [ProbeTelemetryFilterProcessor](#probetelemetryfilterprocessor) calls it three ways on a finished activity, the `url.path` tag, the `http.route` tag, and the route parsed out of the display name (`Telemetry/ProbeTelemetryFilterProcessor.cs:77-79`). [DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck) builds its relative probe URI from `Alive` (`Gateway/DownstreamServiceHealthCheck.cs:46`), so the gateway probes exactly the route its backends map.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The predicate takes a path only; it never sees a query string, and the doc comment states that as the caller's obligation (`:24-25`). Nothing enforces that a host which maps extra health routes puts them under `/health/`, so a differently named probe route would evade the telemetry filters.

### DownstreamProbeVersion

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:11` · Level 0 · enum

- **What it is**: The three-value vocabulary for "which HTTP version does the gateway's downstream `/alive` probe ask for": negotiate it, or pin one of the two answers.
- **Depends on**: Nothing first-party. Its doc comments reference `HttpVersionPolicy` (BCL). It is carried on [GatewayDownstreamHealthCheckOptions](#gatewaydownstreamhealthcheckoptions) and consumed by [DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck).
- **Concept introduced, protocol negotiation as a health-check concern.** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`, `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`. §13 assesses whether an operational signal tells the truth, and this enum exists because a probe that asks the wrong protocol lies in the most expensive direction: it reports a downstream outage that does not exist. The two facts that force the choice are in the options doc comment (`GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:46-54`). A service serving cross-service gRPC on cleartext runs Kestrel `Http2`-only, so h2c prior knowledge is the only thing it answers, and a stock `HttpClient` sends HTTP/1.1, which it refuses. Sending HTTP/2 unconditionally has the mirror-image failure: an HTTP/1.1-only endpoint, or a mixed `Http1AndHttp2` cleartext endpoint where Kestrel disables h2 because there is no ALPN to disambiguate, answers `HTTP_1_1_REQUIRED` forever. Neither fixed answer is right for every head, so the framework makes discovery the default and pinning the exception. **[ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html)**, in its 2026-08-29 update, is the governing record.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `Auto` (`:18`) is the zero value and the default. It tries HTTP/2 first and, when the downstream refuses the **protocol** rather than the connection, falls back to HTTP/1.1 **inside the same check**, so a single readiness poll still produces one verdict (doc comment `:12-17`). The version that answered is then latched for the life of the process, making the fallback a one-time cost per downstream rather than a per-poll one.
  - `Http2` (`:25`) always sends HTTP/2 over cleartext with `HttpVersionPolicy.RequestVersionExact`, so the request never negotiates down (`:20-24`).
  - `Http11` (`:32`) always sends HTTP/1.1 with `RequestVersionOrLower`, which is exactly stock `HttpClient` behavior (`:27-31`).
  - That `Auto` is the zero value is load-bearing beyond defaulting: the latch field in [DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck) stores the underlying `int` and reads zero as "not settled yet" (`DownstreamServiceHealthCheck.cs:50-57`).
- **Why it's built this way**: An enum rather than a `bool` because there are genuinely three states (discover, pin high, pin low), and the previous shape (a per-downstream `ProbeOverHttp2` boolean opt-out) could express only two. ADR-012 records that the boolean was removed outright in v1.168.0 with no compatibility facade left behind. Both pinned values are documented as optimisations ("to skip the one-time negotiation"), never as correctness requirements, which is what keeps a single `AddGatewayDownstreamHealthChecks` call correct for a mixed fleet.
- **Where it's used**: Set on [GatewayDownstreamHealthCheckOptions](#gatewaydownstreamhealthcheckoptions) (`GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:60`), captured at registration (`:174`), passed into each check (`:209`), and branched on at probe time (`DownstreamServiceHealthCheck.cs:70`, `:149`). Neither gateway pins a value today: ADC registers all four downstreams in one call (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:105`) and Store all three (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosts/MMCA.Store.Gateway/Program.cs:112`), both relying on `Auto`. Covered by [GatewayDownstreamHealthChecksTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), whose `Options_DefaultToAutoNegotiation` and the pinned-mode cases (`Probe_WhenPinnedToHttp2_NeverFallsBack`, `Probe_WhenPinnedToHttp11_SendsHttp11`) pin all three values.

### GatewayDownstreamRegistry

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:255` · Level 0 · class (sealed, internal)

- **What it is**: A registration-time ledger of the downstream service names already wired by `AddGatewayDownstreamHealthChecks`, so a repeated name cannot register a second health check under the same name.
- **Depends on**: `IServiceCollection` (it stores itself in the collection as a singleton instance) and `StringComparer` (BCL). Used only by [GatewayHealthCheckExtensions](#gatewayhealthcheckextensions); it hands out no runtime behaviour. Its AppHost-tier twin is [H2cHealthCheckRegistry](#h2chealthcheckregistry).
- **Concept introduced, registration-time state parked in the service collection.** `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`, `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`. `IServiceCollection` is a list of descriptors, not a queryable model: there is no supported way to ask "is a health check named X already registered", and a duplicate health-check name is a startup exception rather than a harmless second registration (class doc comment `GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:249-254`). The idiom the framework reaches for is to park a small mutable object *in the collection itself* as an `ImplementationInstance` and look it up on the next call, which makes idempotence a property of the registration API rather than a rule the caller has to remember.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `private readonly List<string> _names = []` (`:232`). The doc comment above it (`:227-231`) explains the choice of a list over a case-insensitive `HashSet<string>`: a gateway fronts a handful of services, and the comparer-carrying `HashSet` constructor is one of the initializer shapes the IDE0028 analyzer misreports here.
  - `TryClaim(serviceName)` (`:240`) returns `false` when the name is already present, compared with `StringComparer.OrdinalIgnoreCase` (`:242-245`), otherwise appends it and returns `true` (`:247-248`). Case-insensitivity is what makes a second call passing `"CATALOG"` a no-op rather than a duplicate.
  - `GetOrAdd(services)` (`:257`) walks the descriptors looking for one whose `ServiceType` is this type and whose `ImplementationInstance` is a registry (`:259-266`), returning it when found; otherwise it constructs one, registers it with `AddSingleton(registry)` and returns it (`:268-270`).
- **Why it's built this way**: `internal sealed`, because it is an implementation detail of one extension method and part of no public contract; the assembly's `<InternalsVisibleTo Include="MMCA.Common.Aspire.Tests" />` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/MMCA.Common.Aspire.csproj:13`) is what keeps it reachable from its tests. Scanning for an `ImplementationInstance` rather than building a provider is deliberate: this runs during registration, when no provider exists yet.
- **Where it's used**: The shared registration body fetches it once per call (`GatewayHealthCheckExtensions.cs:177`) and claims each name in the loop (`:181`). The idempotence it buys is asserted by [GatewayDownstreamHealthChecksTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) (`AddGatewayDownstreamHealthChecks_IsIdempotent`, which calls the method twice with overlapping and differently-cased names).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The registry is a registration-time artifact that stays registered as a singleton in the built provider; nothing consumes it at runtime. It also lives per `IServiceCollection`, so two independently-built collections do not share claims, which is what makes the tests independent.

### RedisConfigurationOptionsPipeline

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Caching` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Caching/RedisConfigurationOptionsPipeline.cs:19` · Level 0 · class (static, internal)

- **What it is**: A one-method helper that builds the StackExchange.Redis `ConfigurationOptions` for the two Redis connections this package opens outside Aspire's Redis client (the output-cache store and the PING health check's own connection), running them through the same post-configure steps Aspire's client options go through. It returns `null` when no such step is registered, so the caller keeps its plain connection-string path.
- **Depends on**: `IServiceProvider`, `IPostConfigureOptions<ConfigurationOptions>` and `Options.DefaultName` (`Microsoft.Extensions.Options`), and StackExchange.Redis `ConfigurationOptions`. No first-party type: it names neither MMCA.Common.Infrastructure's `IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator` nor any vendor adapter. Consumed by [RedisCachingExtensions](#rediscachingextensions) and [RedisPingHealthCheck](#redispinghealthcheck).
- **Concept introduced, reaching a configurator across a package boundary through a type both sides already know.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`. The class doc comment (`RedisConfigurationOptionsPipeline.cs:7-18`) states the problem. A Redis configurator, for example the Entra token login MMCA.Common.Azure registers through `AddAzureRedisEntraAuthentication` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure/AzureServiceCollectionExtensions.cs:230`), is an `IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator` declared in MMCA.Common.Infrastructure (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Caching/IRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator.cs:8-9`), a package this one does not reference. Aspire's client reads its options through the options pipeline and picks the configurator up for free; a connection this package builds by hand from the connection string would not, and would authenticate with whatever access key the string carries (the condition the startup tripwire names, `Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:167`). The meeting point is the abstraction both sides can name: the configurator registration adds an `IPostConfigureOptions<ConfigurationOptions>` (`:12-16`), and this helper replays every such step onto options it parses itself. Neither package learns the other's name.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `CreateConfigured(services, connectionString)` (`:36`, `internal static`) null-guards `services` (`:38`), collects every registered `IPostConfigureOptions<ConfigurationOptions>` (`:40`) and returns `null` when there are none (`:41-44`). Otherwise it parses the connection string with `ConfigurationOptions.Parse` (`:46`), applies each step under `Options.DefaultName` (`:47-50`) and returns the configured options (`:52`).
- **Why it's built this way**: Returning `null` rather than parsed-but-unconfigured options leaves the no-configurator path exactly as it is: callers assign the result only when it is non-null, so a host without a configurator still connects from the connection string. `internal`, because it bridges two framework call sites and is no consumer extension point; the public extension point is `AddRedisConfigurationOptionsConfigurator` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/DependencyInjection.Caching.cs:253`). Routing the hand-built connections through the same post-configure steps as Aspire's client means one configurator registration covers every Redis connection the framework opens, which is what [ADR-040](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/040-authenticated-output-caching-for-public-reads.html) relies on for the output-cache store.
- **Where it's used**: `AddRedisOutputCaching` assigns its result to `RedisOutputCacheOptions.ConfigurationOptions` when non-null (`Caching/RedisCachingExtensions.cs:115-123`), and [RedisPingHealthCheck](#redispinghealthcheck) calls it when opening its own connection (`Health/RedisPingHealthCheck.cs:102`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The connection string is parsed on every call and nothing is cached here; caching the result is the caller's concern. A post-configure step that throws surfaces where the options are resolved (the output-cache store or the health check), not at registration. Every registered post-configure step for `ConfigurationOptions` is replayed, whatever it configures.

### TrustedCallerMode

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/TrustedCallerMode.cs:8` · Level 0 · enum

- **What it is**: The two-value switch for how the edge limiter meters a request that proves the trusted-caller secret: against the end visitor it forwards, or not at all.
- **Depends on**: Nothing. Carried on [GatewayRateLimitingSettings](#gatewayratelimitingsettings) as `TrustedCallerMode` and branched on by [GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions).
- **Concept introduced, exempting the caller without exempting the visitor.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`. The trusted-caller secret exists because a server-rendered UI host sends every visitor's back-end calls from one address. Exempting that address outright stops the 429s, but it also leaves the visitor unmetered at the edge: every call the host makes on one visitor's behalf skips the per-IP window. `PerVisitor` keeps the fix and closes that gap. The host forwards the visitor's address in `X-Forwarded-For`, and the limiter counts the call against that visitor's per-IP window and every named route policy keyed on the visitor, exactly as the visitor's own direct request would be (`TrustedCallerMode.cs:10-16`). The replica-wide concurrency ceiling still exempts a trusted caller in both modes (`GatewayRateLimitingSettings.cs:158-165`), because that ceiling bounds replica load, not a visitor (`GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:339-343`). **[ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html)** is the governing record for the edge tier.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `PerVisitor = 0` (`:17`) is the default and the zero value. A trusted-caller request that carries a visitor address takes that visitor's window; one with no visitor address (a background call) takes the no-limiter partition (`:10-16`).
  - `Exempt = 1` (`:23`): every trusted-caller request takes the no-limiter partition on the per-IP window and the named route policies, whatever visitor address it carries (`:19-22`).
- **Why it's built this way**: The metered mode is the zero value, so a settings object built without naming the mode, or a configuration section that omits it, meters per visitor (`GatewayRateLimitingSettings.cs:166`). `Exempt` is an explicit choice for a deployment that wants every trusted-caller request unmetered. An enum rather than a `bool` names both behaviors at the configuration site.
- **Where it's used**: Read once, in `ClientIpPartition` (`GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:255-257`), which extracts the visitor address through `TrustedCallerVisitorIp` (`:302`) only under `PerVisitor`. Covered by [GatewayTrustedCallerPerVisitorTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Tests/Gateway/GatewayTrustedCallerPerVisitorTests.cs`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: `PerVisitor` trusts the address the caller writes: the limiter takes the right-most `X-Forwarded-For` entry left after the forwarded-headers step (`GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:292-298`), so a trusted caller that forwards a wrong address meters the wrong visitor. Which calling-host component writes that header, and whether either consumer gateway overrides the default mode, is not determinable from this package's source.

---

### GatewayRateLimitingSettings

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayRateLimitingSettings.cs:48` · Level 1 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: The bound configuration for the edge limiter: how many requests one client IP gets per window, how much work the replica will hold at once, which path prefixes are exempt, the header-plus-secret pair a synthetic-traffic run presents to skip both limiters, and the header-plus-secret pair and [TrustedCallerMode](#trustedcallermode) that decide how a trusted internal caller is metered. Every property has a working default, so the section is optional.
- **Depends on**: `System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations` (`[Range]`, `[Required]`, `[StringLength]`) and [TrustedCallerMode](#trustedcallermode). Consumed by [GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions). Its service-tier counterpart is [RateLimitingSettings](group-12-api-hosting-mapping.md#ratelimitingsettings) in `MMCA.Common.API`.
- **Concept introduced, the per-replica limiter and what it can and cannot promise.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience]`. The remarks on this type (`GatewayRateLimitingSettings.cs:11-36`) are the teaching material and are worth reading whole. Both limiters count **in this process's memory**, so the effective allowance is the configured number multiplied by the replica count: three replicas admit roughly 3 x `PermitLimit` per window from one client IP. That is the accepted trade, not an oversight. An edge limiter exists to stop one misbehaving caller from exhausting a replica's sockets and threads, and it must answer in microseconds on every request, whereas a shared counter puts a network round trip in front of the whole edge and fails open (or fails the edge) whenever the counter store blips. When a limit has to mean the same thing fleet-wide, the distributed counter in `MMCA.Common.API`'s `RateLimitingSettings.Distributed` is the tool. The second remark (`:22-27`) is the operational trap: partitioning is by `Connection.RemoteIpAddress`, so `UseForwardedHeaders` **must** run before `UseGatewayRateLimiting` or every request behind an ingress is attributed to the ingress and collapses into one window.
- **Concept introduced, the synthetic-traffic bypass.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`. The third remark (`:28-34`) names a failure mode that only appears once a system has a scheduled capacity proof: a load run drives its whole traffic from one runner IP, so the per-IP window cannot tell it from an unauthenticated flood and the run ends up measuring the limiter instead of the system. The answer here is a proof-of-secret, not a configuration switch: a run that presents the configured header carrying the configured secret takes the same no-limiter partition a bypassed path takes, and the whole mechanism is off unless the secret is set.
- **Concept introduced, generalizing the bypass to a trusted internal caller.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience]`. `TrustedCallerHeaderName`/`TrustedCallerSecret` (`:120-156`) apply the exact same proof-of-secret mechanism to a second, distinct problem: a server-rendered UI host makes all of its back-end calls, token refreshes above all, from ONE address on behalf of every signed-in visitor, so the per-IP window collapses the whole site into one partition and starts answering 429 under ordinary load. The per-IP limiter cannot tell that address apart from a genuine flood; the fix is not a bigger limit but a second, independently-configured secret that identifies a specific deployed component, never an end user's browser. `TrustedCallerMode` (`:158-166`) then decides what the proof buys: by default the call is counted against the end visitor the caller forwards in `X-Forwarded-For`, so the component escapes the shared-address collapse without its visitors escaping the limiter; only the concurrency ceiling exempts a trusted caller unconditionally. **[ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html)** covers this amendment alongside the synthetic-traffic one.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `SectionName = "GatewayRateLimiting"` (`:51`), a `static readonly string` used for options binding.
  - `PermitLimit` (`:60`, `[Range(1, 1_000_000)]` at `:59`) defaults to **120**, requests per window per client IP per replica. Its doc comment (`:53-58`) states the deliberate divergence from the service tier: this applies to anonymous traffic too, because the service-side global limiter exempts anonymous callers (public reads are output-cached and Blazor Server traffic shares one host IP) and the edge is exactly where an unauthenticated flood has to be stopped.
  - `WindowSeconds` (`:64`, `[Range(1, 3600)]` at `:63`) defaults to **60**.
  - `GlobalConcurrencyLimit` (`:74`, `[Range(1, 1_000_000)]` at `:73`) defaults to **200**. Its doc comment (`:66-72`) explains why a concurrency cap sits beside a rate limit: the failure it guards against is a slow downstream backing requests up until the edge runs out of threads and sockets, which no rate limit prevents and only a ceiling on simultaneous work does. Excess is rejected immediately with 429 rather than queued, so a saturated edge sheds load instead of growing latency.
  - `BypassPathPrefixes` (`:85`) is an `IReadOnlyList<string>`, empty by default, matched case-insensitively on whole path segments. The doc comment (`:76-83`) records that `/health`, `/alive` and `/.well-known` are always exempt regardless of this list, because probes and JWKS discovery run at high frequency by design and throttling them turns a traffic spike into a failed liveness probe and a container restart. It also states the entry rule: every entry must start with `/`, and a bad entry fails registration instead of failing every request. That rule is enforced by the property's `[LeadingSlashPathPrefixes]` attribute (`:84`), see [LeadingSlashPathPrefixesAttribute](#leadingslashpathprefixesattribute).
  - `SyntheticTrafficHeaderName` (`:94`, `[Required]` at `:93`) defaults to `"X-Synthetic-Traffic-Key"`. Only the **name** lives here, and the doc comment (`:87-92`) says why that is safe in `appsettings.json`: the header is worthless without the secret, so renaming it is a coordination change rather than a security control.
  - `SyntheticTrafficSecret` (`:118`, `[StringLength(int.MaxValue, MinimumLength = 32)]` at `:117`) is nullable and null by default, which disables the bypass entirely so no header value can claim it. The remarks (`:101-116`) carry two rules. It must come from a secret store or the environment (`GatewayRateLimiting__SyntheticTrafficSecret`) and never from a checked-in `appsettings` file, because a value in source control is a published key to the edge limiter. And the 32-character floor is validated at registration, so a short value fails startup rather than shipping a guessable bypass; `StringLength` treats null as valid, which is exactly the intended "off" state.
  - `TrustedCallerHeaderName` (`:128`, `[Required]` at `:127`) defaults to `"X-Internal-Caller-Key"`. Same shape as `SyntheticTrafficHeaderName`: only the name lives here, so it is safe in `appsettings.json`. The doc comment (`:120-126`) states that presenting it claims whichever treatment `TrustedCallerMode` selects, the no-limiter partition or the forwarded visitor's own window.
  - `TrustedCallerSecret` (`:156`, `[StringLength(int.MaxValue, MinimumLength = 32)]` at `:155`) is nullable and null by default, disabling the trusted-caller treatment entirely (`:130-135`). Its remarks (`:136-154`) carry the same two rules as `SyntheticTrafficSecret`: production must supply it from a secret store or the environment (`GatewayRateLimiting__TrustedCallerSecret`), never a checked-in file, and it must never be handed to client-side code since it identifies a deployed component, not an end user.
  - `TrustedCallerMode` (`:166`) defaults to [TrustedCallerMode](#trustedcallermode)`.PerVisitor`. The doc comment (`:158-165`) gives both modes: `PerVisitor` counts a proven request against the end visitor's own per-IP window and route policies when the caller forwards the visitor's address in `X-Forwarded-For`, and exempts it when no visitor address is present; `Exempt` exempts every trusted-caller request. The replica-wide concurrency ceiling exempts a trusted caller in both modes. It carries no validation attribute.
- **Why it's built this way**: `sealed` with `init`-only properties, so the settings object the limiter closes over cannot be mutated after validation. Data-annotation attributes rather than hand-written guards, because both entry points into the limiter validate them: the configuration overload through `ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` and the object overload through an explicit `Validator.ValidateObject` call, which is [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html)'s fail-fast contract honoured on both paths. The per-replica choice, the anonymous inclusion, the two-tier bypass and the synthetic-traffic and trusted-caller amendments are all recorded in **[ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html)**, which extends [ADR-019](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/019-rate-limiting.html) with this fourth, edge tier. The trusted-caller secret is a second, independent property rather than a reuse of `SyntheticTrafficSecret`, so the two exemptions can be granted, rotated and revoked one without the other.
- **Where it's used**: Bound from the `"GatewayRateLimiting"` section by `AddGatewayRateLimiting(IConfiguration)` (`GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:379-386`) and read by [GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions)' bypass checks and the two partition functions. Both consumers configure it in the gateway `appsettings.json` beside the route table: ADC keeps the defaults and bypasses `/hubs` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/appsettings.json:21-26`), because a SignalR negotiate-plus-reconnect storm from one shared office address is the pattern a per-IP window misreads as abuse; Store keeps the defaults and bypasses `/Payments` (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosts/MMCA.Store.Gateway/appsettings.json:16-21`), because a 429 to a Stripe webhook is a retry and eventually a disabled endpoint. The synthetic-traffic secret is deployment-only in both apps: a Key Vault secret named `synthetic-traffic-secret` mapped onto `GatewayRateLimiting__SyntheticTrafficSecret` and present only when the parameter is supplied (`MMCA.ADC/infra/main.bicep:979-982`, `:1690`; `MMCA.Store/infra/main.bicep:924-927`), and ADC's scheduled load test presents it as `X-Synthetic-Traffic-Key` (`MMCA.ADC/.github/workflows/load-test.yml:8-11`). `TrustedCallerSecret` is read on the calling-host side by `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI.Web/Security/TrustedCallerHandler.cs` and `DependencyInjection.cs`.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The range bounds constrain each value independently; nothing checks that `GlobalConcurrencyLimit` is sensible relative to `PermitLimit`, or that a bypass prefix matches a real route. The multiplication by replica count is documented on the type but not enforced or measured anywhere in source. Neither secret has a rotation mechanism in source.
