### IdentityLinkExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/IdentityLinkExtensions.cs:23` · Level 0 · class (public static)

- **What it is**: Two AppHost calls that point the Identity service's outward-facing URLs at the right resources: `WithUiLinks(ui)` roots the OAuth redirect and the three emailed links (password reset, email confirmation, sign-in link) at the UI's `https` endpoint, and `WithIssuer(gateway)` sets the JWT issuer to the gateway's `https` endpoint.
- **Depends on**: `Aspire.Hosting` (`IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>`, `GetEndpoint`, `ReferenceExpression`). The configuration it writes is read by the Identity service's own settings classes (`OAuthControllerBase.UIBaseUrlConfigKey`, `PasswordResetSettings`, `EmailConfirmationSettings`, `SignInLinkSettings`, `JwtSettings`), whose section names it mirrors.
- **Concept introduced, deriving a service's public URLs from the resource graph.** `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`, `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`. §33 asks whether the inner loop works without hand-tuned local configuration. Aspire allocates the UI and gateway endpoints at run time, so a hardcoded `https://localhost:6002/reset-password` in appsettings is a guess that breaks the moment a port moves. These calls hand Identity an endpoint reference instead, so every link it emails resolves to wherever the UI actually listens. Both calls add no reference and no wait (doc comments `:66-71`, `:94-97`), so they do not change the AppHost's startup graph.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Six `internal const` section mirrors (`:26-41`): `OAuth` plus the property `UIBaseUrl`, `PasswordReset`, `Authentication:EmailConfirmation`, `Authentication:SignInLinks` and `Jwt`. Three page routes from MMCA.Common.UI: `/reset-password` (`:44`), `/confirm-email` (`:47`) and `/auth/sign-in-link` (`:50`). The private `HttpsEndpointName = "https"` (`:52`).
  - `EnvironmentKey(section, property)` (`:61-62`) maps a configuration path to the environment-variable form .NET binds: every `:` becomes `__` and the property is appended after `__`, so `Authentication:SignInLinks` plus `SignInUrl` becomes `Authentication__SignInLinks__SignInUrl`.
  - `WithUiLinks(ui)` (`:74`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> identity)` block at `:64`) null-guards both builders (`:76-77`), resolves the UI's `https` endpoint once (`:79`) and sets four variables (`:81-91`): `OAuth__UIBaseUrl` to the bare endpoint, and `PasswordReset__ResetUrl`, `Authentication__EmailConfirmation__ConfirmationUrl` and `Authentication__SignInLinks__SignInUrl` to the endpoint plus the matching page route, each built with `ReferenceExpression.Create`.
  - `WithIssuer(gateway)` (`:100`) null-guards and sets `Jwt__Issuer` to the gateway's `https` endpoint (`:105`), the address clients see.
- **Why it's built this way**: The section names are mirrored as `internal const` rather than referenced, because this AppHost-tier package must not reference the service-side packages that own the settings classes (the same rule [Extensions](#extensions-1) applies to its rate-limit sections). `EnvironmentKey` is `internal` so [ServiceTokenExtensions](#servicetokenextensions) composes its own key the same way. Password reset links are the cache-backed flow recorded in **[ADR-091](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/091-cache-backed-password-reset.html)**; the AppHost-only scope is **[ADR-098](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/098-aspire-orchestration-not-testing-or-dashboards.html)**.
- **Where it's used**: ADC sets the issuer (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:385`) and the UI links (`:477`); Store does both at `MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:380-381`. `ServiceTokenExtensions.cs:73` reuses `EnvironmentKey`. Covered by `IdentityLinkExtensionsTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting.Tests/IdentityLinkExtensionsTests.cs`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The mirrored section names are not tied to the settings classes by anything in this file; source shows no cross-assertion here, so a renamed settings section would leave these variables unread.

### ServiceBusEmulatorResource

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceBusEmulatorResource.cs:32` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: The Aspire resource type for the official Azure Service Bus emulator container, publishing both of the emulator's planes and exposing the two connection expressions a consuming service needs.
- **Depends on**: `Aspire.Hosting.ApplicationModel` (`ContainerResource`, `IResourceWithConnectionString`, `EndpointReference`, `ReferenceExpression`, `EndpointProperty`). Provisioned by [Extensions](#extensions-1)' `AddServiceBusEmulatorBroker`, and consumed by the `WithBroker` overload that takes it. The settings it feeds are [MessageBusSettings](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebussettings) / [MessageBusProviders](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebusproviders).
- **Concept introduced, environment parity as a first-class local resource.** `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`, `[Rubric §17, DevOps]`, `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`. §33 assesses how closely the inner loop resembles production. RabbitMQ is fast and dependency-free but is a *different broker with different topology semantics*, so anything that only breaks on Azure Service Bus (entity naming limits, topic and subscription provisioning, scheduled redelivery) stays invisible locally until it reaches production. This resource closes that divergence for a stack that wants it. The class doc (`ServiceBusEmulatorResource.cs:10-16`) explains why **two** endpoints are non-negotiable: the emulator serves two planes on two protocols, AMQP for every publish and consume, and HTTP for the management plane that creates topics, subscriptions and queues. The Azure Service Bus transport provisions its own topology through that plane when its receiver starts, so a broker with no admin plane is not merely limited, it is unusable. **[ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html)**, in its 2026-09-01 revision, is the record.
- **Concept introduced, a connection-string marker as a mode switch.** `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`, `[Rubric §11, Security]`. `ConnectionStringExpression` ends in `UseDevelopmentEmulator=true`, and the doc comment (`:18-24`) says that suffix is not decoration: it keeps the Azure SDK clients on plain TCP and HTTP against a local host, and it is the marker `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure` detects to take its emulator configuration branch. A real Azure Service Bus connection string never carries it, which is what makes the emulator path impossible to enter by accident in production.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Four constants pin the wire contract: `AmqpEndpointName = "amqp"` (`:35`), `AdminEndpointName = "admin"` (`:38`), `AmqpTargetPort = 5672` (`:41`) and `AdminTargetPort = 5300` (`:44`).
  - The constructor (`:48-53`) calls `base(name)` and creates the two `EndpointReference`s against itself. The resource name doubles as the container's network alias (`:47`).
  - `AmqpEndpoint` (`:56`) and `AdminEndpoint` (`:59`) expose those references.
  - `ConnectionStringExpression` (`:66-68`) builds `Endpoint=sb://{host}:{port};SharedAccessKeyName=RootManageSharedAccessKey;SharedAccessKey=SAS_KEY_VALUE;UseDevelopmentEmulator=true;` from the **allocated** AMQP endpoint's host and port properties. The key name and value are the emulator's own fixed placeholders because it authenticates nothing (`:62-64`).
  - `AdminEndpointExpression` (`:81-83`) builds `{scheme}://{host}:{port}` from the admin endpoint. The scheme is read off the allocated endpoint rather than written literally (`:76-78`), so the value tracks whatever the endpoint was declared as (`http` today: the management plane is cleartext by design and reachable only from the local development stack). A consumer hands this to `MessageBus:EmulatorAdminEndpoint`, which is what lets the infrastructure layer build the second administration client the Azure Service Bus transport provisions topology through on the emulator (`:71-74`).
  - The last doc paragraph (`:25-30`) records a deliberate omission: **no health check is declared**. A `WaitFor` on this resource therefore gates on the container running, not on the emulator finishing its warm-up, and the consuming service absorbs the remainder because the transport's receiver provisions and connects in the background, retrying, rather than failing host startup.
- **Why it's built this way**: A dedicated resource type rather than a bare `AddContainer` because the two connection expressions are the reusable part, and `IResourceWithConnectionString` is what lets `WithReference` inject the AMQP string automatically. Both endpoint names and both container ports are `public const` so tests and consumers can assert them without string literals, which is exactly how [ServiceBusEmulatorBrokerTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) pins the published planes.
- **Where it's used**: Constructed by `AddServiceBusEmulatorBroker` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:261-298`) and consumed by the `WithBroker(IResourceBuilder<ServiceBusEmulatorResource>)` overload (`Extensions.cs:341-353`). Both consumer AppHosts can select it through [BrokerSelection](#brokerselection): ADC picks the emulator over RabbitMQ when `ADC_BROKER=servicebus` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:98`) and Store when `STORE_BROKER=servicebus` (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:74`), leaving RabbitMQ as the default inner loop. Covered end to end by [ServiceBusEmulatorBrokerTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) (`ConnectionString_IsTheEmulatorForm_BuiltFromTheAllocatedAmqpEndpoint`, `AdminEndpoint_IsBuiltFromTheAllocatedManagementEndpoint`).

- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The connection string is built from the *allocated* endpoint, so reading it before the application model has allocated ports is not meaningful. Nothing in this file constrains the emulator's own quota or TTL limits; that handling lives in MMCA.Common's `AddBrokerMessaging`, keyed off the `UseDevelopmentEmulator=true` marker, which is not this type's concern.

### ServiceEndpointProfile

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceEndpointProfile.cs:7` · Level 0 · enum

- **What it is**: The two cleartext endpoint profiles an MMCA service can run, used to pick the right AppHost startup probe for it.
- **Depends on**: Nothing. Read by [ServiceInfrastructureExtensions](#serviceinfrastructureextensions).
- **Concept reinforced**: the probe must speak the protocol the endpoint serves, taught at [H2cEndpointHealthCheck](#h2cendpointhealthcheck). `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`. The enum turns that rule into a required argument, so a new service cannot be wired without stating which profile it runs.
- **Walkthrough**: `Http2Only = 0` (`:13`): the cleartext endpoint is h2c only, so the probe is `WithH2cHealthCheck`, since a stock HTTP/1.1 probe is answered with GOAWAY and never turns healthy (`:9-12`). `Mixed = 1` (`:19`): the **[ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html)** mixed profile, where the default endpoint serves `Http1AndHttp2`, so Aspire's stock `WithHttpHealthCheck` works (`:15-18`).
- **Why it's built this way**: Explicit values, and an enum rather than a boolean, so the call site reads as the profile name. ADR-012 records the two profiles.
- **Where it's used**: The branch in `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:80-84`). ADC runs Notification as `Mixed` (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:138`) and Engagement, Conference and Identity as `Http2Only` (`:159`, `:197`, `:222`); Store runs Catalog and Identity as `Http2Only` (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:162`, `:187`).

### HostIntegrationMarker

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Integrations` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationMarker.cs:10` · Level 0 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: A registration receipt. Each vendor adapter's host-level call (a configuration source, an exporter, a DataProtection key-ring sink) adds one of these to the service collection so the cloud-neutral core can tell at startup that the call was made (doc comment `HostIntegrationMarker.cs:3-8`).
- **Depends on**: Nothing beyond `string`. Its `Name` is meant to be one of the [HostIntegrationNames](#hostintegrationnames) constants (`:9`), and the markers are read by [HostIntegrationTripwire](#hostintegrationtripwire).
- **Concept introduced, a receipt instead of a type reference.** `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`, `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`, `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`. The core packages never name an adapter type, because the adapters (`MMCA.Common.Azure`, `MMCA.Common.Aws`, `MMCA.Common.Gcp`) depend on the core and not the other way round. A marker carrying a well-known string is how the core still asks "did the Azure Key Vault call run?" without a compile-time edge to Azure. This is the dependency direction **[ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html)** records for the cloud-neutral core.
- **Walkthrough**: A primary constructor over `name` (`:10`) and one get-only property, `Name` (`:13`). Registered as a plain singleton instance, for example `builder.Services.AddSingleton(new HostIntegrationMarker(HostIntegrationNames.AzureKeyVaultConfiguration))` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:115`), so several markers coexist and resolve together as `IEnumerable<HostIntegrationMarker>`.
- **Why it's built this way**: `public sealed` because adapter packages outside `MMCA.Common.Aspire` must construct it. An instance per integration rather than one flag object keeps every adapter's registration a single additive line that cannot clobber another's.
- **Where it's used**: Added by every adapter call that has a configuration gate: `AddAzureMonitorExporter` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup/AzureMonitorExporterExtensions.cs:63`), `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration` (`AzureKeyVaultConfigurationExtensions.cs:84`), `AddAzureDataProtection` (`AzureDataProtectionExtensions.cs:57`), `AddAwsSecretsManagerConfiguration` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aws/AwsSecretsManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:125`), `AddAwsDataProtection` (`AwsDataProtectionExtensions.cs:88`), `AddGoogleSecretManagerConfiguration` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gcp/GoogleSecretManagerConfigurationExtensions.cs:116`), `AddGoogleCloudDataProtection` (`GoogleCloudDataProtectionExtensions.cs:103`) and `AddGoogleCloudTelemetryExporter` (`GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:127`). The core itself adds the `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` marker from `ConfigureOpenTelemetry()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.Telemetry.cs:148`). Two readers: [HostIntegrationTripwire](#hostintegrationtripwire) at startup, and the two vendor exporters, which scan the service descriptors for the `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` marker and throw `InvalidOperationException` when it is absent, so they cannot be registered ahead of the trace filter processors (`AzureMonitorExporterExtensions.cs:44-51`, `GoogleCloudTelemetryExporterExtensions.cs:104-107`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: Nothing enforces that `Name` is one of the [HostIntegrationNames](#hostintegrationnames) constants; a misspelled name is simply a receipt nothing checks.

### HostIntegrationNames

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Integrations` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationNames.cs:8` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: The vocabulary of well-known [HostIntegrationMarker](#hostintegrationmarker) names. The core registers `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` itself; every other name is registered by the adapter call that owns it and checked at startup against the configuration that needs it (doc comment `HostIntegrationNames.cs:3-7`).
- **Depends on**: Nothing. Read by [HostIntegrationTripwire](#hostintegrationtripwire) and by the adapter packages that write the markers.
- **Walkthrough**: Fourteen `public const string` values, each equal to its own identifier: `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` (`:14`, registered by `ConfigureOpenTelemetry()` and so by `AddServiceDefaults()`, the receipt a vendor exporter must be registered after), `AzureMonitorExporter` (`:17`), `AzureKeyVaultConfiguration` (`:20`), `AzureDataProtection` (`:23`), `AwsSecretsManagerConfiguration` (`:26`), `AwsDataProtection` (`:29`), `GoogleSecretManagerConfiguration` (`:32`), `GoogleCloudDataProtection` (`:35`) `GoogleCloudTelemetryExporter` (`:38`) and `AwsTelemetryExporter` (`:41`), then the four database and cache token-authentication receipts, registered by `services`-level calls that take `configuration`: `AzurePostgreSqlEntraAuthentication` (`:44`), `AzureRedisEntraAuthentication` (`:47`), `AwsRdsIamAuthentication` (`:50`) and `GoogleCloudSqlIamAuthentication` (`:53`). Each doc comment names the registering call and its package.
- **Why it's built this way**: Constants in the core rather than in each adapter, so the core can check a name without referencing the package that writes it ([ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html)). `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` is the odd one out: it is not checked by the tripwire but is the ordering receipt the Azure Monitor and Google Cloud exporters require.
- **Where it's used**: Every `HostIntegrationMarker` construction listed under [HostIntegrationMarker](#hostintegrationmarker), the nine `Check` and four `CheckBooleanGate` calls in [HostIntegrationTripwire](#hostintegrationtripwire) (`HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:98-168`), and `HostIntegrationTripwireTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Tests/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwireTests.cs`).

### HostIntegrationTripwireOptions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Integrations` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwireOptions.cs:8` · Level 0 · class (internal, sealed, empty)

- **What it is**: An empty options type whose only job is to carry [HostIntegrationTripwire](#hostintegrationtripwire) through `ValidateOnStart`, so the check runs with the other startup validators, before any hosted service starts (doc comment `HostIntegrationTripwireOptions.cs:3-7`).
- **Depends on**: Nothing; it has no members (`:8` is a bodyless `internal sealed class HostIntegrationTripwireOptions;`).
- **Why it's built this way**: The options validation pipeline is the framework's existing fail-fast hook (**[ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html)**). Riding it with a placeholder options type gets the tripwire the same timing and the same aggregated `OptionsValidationException` as every bound settings class, without inventing a second startup-check mechanism.
- **Where it's used**: Registered in the Common.Aspire [Extensions](#extensions-1) with `builder.Services.AddOptions<HostIntegrationTripwireOptions>().ValidateOnStart()` plus a `TryAddEnumerable` of `IValidateOptions<HostIntegrationTripwireOptions>` mapped to `HostIntegrationTripwire` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:50-52`). `AddServiceDefaults_ValidatesOnStart_AndFailsTheHost` in `HostIntegrationTripwireTests.cs:340` pins that wiring.

### H2cEndpointHealthCheck

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/H2cEndpointHealthCheck.cs:23` · Level 1 · class (sealed, internal)

- **What it is**: An AppHost-side `IHealthCheck` that GETs one path on an Aspire endpoint over HTTP/2 with prior knowledge (h2c) and reports the outcome as that resource's health.
- **Depends on**: `Aspire.Hosting.ApplicationModel` (`EndpointReference`), `IHealthCheck` / `HealthCheckResult` (ASP.NET Core diagnostics), and `SocketsHttpHandler` / `HttpClient` / `HttpVersionPolicy` (BCL). Registered by [H2cHealthCheckExtensions](#h2chealthcheckextensions), whose `ProbeTimeout` it reuses. It is the AppHost-tier analogue of [DownstreamServiceHealthCheck](#downstreamservicehealthcheck), sharing the same version pair.
- **Concept introduced, gating a `WaitFor` on a protocol the resource actually speaks.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`, `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`. Aspire's stock HTTP health check probes with a default `HttpClient`, so the request goes out HTTP/1.1 and an Http2-only cleartext endpoint refuses it with GOAWAY `HTTP_1_1_REQUIRED` (doc comment `:11-16`). The check therefore never turns healthy, the resource cannot be health-gated at all, and every `WaitFor` edge into it degrades to "the process started". The version pair below is the whole difference.
- **Concept introduced, a keep-alive ping as a correctness control rather than tuning.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience]`, `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`. This is the most instructive comment in the chapter (`:25-45`) and it describes a real wedge. The client is shared for the process lifetime and HTTP/2 pools **one** connection per origin. Aspire's endpoint proxy listens before the target Kestrel does, so the first probe of a starting resource can open a connection that is accepted and then never answered: the TCP connect succeeds, the HTTP/2 handshake never completes, and the proxy holds that socket open for the life of the AppHost. Without pings, every later probe queues onto that one zombie connection and times out forever, even after the service is up, so the resource can never turn healthy and every `WaitFor` edge into it wedges the whole stack (the comment cites the 2026-08-31 apphost-smoke wedge and nightly runs 33392642362 / 33399413579). The pings bound the zombie's life instead: a connection that stops answering pings is torn down and the next probe opens a fresh one. The comment also records the fix that does **not** work and why, `ConnectTimeout` does not cover this case because the connect itself succeeds.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `ProbeHandler` (`:46-52`) is a `static readonly SocketsHttpHandler` with `UseProxy = false` (`:48`, the target is a sibling process on the developer's own machine or a sibling replica inside the deployment network) and the ping trio: `KeepAlivePingDelay` and `KeepAlivePingTimeout` of one second each (`:49-50`) with `HttpKeepAlivePingPolicy.Always` (`:51`).
  - `ProbeClient` (`:59-60`) is one process-lifetime `HttpClient` over that handler, constructed with `disposeHandler: false` and `Timeout = H2cHealthCheckExtensions.ProbeTimeout`. Static rather than per-registration so a handful of gated resources do not each hold their own connection pool, and so this type owns no disposable instance state (`:54-58`).
  - Three readonly fields (`:62-64`): a `Func<string>` URL resolver, the path, and a send delegate. The production constructor (`:71-74`) takes an `EndpointReference` and passes `() => endpoint.Url` plus `ProbeClient.SendAsync`. The endpoint URL is resolved **on every check, not captured at registration** (`:18-21`), because the AppHost registers this check while building the application model and Aspire only allocates the endpoint once the resource starts.
  - The second constructor (`:88-96`) is the test one, taking the resolver and the send delegate directly. Its doc comment (`:76-83`) explains the choice of a send delegate over an `HttpMessageHandler`: it keeps this type free of a disposable instance field, since the shared client is process-lifetime state and a per-instance client would make every registration an owner of one.
  - `CheckHealthAsync` (`:99`) resolves the URL first, inside its own `try` (`:106-120`). An `InvalidOperationException` there means the endpoint has no allocation yet, which is the normal state for the first polls after the AppHost starts, and it returns the registration's failure status rather than Healthy and rather than letting the exception escape. The comment (`:112-115`) states the invariant: Aspire releases a wait only on Healthy, so a pre-allocation poll must not pass.
  - It then issues the GET (`:124-135`) with `Version = HttpVersion.Version20` and `VersionPolicy = HttpVersionPolicy.RequestVersionExact` (`:131-132`), the pair whose comment (`:128-130`) restates the h2c rule: with no TLS there is no ALPN to negotiate with, so the request has to go out as HTTP/2 and must not be allowed to fall back. Success returns `Healthy` with a description naming the status and the protocol (`:137-142`); any other status returns the registration's failure status with the same description (`:145`).
  - The catch (`:147-161`) is narrow (`HttpRequestException`, `TaskCanceledException`, `TimeoutException`, `InvalidOperationException`, `UriFormatException`) so a genuine programming error still surfaces. It carries **no cancellation guard**, and the comment (`:149-156`) explains that this is the considered choice rather than an omission: the registration's own timeout cancels the same token this method receives, so with such a guard a probe that outlives `ProbeTimeout` would slip past the filter and be logged by the health service as an unhandled exception rather than reported here. The other canceller is AppHost shutdown, where an Unhealthy result goes nowhere and costs nothing.
- **Why it's built this way**: `internal sealed` with two constructors, one for production and one that injects both collaborators, which is what lets the tests exercise the pre-allocation branch and the timeout branch without a running AppHost. The shared static client is the deliberate counterpart of that: process-lifetime state kept out of instance fields.
- **Where it's used**: Constructed by `WithH2cHealthCheck` inside its `HealthCheckRegistration` factory (`H2cHealthCheckExtensions.cs:131`). Covered by [H2cHealthCheckExtensionsTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), whose cases pin the version pair (`Probe_SendsHttp2WithPriorKnowledge`), the ping guard (`ProbeHandler_KeepsTheKeepAlivePingGuardAgainstPoisonedConnections`), the pre-allocation branch (`Probe_WhenTheEndpointIsNotAllocatedYet_ReportsUnhealthyWithoutThrowing`) and the cancelled-token branch (`Probe_WhenTheRegistrationTimeoutHasCancelledTheToken_StillReportsUnhealthyRatherThanThrowing`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: The static handler and client are shared across every gated resource in the AppHost process, so the ping settings are global rather than per resource. This type is AppHost-only: nothing in a deployed service uses it, and the platform's own probes go through the dedicated Http1 listener described at [KestrelEndpointExtensions](#kestrelendpointextensions).

### H2cHealthCheckExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/H2cHealthCheckExtensions.cs:42` · Level 1 · class (static)

- **What it is**: One call, `WithH2cHealthCheck(path, endpointName)`, that registers an HTTP/2 health check against a project resource's endpoint and associates it with the resource, so `WaitFor` edges into that resource wait for a real answer instead of merely for process start.
- **Depends on**: [H2cEndpointHealthCheck](#h2cendpointhealthcheck) (the check it constructs), [H2cHealthCheckRegistry](#h2chealthcheckregistry) (the de-duplication ledger), plus `Aspire.Hosting` (`IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>`, `EndpointReference`, `WithHealthCheck`) and `HealthCheckRegistration` (ASP.NET Core).
- **Concept introduced, a startup gate must probe liveness, never readiness.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`, `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`. This is the rule to carry away from the whole chapter, and the doc comment on `DefaultProbePath` (`:44-53`) argues it as a **cycle** rather than a preference. A readiness endpoint aggregates downstream and warm-up checks, so gating startup on it can deadlock the dependency graph: the gateway waits for a service to report ready, that service's readiness includes a warm-up task that fetches through the gateway, and neither side can complete first. Liveness answers as soon as the process can serve a request, which is exactly what a dependent resource needs to know before it starts. **[ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html)**'s 2026-08-29 update records the same reasoning and notes that the mixed-endpoint hosts' stock gates moved to `/alive` for the same reason, leaving only the UI resources (which nothing waits for) gated on `/health/ready`.
- **Concept introduced, recording a rejected alternative in the code that replaced it.** `[Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]`, `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`. The class doc's second half (`:24-35`) exists so one specific idea is not re-proposed: surfacing the deployed HTTP/1.1 health-probe listener as an Aspire endpoint and pointing a stock check at it. Injecting `HealthProbe__Port` locally flips [KestrelEndpointExtensions](#kestrelendpointextensions) out of endpoint-defaults mode and into explicit-listener mode, whose `Listen` calls then override the `ASPNETCORE_URLS` binding Aspire injects, so the service stops listening on the dynamic port Aspire allocated and every co-hosted service collides on one fixed cleartext port. The probe listener is a deployment-only construct; locally, the answer is to speak the protocol the service already serves.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `DefaultProbePath = "/alive"` (`:54`) and `DefaultEndpointName = "http"` (`:60`, the cleartext endpoint is the h2c one, since an `https` endpoint negotiates the version through ALPN and needs nothing special).
  - `ProbeTimeout = TimeSpan.FromSeconds(2)` (`:67`), matching the gateway's downstream budget, and short for the same reason (`:62-66`): Aspire polls this check for as long as a dependent resource is waiting, and a probe slower than the poll interval turns the wait into a queue.
  - `CheckKey(resourceName, endpointName)` (`:76-77`) composes `{resource}-h2c-{endpoint}`. The endpoint name is part of the key because a resource may expose more than one endpoint worth gating (`:69-71`).
  - `WithH2cHealthCheck(path = DefaultProbePath, endpointName = DefaultEndpointName)` (`:111`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> builder)` block at `:79`) guards the builder and rejects blank arguments (`:115-117`), reaches the AppHost's own service collection (`:119`), builds the key (`:120`), and **returns the builder unchanged** when the ledger has already claimed it (`:122-125`), which is what makes a second call a no-op rather than a duplicate-key startup exception.
  - It then resolves the endpoint with `builder.GetEndpoint(endpointName)` (`:127`). That resolution is lazy by design (`:105-108`): an endpoint name the resource never declares is not an error here, it surfaces as a permanently unhealthy check, which keeps the dependent resource waiting instead of letting it start against a service nobody verified.
  - The registration (`:129-134`) names the key, constructs an [H2cEndpointHealthCheck](#h2cendpointhealthcheck) over that endpoint and path, sets `failureStatus: HealthStatus.Unhealthy`, passes `tags: null` (AppHost-side checks are not on a service's `/health` endpoints, so the [HealthCheckTags](#healthchecktags) vocabulary does not apply), and applies `ProbeTimeout`.
  - The final `return builder.WithHealthCheck(key)` (`:138`) is the half that is easy to miss, and the comment says so (`:136-137`): `WithHealthCheck` only **associates** the named check with the resource, while the registration above is what supplies it, and both halves are required for a `WaitFor` edge to gate.
- **Why it's built this way**: An extension on `IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>` so it chains into the existing AppHost fluent wiring, with defaults chosen so the common call is `.WithH2cHealthCheck()` with no arguments. Idempotence is engineered rather than assumed (see [H2cHealthCheckRegistry](#h2chealthcheckregistry)). The doc comment is explicit that this replaces the stock extension only where it must: for an endpoint that serves `Http1AndHttp2`, Aspire's `WithHttpHealthCheck` is fine and this one is unnecessary (`:85-89`). The class carries the same scoped `CA1708` suppression as its siblings (`:37-40`).
- **Where it's used**: Applied with the default path to every [ServiceEndpointProfile](#serviceendpointprofile) `Http2Only` service by [ServiceInfrastructureExtensions](#serviceinfrastructureextensions)' `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:82-84`), so neither AppHost calls it directly any more than it calls `WithHttpHealthCheck` for its services. ADC gates Engagement, Conference and Identity that way (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:159`, `:197`, `:222`), with the AppHost's own narrative block repeating the rationale (`:328-337`); Store gates Catalog and Identity (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:162`, `:187`). [GatewayUiExtensions](#gatewayuiextensions) reuses `DefaultProbePath` for the gateway's stock liveness check. Covered by [H2cHealthCheckExtensionsTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), whose cases pin the defaults, the association, the registration, idempotence, per-endpoint keying, cross-builder isolation, the lazy endpoint resolution and both argument guards.

- **Caveats / not-in-source**: This is AppHost-only wiring, so it affects local and CI runs rather than a deployed environment: ADR-012 records that production probes keep targeting the dedicated Http1-only listener. Nothing here validates that the probed path exists on the resource; a wrong path produces a permanently unhealthy check, which by design keeps dependents waiting.

### HostIntegrationTripwire

> MMCA.Common.Aspire · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Integrations` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:15` · Level 1 · class (internal, sealed)

- **What it is**: A startup validator that fails the host when configuration asks for a vendor integration whose adapter call was never made. A deployment that still sets `KeyVault:Uri` but no longer calls `AddAzureKeyVaultConfiguration()` would otherwise start cleanly with every vault secret missing, or ship no telemetry at all, and look healthy while doing it (doc comment `HostIntegrationTripwire.cs:5-12`).
- **Depends on**: `IConfiguration`, `IEnumerable<`[HostIntegrationMarker](#hostintegrationmarker)`>` (the receipts), [HostIntegrationNames](#hostintegrationnames) (what each receipt is called) and `IValidateOptions<`[HostIntegrationTripwireOptions](#hostintegrationtripwireoptions)`>` (`Microsoft.Extensions.Options`), which is how it runs.
- **Concept introduced, a cloud-neutral core still owes a loud failure for a missing adapter.** `[Rubric §13, Observability & Operability]`, `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §17, DevOps]`. Moving vendor code out of the core ([ADR-134](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/134-cloud-neutral-core-vendor-adapters.html)) turns each vendor feature into an opt-in call, and an opt-in call can be forgotten without any compile error. The tripwire converts that silent omission into a startup failure that names the missing call and its package, in line with the fail-fast contract of **[ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html)**. The integrations it guards are the ones whose absence is invisible at runtime: telemetry export ([ADR-041](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/041-observability-and-telemetry.html)), secret sources ([ADR-061](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/061-runtime-secret-management.html)) and the shared DataProtection key ring ([ADR-069](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/069-shared-data-protection-key-ring.html)).
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Nine `internal const` presence-gate keys (`:20-56`): `APPLICATIONINSIGHTS_CONNECTION_STRING`, `KeyVault:Uri`, `DataProtection:BlobStorageUri`, `SecretsManager:NamePrefix`, `DataProtection:S3ObjectUri`, `GoogleSecretManager:NamePrefix`, `DataProtection:GcsObjectUri`, `GoogleCloudTelemetry:ProjectId` and `AwsTelemetry:Region`, beside the boolean database and Redis token gates (`:63-81`). One more constant is not a gate: the retired AWS key `DataProtection:SsmParameterPrefix` (`:39`), which fails startup whenever it is set, naming `DataProtection:S3ObjectUri` as its replacement (`:123-127`), because nothing reads it any more and a host still carrying it would otherwise run every replica on its own in-memory keys. The Google Secret Manager comment (`:41-46`) explains why the prefix, not the project id, is the gate: the prefix declares that the host reads its settings from Secret Manager, while a project id alone only names a location.
  - `KeyRingKeys` (`:84-89`) lists the three DataProtection gates, and `_registered` (`:91`) is a `HashSet<string>` of every marker's `Name`, built once in the constructor.
  - `Validate(name, options)` (`:94`) makes nine `Check` calls (`:98-147`), one per presence gate paired with its integration name and a consequence message that names the call to add and where (for example "Reference MMCA.Common.Azure.Startup (or MMCA.Common.Azure, which includes it) and call builder.AddAzureMonitorExporter() after AddServiceDefaults()", `:101`), then four `CheckBooleanGate` calls (`:149-168`) for the Azure PostgreSQL, RDS IAM, Cloud SQL IAM and Azure Redis token configurators. `Check` (`:183-189`) adds `"{key} is set, but {consequence}."` when the key is set and the receipt is missing; `IsSet` (`:207`) treats blank and whitespace values as unset. `CheckBooleanGate` (`:191-205`) reads its key through `ReadBooleanGate` (`:214-223`): blank or absent is off, `true` or `false` in any case parses, and any other value is itself a failure naming the accepted values, because unreadable is not off: whoever wrote `yes` or `1` meant on (`:196-197`). A true gate with no receipt adds `"{key} is true, but {consequence}."`.

  - A cross-check (`:170-178`): more than one configured key ring fails even when every adapter call was made, because whichever registration runs last owns the repository, so services that disagree on the order mint cookies and tokens the others cannot decrypt (`:170-172`). The message names the keys that are set and asks to clear the ones for clouds the host does not run on.
  - All failures are collected and returned together through `ValidateOptionsResult.Fail(failures)` (`:180`), so a host missing several calls learns about all of them in one start.
- **Why it's built this way**: An `IValidateOptions<T>` over an empty options type rather than a hosted service, so it runs with the other `ValidateOnStart` validators before any hosted service starts. It reads markers rather than adapter types so the core keeps no reference to Azure, AWS or Google packages. `ServiceDefaultsTelemetry` is deliberately not checked here: that receipt orders exporters and is enforced by the exporters themselves (see [HostIntegrationMarker](#hostintegrationmarker)).
- **Where it's used**: Registered unconditionally by the Common.Aspire [Extensions](#extensions-1) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:46-52`), so every host built on service defaults carries it. Covered by `HostIntegrationTripwireTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Tests/Integrations/HostIntegrationTripwireTests.cs`), including `ConfiguredWithoutTheAdapterCall_FailsNamingTheCall` (`:45`), `EveryMissingIntegration_IsReportedInOneFailure` (`:78`), `AllThreeDataProtectionKeyRings_FailOnce_NamingEach` (`:129`), `GoogleSecretManagerProjectIdAlone_StaysSilent` (`:147`) and `AddServiceDefaults_ValidatesOnStart_AndFailsTheHost` (`:177`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: It checks only that a receipt exists, not that the adapter call succeeded or ran in the right order. A gate key it does not list (any new vendor setting) gets no protection until a constant and a `Check` or `CheckBooleanGate` call are added here.

---

### Extensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:24` · Level 1 · class (static)

> Disambiguation: this is the **Common.Aspire.Hosting** `Extensions` (the AppHost-side broker, mail, JWKS, E2E and data-source wiring). The other class named `Extensions` in this chapter is the framework's [Common.Aspire `Extensions`](#extensions-1) (the canonical service-defaults bootstrap, service-side). Neither app ships a local `ServiceDefaults` project; their services consume the Common.Aspire one directly.

- **What it is**: AppHost-side Aspire extension methods that wire shared local infrastructure (a RabbitMQ broker or the Azure Service Bus emulator, a MailDev SMTP sink), JWKS-based identity discovery, CI/E2E signing keys and limit lifts, and per-service database routing onto Aspire resources.
- **Depends on**: [ServiceBusEmulatorResource](#servicebusemulatorresource) and `Aspire.Hosting` / `Aspire.Hosting.RabbitMQ` / `Aspire.Hosting.Azure` / `Aspire.Hosting.PostgreSQL` (`IDistributedApplicationBuilder`, `IResourceBuilder<ContainerResource>`, `IResourceBuilder<RabbitMQServerResource>`, `IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>`, `IResourceBuilder<SqlServerDatabaseResource>`, `IResourceBuilder<PostgresDatabaseResource>`, `AzureCosmosDBDatabaseResource`, `ReferenceExpression`). Conceptually pairs with [MessageBusProviders](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebusproviders) / [MessageBusSettings](group-14-module-system-composition.md#messagebussettings) (the env vars it sets), the JWKS auth side ([IJwksProvider](group-08-auth.md#ijwksprovider), [RsaJwksProvider](group-08-auth.md#rsajwksprovider)), the multi-source routing in [DataSourceResolver](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#datasourceresolver) / [EntityDataSourceRegistry](group-07-persistence-ef-core.md#entitydatasourceregistry), and [GatewayRateLimitingSettings](#gatewayratelimitingsettings) (the section its gateway lift writes into), plus [MailDevDefaults](#maildevdefaults) (the linked source of both MailDev ports).
- **Concept introduced, the AppHost tier is a separate package on purpose.** `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`, `[Rubric §8, Data Architecture]`, `[Rubric §17, DevOps]`, `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §32, Dependency & Supply-Chain]`. This assembly is deliberately separate from `MMCA.Common.Aspire` (the service-defaults assembly every running service consumes) so that running services do not pull in the heavy `Aspire.Hosting` tooling package (doc comment `Extensions.cs:14-18`). The rule bites in a visible place: rather than referencing `GatewayRateLimitingSettings.SectionName`, this class **mirrors the literal** as its own constant, and the comment (`:104-111`) states both halves of the trade, that the AppHost tier must not pull in the service-defaults graph (OpenTelemetry, Redis, the health-check drivers, Serilog) to spell one configuration key, and that a unit test cross-asserts the two are equal so a section rename cannot silently orphan the lift. That is the general answer to "a duplicated literal across a package boundary": duplicate it, then pin the duplication with a test. **[ADR-098](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/098-aspire-orchestration-not-testing-or-dashboards.html)** records the scope of Aspire adoption here (orchestration and service defaults, not a testing substrate). The class also carries a scoped `CA1708` suppression (`:19-22`) documenting the known analyzer false positive: with two or more `extension(T)` blocks in one static class, the compiler-generated grouping members trip the "identifiers should differ by more than case" rule.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - **Constants.** `DefaultBrokerResourceName = "rabbitmq"` (`:29`) and `E2eRegistrationsPerIpPerHour = 1000` (`:36`), the latter "high enough that no suite can reach it" while production keeps the real anti-abuse throttle (`:31-35`). The E2E group adds `E2eLiftTriggerVariable = "E2E_LIFT_REGISTRATION_THROTTLE"` (`:43`, internal, the CI trigger both lifts read), the InteractiveServer pair `E2eForceServerTriggerVariable = "E2E_FORCE_SERVER"` (`:49`) and `E2eForceServerConfigurationKey = "E2E__ForceServer"` (`:55`), and the InteractiveWebAssembly pair `E2eForceWebAssemblyTriggerVariable = "E2E_FORCE_WASM"` (`:63`) and `E2eForceWebAssemblyConfigurationKey = "E2E__ForceWebAssembly"` (`:69`); the doc comment on the first (`:57-62`) states that a forced-WebAssembly run also implies both lifts and vetoes `WithE2eForceServer`, so it needs no second variable. The lifted values are `E2eGatewayPermitLimit = 100000` (`:75`), `E2eGatewayGlobalConcurrencyLimit = 10000` (`:81`), `E2eAuthTightPermitLimit = 100000` (`:87`) and `E2eAuthIpPermitLimit = 100000` (`:94`), composed into keys from four internal constants: `RateLimitingSection = "RateLimiting"` (`:102`, mirroring `RateLimitingSettings.SectionName`), `GatewayRateLimitingSection` (`:112`), `MmcaGatewaySection = "MmcaGateway"` (`:119`) and `AuthTightPolicyName = "auth-tight"` (`:126`, the one part with no framework type to derive it from, because a route policy name is app configuration). The emulator group pins `DefaultServiceBusEmulatorResourceName = "servicebus"` (`:131`), `ServiceBusEmulatorImageRegistry = "mcr.microsoft.com"` (`:136`), `ServiceBusEmulatorImage` (`:141`) and `ServiceBusEmulatorImageTag = "2.0.1"` (`:150`), the tag comment (`:143-149`) recording that 2.x is a floor rather than a preference: the HTTP management plane the Azure Service Bus transport provisions its topology through shipped in 2.0.0, so a silent downgrade to a 1.x image leaves the broker unusable rather than merely older. The mail group fixes `DefaultMailDevResourceName = "maildev"` (`:155`), `MailDevHttpPort` (`:163`) and `MailDevSmtpPort` (`:169`), both read from the linked [MailDevDefaults](#maildevdefaults) so `MMCA.Common.Testing.E2E`'s `MailDevInbox` reads the inbox from the same port (`:157-162`), and `MailDevImageTag = "3.0.0"` (`:178`), pinned because the untagged image drifted (a fresh CI pull took 3.0.0 while a developer's persistent container stayed on 2.2.1) and 3.x serves the REST message list at `/api/email` rather than `/email` (`:171-177`).

  - **`AddMailDev(name)`** (`:201`, in an `extension(IDistributedApplicationBuilder builder)` block at `:180`): `AddContainer(name, "maildev/maildev", MailDevImageTag)` with a persistent lifetime and two published endpoints (`:206-209`). Both host ports are **fixed rather than dynamic** (`:186-191`), because the web UI is opened by hand and the SMTP port is what each consumer's `Smtp:Port` setting names; the persistent lifetime is what lets the captured inbox survive an AppHost restart. The last doc paragraph (`:192-197`) is the upgrade step that follows from it: a persistent container created from another image keeps running until removed, so remove it once (`docker rm -f`, AppHost stopped) after a tag change.

  - **`AddMessageBroker(name)`** (`:220`): `builder.AddRabbitMQ(name).WithManagementPlugin()` (`:224`), so one call provisions the broker container with its management UI. Production overrides the connection string via configuration so the same projects can target Azure Service Bus without an AppHost change (`:213-216`). See **[ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html)**.

  - **`AddServiceBusEmulatorBroker(sqlServer, name)`** (`:261`): the parity option. It constructs a [ServiceBusEmulatorResource](#servicebusemulatorresource) (`:269`) and chains `WithImage` **before** `WithImageRegistry`, with the comment explaining the ordering (`:272-273`): the registry call updates the image annotation the image call creates, and throws when there is none yet. It then publishes the AMQP endpoint as `tcp` and the admin endpoint as HTTP (`:276-282`), sets `ACCEPT_EULA=Y` (`:287`, with the comment at `:284-286` noting the image is only ever used for local development and test), passes the **existing** SQL Server's host and SA password as `SQL_SERVER` / `MSSQL_SA_PASSWORD` (`:288-293`), and finishes with `.WaitFor(sqlServer)` (`:297`) because the emulator's first act is to create its schema. The doc comment (`:227-260`) carries the design: the emulator stores its state in SQL Server, so it reuses the stack's engine rather than starting a second one, it is opt-in because it costs a container and a warm-up, and host ports are left to Aspire because nothing outside the stack dials them.

  - **`WithBroker<TResource>(broker)`, RabbitMQ overload** (`:312`, in a generic `extension<TResource>(IResourceBuilder<TResource> service) where TResource : IResourceWithEnvironment, IResourceWithWaitSupport` block at `:301-302`): `.WithReference(broker)` + `.WaitFor(broker)` + `.WithEnvironment("MessageBus__Provider", "RabbitMq")` (`:319-321`). One fluent step wires the reference, holds the service until the broker is healthy, and selects the transport. The generic constraint statically restricts the method to capable resources.

  - **`WithBroker<TResource>(broker)`, emulator overload** (`:341`): the same reference and wait, `MessageBus__Provider=AzureServiceBus`, and two more env vars, `MessageBus__ConnectionString` from the resource's emulator-form connection string and `MessageBus__EmulatorAdminEndpoint` from its management-plane expression (`:347-352`); the doc comment (`:324-330`) describes that address as what the Azure Service Bus transport needs to build its administration client. It then restates the mode-switch rule (`:331-337`): the connection string carries `UseDevelopmentEmulator=true`, which is the marker the Azure Service Bus transport keys its emulator branch off, and that transport is `AddAzureServiceBusTransport()` from `MMCA.Common.Azure`, which the service must register itself. A production connection string never carries the marker, so the emulator path cannot be entered by accident.

  - **`WithJwksDiscovery<TResource>(identity, gateway?)`** (`:370`, both parameters `IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>`): `.WithReference(identity)` + `.WaitFor(identity)` (`:378-379`), then a deferred `.WithEnvironment(context => ...)` (`:380`) that sets `Authentication__JwtBearer__Authority` (`:396`), which the consuming service passes to `AddForwardedJwtBearer(authority, audience, configuration, environment)` (`:357`). It **prefers the gateway HTTPS endpoint** over Identity's (`:393-395`) because, per the inline comment (`:382-392`), Identity listens HTTP/2-only on cleartext for gRPC h2c, but the default `JwtBearer` backchannel `HttpClient` sends HTTP/1.1, which Kestrel rejects on an Http2-only endpoint. The gateway terminates TLS, supports HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 via ALPN, and forwards `/.well-known/*` to Identity, so the metadata fetch works end to end. With no gateway passed it falls back to Identity's HTTPS endpoint, with the HTTP/1.1 caveat. See [ADR-004](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/004-authentication-dual-fetch.html), [ADR-008](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/008-service-extraction-topology.html) and [ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html).

  - **`WithE2eRsaKeys()`** (`:414`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> identity)` block at `:401`): a CI/E2E-only helper that reads `E2E_JWT_PRIVATE_KEY_PEM` / `E2E_JWT_PUBLIC_KEY_PEM` from the AppHost's own environment (`:418-419`), returns the builder unchanged when either is missing (`:420-423`), and otherwise maps them onto `Jwt__RsaPrivateKeyPem`, `Jwt__RsaPublicKeyPem` and `Jwks__RsaPublicKeyPem` (`:425-428`). The doc comment (`:403-411`) records the failure it prevents: without the forwarding, every CI login and register fails with "No supported key formats were found" and the readiness gate times out. It is a no-op locally and in production, where the variables are absent and user-secrets or Key Vault supply the keys.

  - **`WithE2eRegistrationThrottleLift()`** (`:456`): computes `lift` as `IsTrue(E2eLiftTriggerVariable) || IsTrue(E2eForceWebAssemblyTriggerVariable)` (`:460`) and, when lifted, sets two variables formatted invariantly (`:462-470`): `LoginProtection__MaxRegistrationsPerIpPerHour` to `E2eRegistrationsPerIpPerHour` and `RateLimiting__AuthIpPermitLimit` to `E2eAuthIpPermitLimit`; otherwise it returns the builder untouched. The doc comment states both problems. Production allows ten registrations per IP per hour, and an E2E suite registers far more than that from a single localhost IP, so the default refuses every register test past the tenth and the failures look like broken registration (`:432-438`). The per-IP auth limiter (default 30 a minute) runs out the same way, because with passkeys offered every visit to the login page spends one auth-ip permit on the passkey autofill options call before the login spends another, so the suite's logins fail with 429 (`:440-446`).

  - **`WithE2eForceServer()`** (`:490`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> ui)` block at `:474`): the render-mode peer of the two lifts, applied to the **UI** resource instead of a service. It computes `force` as `IsTrue(E2eForceServerTriggerVariable) && !IsTrue(E2eForceWebAssemblyTriggerVariable)` (`:494`) and, when forced, sets `E2E__ForceServer=true` (`:496-498`); otherwise it returns the builder untouched. The doc comment (`:477-481`) gives the reason: under InteractiveAuto the post-login second page load switches to the background-downloaded WASM runtime, whose boot on a shared 2-core CI runner exceeds every suite wait, so the suite pins the host to InteractiveServer. When `E2E_FORCE_WASM` also reads true nothing is written: forced WebAssembly wins (`:483-486`). The UI host reads `E2E:ForceServer` itself; this method only forwards the switch.
  - **`WithE2eForceWebAssembly()`** (`:514`): the opposite pin, for local runs. When `E2E_FORCE_WASM` reads true it sets `E2E__ForceWebAssembly=true` (`:518-520`), so every interactive page boots the WebAssembly runtime and the client render path can be exercised end to end; the UI host reads the key through `E2eRenderMode.Resolve` in MMCA.Common.UI.Web (`:502-506`). Absent in CI and in production, so it is safe to call unconditionally (`:508-510`).

  - **`WithE2eGatewayRateLimitLift()`** (`:548`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> service)` block at `:524`): the same trigger (`:552`) applied to the **edge** limiter, returning the builder unchanged when not lifted (`:554-557`). When lifted it writes three overrides (`:559-568`): `GatewayRateLimiting__PermitLimit`, `GatewayRateLimiting__GlobalConcurrencyLimit`, and the per-route `MmcaGateway__RateLimiterPolicies__auth-tight__PermitLimit`, all composed from the mirrored section constants rather than hard-coded strings. The doc comment (`:526-545`) is worth reading for the diagnosis: the whole suite arrives from one loopback client IP, so the per-IP window that protects production reads the suite itself as the flood (it names ADC run 32185349945 and its twelve login failures), and the named anti-credential-stuffing policy has the same problem for the same reason. It closes with the operational warning that a regression here surfaces as login and register E2E reds, never as a build failure. All three E2E triggers are read by the private `IsTrue(variable)` helper (`:689-690`): only `true`, in any case, switches a trigger on.

  - **`WithSQLServerDataSource(database, logicalName)`** (`:587`): the AppHost manifestation of **[ADR-006](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/006-database-per-service.html)**. One fluent chain: `.WithReference(database)`, `.WaitFor(database)`, then a single `.WithEnvironment` setting `DataSources__{logicalName}__SQLServerConnectionString` (`:594-597`), which the double-underscore convention flattens to `DataSources:{logicalName}:SQLServerConnectionString` for the multi-source routing layer. The doc comment (`:577-582`) is explicit that **that one entry is the whole configuration**: with no top-level connection string, the single database a host declares this way also becomes its `Default` source, so the framework's own tables and the readiness health check both land on it and the logical name collapses onto `Default`, giving one context, one change tracker and one migration set per deployed service.
  - **`WithPostgreSQLDataSource(database, logicalName)`** (`:617-628`, doc comment `:600-616`): the PostgreSQL peer of `WithSQLServerDataSource`, added alongside **[ADR-113](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/113-postgresql-as-a-first-class-engine.html)** (PostgreSQL as a first-class engine). Same shape, one fluent chain over `IResourceBuilder<PostgresDatabaseResource>`, setting `DataSources__{logicalName}__PostgreSQLConnectionString` instead. The doc comment restates the SQL Server rule verbatim: that one entry is the whole configuration, and with no top-level connection string the single database a host declares this way also becomes its `Default` source.
  - **`WithCosmosDataSource(database, logicalName)`** (`:646`): the Azure Cosmos DB sibling ([ADR-018](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/018-polyglot-persistence.html)). `.WithReference` + `.WaitFor` + two env vars: the account connection string the resolver hands to `CosmosDbContext.UseCosmos(...)`, and `CosmosDatabaseName`, since `UseCosmos` takes the database name separately. Unlike SQL Server, a service typically uses Cosmos for *one* module alongside its SQL Server source, so this is layered on top of rather than instead of `WithSQLServerDataSource`.
  - **`WithSqliteDataSource(logicalName, filePath)`** (`:671`): the SQLite sibling. SQLite has no Aspire container resource (it is an in-process file), so this only injects `DataSources__{logicalName}__SqliteConnectionString` built as `Data Source=<path>`. Note the different signature, a `filePath` string instead of a database resource.
- **Why it's built this way**: Fluent extensions on `IResourceBuilder<T>` match the Aspire AppHost idiom; the assembly split keeps service runtimes free of AppHost-only dependencies; the optional `gateway?` lets a monolith deployment still use JWKS by pointing straight at Identity. The four `With*DataSource` helpers share one naming shape so an engine move is a one-line AppHost change, and making `logicalName` an explicit parameter keeps each service's database identity discoverable by reading the AppHost; `WithPostgreSQLDataSource` mirrors `WithSQLServerDataSource`'s doc comment almost word for word rather than abbreviating it, which is deliberate: the "one entry is the whole configuration" rule is the part a reader must not miss on either engine. The five `WithE2e*` helpers are the same idea applied to test infrastructure: the *knowledge* that CI needs a keypair forwarded, the limiters lifted and the UI host pinned to one render mode lives in the framework rather than being copied into each app's AppHost. None takes a call-site flag, and each is inert unless its trigger environment variable is set (the two lifts read `E2E_LIFT_REGISTRATION_THROTTLE` or `E2E_FORCE_WASM`, `WithE2eForceServer` reads `E2E_FORCE_SERVER` and yields to `E2E_FORCE_WASM`, `WithE2eForceWebAssembly` reads `E2E_FORCE_WASM`, and `WithE2eRsaKeys` reads the key variables directly), so an AppHost calls every one unconditionally.
- **Where it's used**: The AppHost `Program.cs` of every consumer, much of it through the composite helpers. ADC chooses its broker through [BrokerSelection](#brokerselection) (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:98`), which calls `AddServiceBusEmulatorBroker(sqlServer)` when the variable is `servicebus` and otherwise `AddMessageBroker()` with a persistent lifetime, adds MailDev once (`:106`), and wires each service's database through [ServiceInfrastructureExtensions](#serviceinfrastructureextensions)' `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` (`:137-138`, `:159`, `:197`, `:222`), which calls `WithSQLServerDataSource` itself. It forwards E2E keys to Identity (`:231`), wires JWKS discovery for the three non-Identity services through the gateway (`:373-375`), pins the UI with `ui.WithE2eForceWebAssembly()` (`:409`) and `ui.WithE2eForceServer()` (`:417`), and applies both lifts (`:423` on Identity, `:430` on the gateway). Store does the same for its three services: broker via `AddSelectedBroker("STORE_BROKER", sqlServer)` at `MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:74`, MailDev at `:80`, `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure` at `:162`, `:187`, `:245`, `WithE2eRsaKeys()` at `:200`, `WithJwksDiscovery` at `:337-338`, the lifts at `:348` and `:357`, and the UI pins at `:405` and `:410`. The Helpdesk seed app calls the data-source helper directly on its single host: `MMCA.Helpdesk/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Helpdesk.AppHost/Program.cs:33`. `WithPostgreSQLDataSource`, `WithCosmosDataSource`, and `WithSqliteDataSource` are available framework plumbing but no consumer wires them today: every ADC and Store service runs on SQL Server. The E2E lifts are covered by [E2eLiftTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), `WithE2eForceServer` by `E2eForceServerTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting.Tests/E2eForceServerTests.cs`), the emulator path by [ServiceBusEmulatorBrokerTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup).

- **Caveats / not-in-source**: For all four `With*DataSource` helpers the `logicalName` must match the key the owning entity's configuration derives (module namespace or `[UseDatabase]`); source shows no validation of that string (only `WithSqliteDataSource` rejects a blank `filePath`, `:676`), so a mismatch routes that entity to the `Default` source rather than failing at startup. `WithSQLServerDataSource` sets only the `DataSources__` key; a host that also needs the top-level connection string sets it itself, as the Helpdesk AppHost does and explains (`MMCA.Helpdesk/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Helpdesk.AppHost/Program.cs:10-12`, `:34`). No consumer AppHost calls `WithPostgreSQLDataSource` yet, so it has no live usage site beyond the framework's own tests.

### ServiceTokenExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceTokenExtensions.cs:22` · Level 1 · class (public static)

- **What it is**: The AppHost half of service-to-service token signing: one call adds the shared signing key as a generated, persisted secret parameter, and a second hands that parameter to each service as `Grpc__ServiceToken__SigningKey`.
- **Depends on**: `Aspire.Hosting` (`ParameterResource`, `GenerateParameterDefault`, `AddParameter`, `IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>`) and `EnvironmentKey` from [IdentityLinkExtensions](#identitylinkextensions). The value is read service-side by `ServiceTokenSettings`, whose section and property names it mirrors.
- **Concept introduced, a generated secret that survives restarts.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`. Every service that mints or verifies a service token must hold the same key, and a developer should not have to create one by hand. `AddServiceTokenSigningKey` asks Aspire to generate the value when configuration supplies none and to persist it to the AppHost's user secrets (doc comment `:38-43`), so the key is random per developer, never committed, and stable across AppHost restarts. Handing services the parameter itself rather than a copy of its value (`:61-63`) keeps the secret flagged as a secret all the way to the resource.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `DefaultSigningKeyParameterName = "grpc-service-token-signing-key"` (`:28`), chosen because it is the user-secrets slot existing AppHosts already persisted the key under, so adopting the helper regenerates no developer key (`:24-27`). Two internal mirrors compose the target key: `ServiceTokenSection = "Grpc:ServiceToken"` (`:31`) and `SigningKeyProperty = "SigningKey"` (`:34`).
  - `AddServiceTokenSigningKey(name)` (`:46`, in an `extension(IDistributedApplicationBuilder builder)` block at `:36`) guards its arguments (`:48-49`) and calls `AddParameter` with `GenerateParameterDefault { MinLength = 64, Special = false }`, `secret: true` and `persist: true` (`:51-55`). Sixty-four letters and digits is valid Base64 decoding to 48 bytes, above the 32-byte floor `ServiceTokenSettings` enforces (`:39-41`).
  - `WithServiceTokenSigningKey(signingKey)` (`:67`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> service)` block at `:59`) guards and sets `Grpc__ServiceToken__SigningKey` to the parameter (`:72-74`).
- **Why it's built this way**: The section names are mirrored rather than referenced for the same AppHost-tier packaging reason as [Extensions](#extensions-1). The default parameter name is the compatibility choice: a rename would silently mint a new key on every developer machine.
- **Where it's used**: ADC adds the key once and hands it to Identity, Engagement and Notification (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:244-247`); Store adds it and hands it to Identity and Sales (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:212-213`, `:270`). Covered by `ServiceTokenExtensionsTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting.Tests/ServiceTokenExtensionsTests.cs`).

### GatewayUiExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/GatewayUiExtensions.cs:16` · Level 2 · class (public static)

- **What it is**: The edge half of an MMCA AppHost in three calls: `WithMmcaGateway` wires the gateway over the services it fronts, `WithMmcaUi` wires the UI head over the gateway and the services, and `WithGatewayApiEndpoints` tells the UI where the gateway lives.
- **Depends on**: `Aspire.Hosting` (`IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource>`, `WithExternalHttpEndpoints`, `WithHttpHealthCheck`, `WithEndpoint`, `GetEndpoint`) and [H2cHealthCheckExtensions](#h2chealthcheckextensions)' `DefaultProbePath`.
- **Concept reinforced**: probe liveness for a startup gate, taught at [H2cHealthCheckExtensions](#h2chealthcheckextensions), plus its one deliberate exception. `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`. The gateway's readiness aggregate includes one probe per downstream service, so a startup gate on it means any one unreachable downstream stops `ui.WaitFor(gateway)` from ever releasing; the doc comment (`:27-36`) names the 2026-08-29 UI WaitFor wedge, where the stack never became ready and a whole E2E run reported zero tests executed. The UI is the exception: it gates on **readiness**, because it runs the default `Http1AndHttp2` profile, maps the readiness endpoint itself, and nothing in the AppHost waits for it, so no startup cycle can form; the E2E suite and a developer's browser then wait for a real 200 (`:70-76`).
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `HttpsEndpointName = "https"` (`:18`).
  - `WithMmcaGateway(httpsPort, params services)` (`:41`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> gateway)` block at `:20`) guards its arguments (`:45-46`), adds a reference to and a wait on each service in the order given (`:48-52`), then marks the endpoints external, adds `WithHttpHealthCheck("/alive")` and pins the `https` endpoint to `httpsPort` (`:54-57`), the well-known URL every client and the E2E suite dial.
  - `WithMmcaUi(gateway, httpsPort, params services)` (`:82`, in the `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> ui)` block at `:61`) marks the UI external with a reference to and wait on the gateway (`:91-93`), a reference to and wait on each service (`:95-99`), then `WithHttpHealthCheck("/health/ready")` and the pinned `https` endpoint (`:101-103`). The UI project must be added with its `https` launch profile so that endpoint exists to pin.
  - `WithGatewayApiEndpoints(gateway)` (`:122`) sets both `Api__ApiEndpoint` and `Api__WasmApiEndpoint` to the gateway's `https` endpoint (`:127-131`). The doc comment (`:110-118`) explains why both: the WASM value is what the browser receives (and what the Blazor CSP pins `connect-src` to), and a browser cannot resolve an Aspire service-discovery name; the server value defaults to `http://gateway`, which every `IHttpClientFactory` client resolves, but a SignalR `HubConnection` builds its own handler, never sees the discovery handler, and so needs the real endpoint too.
- **Why it's built this way**: Both AppHosts wired the gateway and UI identically, so the shape lives once in the framework, with the probe rule written into it rather than left to each app. Deployed environments own their own probes: the doc comment records that the ADC and Store Container Apps also point the gateway's readiness probe at `/alive`, leaving `/health/ready` a reporting endpoint (`:34-36`).
- **Where it's used**: ADC wires the gateway on port 6001 over four services (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:364`), the UI on 6002 (`:394`) and the UI's API endpoints (`:469`); Store wires the gateway (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:320`) and the UI (`:367`) the same way.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: No test file for this type appears in the brief's usage sites; not determinable from source here which tests cover it.

### ServiceInfrastructureExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/ServiceInfrastructureExtensions.cs:16` · Level 2 · class (public static)

- **What it is**: One call, `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure`, that wires a backend service to its database, cache, broker and mail server and adds the startup probe its endpoint profile needs.
- **Depends on**: [Extensions](#extensions-1)' `WithSQLServerDataSource`, [BrokerSelection](#brokerselection)' `WithSelectedBroker`, [H2cHealthCheckExtensions](#h2chealthcheckextensions) and [ServiceEndpointProfile](#serviceendpointprofile), plus `Aspire.Hosting` (`IResourceBuilder<SqlServerDatabaseResource>`, `IResourceBuilder<IResourceWithConnectionString>`, `IResourceBuilder<IResource>`, `WithHttpHealthCheck`).
- **Concept introduced, one composite call per service as the AppHost's unit of repetition.** `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`, `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`. Every service needs the same five edges and one probe, and a hand-written chain per service is where one service quietly loses its cache wait or gets a readiness gate. The doc comment (`:20-44`) fixes the order (database, cache reference, broker, cache wait, mail wait, probe, so the waits are database, broker, cache, mail), keeps the service's endpoints internal because a backend is reached through the gateway (`:28-32`), always probes liveness (`:34-37`), and references only the cache, the database and the broker, never another project, so the AppHost's project-to-project edges are exactly what the host declares itself (`:39-43`).
- **Walkthrough**: `WithMmcaServiceInfrastructure(database, logicalName, cache, mailServer, attachBroker, profile, probeEndpointName = null)` (`:57-64`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> service)` block at `:18`) null-guards every resource and the delegate and rejects a blank `logicalName` (`:66-71`). It chains `WithSQLServerDataSource(database, logicalName)`, `WithReference(cache)`, `WithSelectedBroker(attachBroker)`, `WaitFor(cache)` and `WaitFor(mailServer)` (`:73-78`). The probe branch (`:80-84`): `Mixed` gets Aspire's `WithHttpHealthCheck("/alive", endpointName: probeEndpointName)`, where a null name leaves the endpoint choice to Aspire; `Http2Only` gets `WithH2cHealthCheck("/alive", probeEndpointName ?? "http")`.
- **Why it's built this way**: `attachBroker` is the delegate `AddSelectedBroker` returns, so this helper stays broker-neutral ([ADR-066](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/066-broker-transport-selection.html)). The profile is a required argument so the probe choice that **[ADR-012](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/012-grpc-host-transport.html)** records cannot be skipped. Host-specific additions (seeding switches, cross-service references, E2E keys) chain after the call, keeping the composite free of app knowledge (**[ADR-098](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/098-aspire-orchestration-not-testing-or-dashboards.html)**).
- **Where it's used**: ADC wires all four services through it, Notification as `Mixed` probing its `http` endpoint (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:137-138`) and Engagement, Conference and Identity as `Http2Only` (`:159`, `:197`, `:222`); Store wires Catalog (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:162`), Identity (`:187`) and Sales (`:245`). Covered by `ServiceInfrastructureExtensionsTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting.Tests/ServiceInfrastructureExtensionsTests.cs`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: Only SQL Server is accepted as the service database (`IResourceBuilder<SqlServerDatabaseResource>`, `:58`); a PostgreSQL service would chain `WithPostgreSQLDataSource` by hand.

### MmcaReplicas

> MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting · `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/MmcaReplicas.cs:24` · Level 4 · class (public static)

- **What it is**: The AppHost switch for running services as several replicas locally: `FromEnvironment` reads the count from an environment variable, and `WithMmcaReplicas` applies it to one service.
- **Depends on**: `Aspire.Hosting` (`WithReplicas`) and [Extensions](#extensions-1)' `RateLimitingSection` (`Extensions.cs:102`).
- **Concept introduced, rehearsing multi-replica behaviour in the inner loop.** `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`, `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`. Bugs that appear only with more than one instance (per-instance caches, in-memory rate-limit windows, background work running twice) are invisible to a single-instance stack. This type makes "run it at three replicas" an environment variable instead of an AppHost edit, and it carries the one setting a replicated service needs to stay correct: `RateLimiting__Distributed=true`, because without it each replica keeps its own in-memory window and a per-user limit multiplies by the replica count (`:53-57`). The rate-limiting design is **[ADR-019](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/019-rate-limiting.html)**.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `FromEnvironment(variableName)` (`:33`) rejects a blank name (`:35`), parses the variable as an invariant-culture integer (`:37-41`), and returns the value only when it parsed and is above 1, otherwise 1 (`:43`), so unset or unparsable means the single-instance stack.
  - `WithMmcaReplicas(replicas, distributedRateLimiting)` (`:63`, in an `extension(IResourceBuilder<DotnetProjectResource> service)` block at `:46`) returns the builder unchanged for a count of 1 or less (`:67-70`), so it is safe to call unconditionally with the `FromEnvironment` value; otherwise it calls `WithReplicas(replicas)` (`:72`) and, when asked, adds `RateLimiting__Distributed=true` (`:74-76`).
- **Why it's built this way**: The distributed switch is a parameter rather than automatic because the gateway's edge limiters stay per replica, as in production (`:57-59`).
- **Where it's used**: ADC reads `ADC_REPLICAS` and applies it to its four services with the distributed limiter and to the gateway without (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:449-454`); Store reads `STORE_REPLICAS` and applies it to its three services and the gateway, all with `distributedRateLimiting: false` (`MMCA.Store/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Store.AppHost/Program.cs:392-396`). Covered by `MmcaReplicasTests` (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting.Tests/Topology/MmcaReplicasTests.cs`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: Why Store passes `false` for its services is not stated in the AppHost lines read here; not determinable from the source cited.
