### GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway.RateLimiting` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/RateLimiting/GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:29` · Level 3 · class (static)

- **What it is**: the registration of the **named** rate-limiter policies a YARP route references by name. One `extension(IServiceCollection)` member, `AddGatewayRoutePolicies(GatewaySettings)`, plus the `internal static Partition` helper that decides what one request counts against and two `internal const` item keys it reads: `TrustedInternalCallerItemKey`, which exempts a request the edge limiter has already proven trusted, and `TrustedCallerVisitorIpItemKey`, which re-keys a trusted caller the edge limiter meters per visitor.
- **Depends on**: [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings), [GatewayRoutePolicySettings](#gatewayroutepolicysettings), [GatewayRetryAfter](#gatewayretryafter) and [RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter](group-12-api-hosting-mapping.md#remainingwindowfixedwindowratelimiter) (from `MMCA.Common.RateLimiting`). Externals: `System.Threading.RateLimiting` (`RateLimitPartition`, `FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions`, `QueueProcessingOrder`), `Microsoft.AspNetCore.RateLimiting` via `AddRateLimiter`, `HttpContext`, `System.Net.IPAddress`, `TimeProvider`, and `Validator` from data annotations. Called by [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions). Coupled by string literals, not a reference, to [GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions), which writes the `HttpContext.Items` entries this type reads.
- **Concept introduced, named policies as an additive second limiter.** `[Rubric §11, Security]`, `[Rubric §17, DevOps & Deployment]`. The doc's second paragraph is the operationally important part (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/RateLimiting/GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:17-23`): these policies are **additive** to any global limiter the host installs (for example `MMCA.Common.Aspire.Gateway.AddGatewayRateLimiting`, which assigns `RateLimiterOptions.GlobalLimiter`). ASP.NET Core evaluates the global limiter and the route's named policy independently, so a request must satisfy **both**. Nothing here overwrites the global limiter, and the two packages stay independent of each other. The division of labour is also worth naming: YARP already knows how to attach a named policy to a route, what it cannot do is create one (`:13-16`), so this method exists purely to fill that gap without every gateway hand-writing an `AddPolicy` block.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `TrustedInternalCallerItemKey = "MMCA.Common.Gateway.TrustedInternalCaller"` (`:40`), `internal const`. Its doc (`:31-39`) is the contract: the edge limiter in `MMCA.Common.Aspire` (`AddGatewayRateLimiting`) stamps this `HttpContext.Items` key with `true` once a request has proven the trusted-internal-caller secret, and because that limiter is the global limiter it runs before the route policy in the same middleware, so the stamp is present by the time `Partition` runs. The two packages do not reference each other, so the literal is repeated on the Aspire side (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:69`), which writes it at `:265`. A host without the edge limiter never stamps it, and every request is then limited as if the key did not exist.
  - `TrustedCallerVisitorIpItemKey = "MMCA.Common.Gateway.TrustedCallerVisitorIp"` (`:49`), `internal const`. Its doc (`:42-48`): when the edge limiter meters a trusted internal caller **per visitor**, it stamps this key with the forwarded visitor's `IPAddress` instead (Aspire-side twin at `GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:78`, written at `:271`). `Partition` then keys on that address rather than the connecting one, so a visitor's requests through a server-rendered UI host and the same visitor's direct requests share one window.
  - `AddGatewayRoutePolicies(GatewaySettings settings)` (`:61-95`) inside the `extension(IServiceCollection services)` block (`:51`).
    - Null-guards both the collection and the settings (`:63-64`), then **short-circuits when no policies are declared** (`:66-69`), so a gateway with no named policies never calls `AddRateLimiter` at all.
    - Validates every policy up front with `Validator.ValidateObject(policy, new ValidationContext(policy), validateAllProperties: true)` (`:71-74`). This runs before any registration, so an out-of-range `PermitLimit` throws at registration rather than at the first throttled request. The doc names the governing decision (`:56-57`), [ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html) fail-fast.
    - Calls `AddRateLimiter` (`:76`), sets `RejectionStatusCode = StatusCodes.Status429TooManyRequests` (`:78`), and wraps the existing `OnRejected` callback with [GatewayRetryAfter](#gatewayretryafter)`.WithRetryAfterHeader(options.OnRejected)` (`:82`), so a 429 from a named policy carries a `Retry-After` header in whole seconds. The comment above it (`:80-81`) records why it chains rather than assigns: `OnRejected` is one shared slot on `RateLimiterOptions`, and the edge limiter in `MMCA.Common.Aspire` installs the same header writer, so each registration wraps the one before it. It then registers one `options.AddPolicy<string>(name, ...)` per entry (`:92`), skipping blank names (`:86-89`). `var captured = policy` (`:91`) is the loop-variable capture guard that keeps each registered delegate bound to its own policy.
  - `Partition(HttpContext, GatewayRoutePolicySettings, TimeProvider? timeProvider = null)` (`:113-155`), `internal` so the partition-key selection is unit-testable (`:112`). The optional clock (`:116`, doc `:108-110`) only feeds the window's remaining-time hint; `null`, the production path, is the system clock. After its null guards (`:118-119`) it picks the client address in three steps (`:121-134`):
    - a visitor address under `TrustedCallerVisitorIpItemKey` wins: the trusted caller is metered on the same key the visitor's own request takes (`:122-126`);
    - otherwise a `true` under `TrustedInternalCallerItemKey` returns `RateLimitPartition.GetNoLimiter(GatewayRoutePolicySettings.GlobalPartitionKey)` (`:127-130`). The doc gives the reason (`:101-103`): this is the same exemption the edge limiter grants, and a server-rendered UI's sign-ins all leave from one address, so without it they would share one per-IP bucket;
    - otherwise `httpContext.Connection.RemoteIpAddress` (`:131-134`).
    - With the address chosen, it asks the policy for the key (`:136`) and branches (`:143-154`). A `null` key yields `RateLimitPartition.GetNoLimiter(...)`, the fail-open path [GatewayRoutePolicySettings](#gatewayroutepolicysettings) explains; otherwise `RateLimitPartition.Get(key, ...)` builds a [RemainingWindowFixedWindowRateLimiter](group-12-api-hosting-mapping.md#remainingwindowfixedwindowratelimiter) over `FixedWindowRateLimiterOptions` from the policy's `PermitLimit`, `WindowSeconds` and `QueueLimit`, with `QueueProcessingOrder.OldestFirst` and `AutoReplenishment = false` (`:145-154`).
- **Why it's built this way**: a fixed window is the only limiter shape exposed, which keeps the configuration surface two numbers wide. The comment above the return (`:138-142`) explains the wrapper: the framework's `GetFixedWindowLimiter` always reports the whole window as its `Retry-After`, so the wrapper reports the time actually left in it. `AutoReplenishment` is off because the wrapper forces it and because the middleware's partitioned endpoint limiter replenishes every partition on its own timer, which `GetFixedWindowLimiter` already relied on by turning the flag off itself. `RejectionStatusCode` is set here rather than left to the host so a throttled request is a 429 regardless of which package installed the limiter, and the `Retry-After` writer is chained in for the same reason: the caller learns when to come back whichever limiter refused it. The class carries the same scoped `CA1708` suppression as the other `extension(T)` classes in the kit (`:25-28`).
- **Where it's used**: called from `GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.Wire` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:106`), so both `AddMmcaGateway` overloads register it. In the consumer gateways the resulting `auth-tight` policy is enforced by the single `app.UseGatewayRateLimiting()` call that also serves the global edge limiter (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:203` with the explanation just above it at `:201`, `MMCA.Store/Source/Hosts/MMCA.Store.Gateway/Program.cs:191`). Covered by [GatewayRoutePolicyTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) and [GatewayRoutePolicyRetryAfterTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: `AddRateLimiter` is additive, so calling it from both this method and a host's own global-limiter registration is safe, but nothing here detects a host that declared policies and then forgot `UseRateLimiter()`. In that case the policies are registered and simply never applied, with no error. Both item keys rest on two copies of one string literal each (`GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions.cs:40` and `:49`, `GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:69` and `:78`); source shows nothing that ties them together at compile time, so a rename on one side would silently end the exemption (or the per-visitor keying) on the named policies while the edge limiter kept applying it.

### GatewaySettingsValidator

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettingsValidator.cs:19` · Level 3 · class (internal, sealed)

- **What it is**: the validator for the objects nested inside [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings): the trace-header settings, both halves of the health-check defaults, and the connect-retry block. It implements `IValidateOptions<GatewaySettings>` and also exposes a static `ThrowIfInvalid` for registration time.
- **Depends on**: [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings) and, through its `NestedObjects` list (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettingsValidator.cs:50-56`), [GatewayTraceHeaderSettings](#gatewaytraceheadersettings), [GatewayPassiveHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewaypassivehealthcheckdefaults), [GatewayActiveHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewayactivehealthcheckdefaults) and [GatewayRetrySettings](#gatewayretrysettings). Externals: `System.ComponentModel.DataAnnotations` (`Validator`), `Microsoft.Extensions.Options` (`IValidateOptions<T>`). Wired by [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions).
- **Concept introduced, validation that follows the object graph.** `[Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]`, `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`. The class doc states the gap it closes (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettingsValidator.cs:6-11`): `ValidateDataAnnotations` reads only the attributes on `GatewaySettings`'s own properties and does not recurse, so a blank trace-header name used to pass startup and then throw from every proxied request. This type validates each nested object explicitly, which is the ADR-070 fail-fast configuration contract applied to the gateway ([ADR-070](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/070-fail-fast-configuration-contract.html), [ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html)).
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `ThrowIfInvalid(GatewaySettings)` (`:23-29`): static; runs `Validator.ValidateObject(..., validateAllProperties: true)` on each nested object, so the first failure throws a `ValidationException`.
  - `Validate(string? name, GatewaySettings options)` (`:32-48`): the `IValidateOptions` path. Null-guards, then runs `Validator.TryValidateObject` on each nested object and collects every failure as `MmcaGateway:{TypeName}: {message}` (`:42-43`). Returns `ValidateOptionsResult.Success` when none, else `Fail(failures)` (`:47`), so a host sees all bad values at once rather than the first.
  - `NestedObjects` (`:50-56`), private: `TraceHeaders`, `HealthCheckDefaults.Passive`, `HealthCheckDefaults.Active`, `Retry` (`:52-55`). The last entry is what makes an out-of-range `MaxRetryAttempts` or `Delay` fail at registration and at start.
- **Why it's built this way**: two entry points for two moments. The static throwing form fails registration for both `AddMmcaGateway` overloads (the settings overload never touches the options pipeline), mirroring how the route policies fail fast. The `IValidateOptions` form is registered only by the configuration overload and re-checks the bound value at host start (class doc `:12-17`).
- **Where it's used**: `GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:70-71` (registered via `TryAddEnumerable`) and `:105` (`ThrowIfInvalid` inside `Wire`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the type is `internal`, so a host cannot reuse it or replace it. Only the four listed nested objects are checked; a new nested settings object added to `GatewaySettings` is not validated until it is added to `NestedObjects`.

### GatewayReverseProxyExtensions

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:35` · Level 4 · class (static)

- **What it is**: the single composition entry point for the gateway kit. Two `AddMmcaGateway` overloads on `IReverseProxyBuilder`, one binding from `IConfiguration` and one taking already-built settings, over one shared private `Wire` method, plus a private `AddConnectRetry` that swaps in the retrying forwarder factory when the retry is enabled.
- **Depends on**: [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings), [GatewayRetrySettings](#gatewayretrysettings), [GatewaySettingsValidator](#gatewaysettingsvalidator), [GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter](#gatewayclusterprofileconfigfilter), [GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter](#gatewayhealthcheckdefaultsconfigfilter), [GatewayRoutePolicyExtensions](#gatewayroutepolicyextensions), [GatewayTraceHeaderTransformProvider](#gatewaytraceheadertransformprovider), [GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider](#gatewaybypassheadertransformprovider), [GatewayHeaderEncoding](#gatewayheaderencoding), [GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory](#gatewayretryforwarderhttpclientfactory). Externals: `Yarp.ReverseProxy` (`IReverseProxyBuilder`, `AddConfigFilter`, `AddTransforms`, and `IForwarderHttpClientFactory` / `ForwarderHttpClientFactory` from `Yarp.ReverseProxy.Forwarder`), `KestrelServerOptions`, `Microsoft.Extensions.Options`, `Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration`.
- **Concept introduced, a composition root defined by what it declines to load.** `[Rubric §7, Microservices Readiness]`, `[Rubric §3, Clean Architecture]`, `[Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]`. The class doc is explicit (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:24-29`): it deliberately does **not** load the route table. `LoadFromConfig`, or any other `IProxyConfigProvider`, stays the host's call, because which section owns the routes, and whether they come from configuration at all, is a host decision. This method adds behavior **on top of** whatever config source the host chose. That single restraint is what reconciles a shared framework package with [ADR-089](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/089-gateway-topology-owned-by-configuration.html)'s consumer-owned route table, and [ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html) records the same package boundary from the edge-responsibility side.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `extension(IReverseProxyBuilder builder)` (`:37`) holding both public overloads.
  - `AddMmcaGateway(IConfiguration configuration)` (`:56-74`): null-guards (`:58-59`), takes the `MmcaGateway` section (`:61`), registers `AddOptions<GatewaySettings>().Bind(section).ValidateDataAnnotations().ValidateOnStart()` (`:63-66`), adds [GatewaySettingsValidator](#gatewaysettingsvalidator) as an `IValidateOptions<GatewaySettings>` through `TryAddEnumerable` (`:70-71`, because `ValidateDataAnnotations` does not recurse into the nested objects, `:68-69`), then calls `Wire` with `section.Get<GatewaySettings>() ?? new GatewaySettings()` (`:73`). The eager `Get<T>` is necessary because the named rate-limiter policies and the retry switch must be read at registration time, before any `IOptions<T>` can be resolved.
  - `AddMmcaGateway(GatewaySettings settings)` (`:82-93`): for a host composing its settings in code. It registers an explicit `IOptions<GatewaySettings>` singleton via `Options.Create(settings)` (`:90`) rather than a `Configure<T>` callback, and the inline comment says why (`:87-89`): `GatewaySettings` is init-only by design, since the filters close over it for the process lifetime, so the value has to be supplied whole rather than mutated into place.
  - `Wire(IReverseProxyBuilder, GatewaySettings)` (`:100-121`), private, in this order:
    - `GatewaySettingsValidator.ThrowIfInvalid(settings)` (`:105`) so both overloads fail fast at registration (a blank trace-header name would otherwise throw from `Headers.Remove("")` on every proxied request, `:102-104`);
    - `AddGatewayRoutePolicies(settings)` on the service collection (`:106`);
    - `AddConnectRetry` only when `settings.Retry.Enabled` (`:108-111`);
    - `AddOptions<KestrelServerOptions>().PostConfigure(GatewayHeaderEncoding.Apply)` (`:114`), post-configured so it wraps whatever selector the host's own `ConfigureKestrel` set (`:113`);
    - then chains `AddConfigFilter<GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter>()` (`:117`), `AddConfigFilter<GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter>()` (`:118`), `AddTransforms<GatewayTraceHeaderTransformProvider>()` (`:119`) and `AddTransforms<GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider>()` (`:120`), the last of which removes the edge limiter's trusted-caller and synthetic-traffic headers from every proxied request (class doc `:21-23`).
  - `AddConnectRetry(IServiceCollection)` (`:130-155`), private. It collects the non-keyed `IForwarderHttpClientFactory` registrations by implementation type (`:132-135`). Anything other than YARP's own `ForwarderHttpClientFactory` or the retrying factory, or a registration with no recoverable type (a factory delegate), throws an `InvalidOperationException` naming it and telling the operator to keep that factory and set `MmcaGateway:Retry:Enabled` to false, or remove it (`:137-146`). An existing retrying registration returns early, so a second call is a no-op (`:148-151`). Otherwise it adds `TimeProvider.System` with `TryAddSingleton` (`:153`) and registers [GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory](#gatewayretryforwarderhttpclientfactory) as the `IForwarderHttpClientFactory` (`:154`); the doc notes this lands after `AddReverseProxy`'s own `TryAdd`, so the last registration wins (`:123-127`).
  - The public method's `<remarks>` carry the two rules a reader needs (`:45-55`): the two config filters are independent so their relative order carries no meaning, and **this registers services and maps nothing**, so the host still calls `MapReverseProxy()`, and `UseRateLimiter()` if it declared any named policies.
- **Why it's built this way**: one entry point rather than a list of opt-in calls means a gateway gets the whole coherent set or none of it, which is what keeps two consumer gateways behaving identically. The two overloads exist because configuration binding and in-code composition are both legitimate: tests and in-process hosts build settings directly, deployed gateways bind a section. Both funnel through `Wire`, so there is exactly one list of what the kit installs. The retry is the one piece that replaces a YARP service rather than adding to it, which is why `AddConnectRetry` refuses to overwrite a factory the host composed itself instead of silently winning. The scoped `CA1708` suppression (`:31-34`) is the same `extension(T)` analyzer false positive documented across the kit.
- **Where it's used**: both consumer gateways chain it between `LoadFromConfig` and `AddServiceDiscoveryDestinationResolver`: `MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:172-174` (with a summary of what it adds starting at `:149`) and `MMCA.Store/Source/Hosts/MMCA.Store.Gateway/Program.cs:159-161` (summary starting at `:134`). Covered by [AddMmcaGatewayTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), which asserts that one call registers both config filters and the transform provider (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway.Tests/AddMmcaGatewayTests.cs:21`), that the settings overload makes them resolvable (`:43`), that a disabled retry keeps YARP's factory (`:107`), that an enabled one registers the retrying factory (`:122`), and that an enabled retry beside a foreign factory throws naming `MmcaGateway:Retry:Enabled` (`:148`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the configuration overload reads the section twice, once through the options pipeline and once eagerly via `Get<T>`. The eager read is the one the named rate-limiter policies and the retry switch come from, so a policy added, or the retry turned on, by a later configuration reload would not be registered. Nothing in source signals that; the overload's contract is a startup-time snapshot.

### BrokerResilienceDefaults

> MMCA.Common.Shared · `MMCA.Common.Shared.Resilience` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/BrokerResilienceDefaults.cs:25` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: four `static` properties that are the single source of truth for the circuit breaker guarding the outbox's broker-publish call: the failure ratio that opens the circuit, the minimum number of attempts before that ratio is judged, the rolling window it is judged over, and how long the circuit stays open.
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party, and nothing beyond `TimeSpan`, `double` and `int` (BCL). Read by [OutboxProcessor](group-04-events-outbox.md#outboxprocessor) in `MMCA.Common.Infrastructure`. It is the deliberate sibling of [HttpResilienceDefaults](#httpresiliencedefaults), and the class doc says so (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/BrokerResilienceDefaults.cs:5-7`): both live in Shared "so the numbers are reviewable in one place rather than buried as literals inside a background service".
- **Concept introduced, a breaker that shortens discovery rather than protecting a dependency.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`, `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`. The usual argument for a circuit breaker is to spare a struggling dependency further load. This one is about the **caller's** wasted time, and the class doc lays out the arithmetic (`:8-17`): when the broker is down, every publish in a 50-row batch waits out its own transport timeout before failing, so one cycle can spend minutes doing nothing but timing out, and the retry loop queues the same wait again on the next cycle. The breaker turns the second and later attempts into an immediate rejection. Crucially **nothing is lost by failing fast**: a row the circuit rejected stays unprocessed, is re-leased for about the `BreakDuration`, and is retried on a later cycle, and the rejection costs that row **no retry**, because nothing reached the broker (`:12-16`). The breaker only changes how long the processor spends rediscovering that the broker is still down. The second half of the doc (`:18-23`) is the composition rule worth internalising: it is deliberately **not** paired with a retry strategy, because the outbox already owns retry (`RetryCount`, exponential backoff with jitter, `MaxRetries` then dead-letter), and a Polly retry inside a publish would multiply against that budget and make a row's effective attempt count an accident of two independent policies.
- **Walkthrough**: four expression-bodied `static` properties, each carrying its own justification.
  - `FailureRatio => 0.5` (`:33`): half the publishes in the window must fail before the circuit opens. Half rather than a lower bar because a partially degraded broker still delivering half its messages is worth continuing to drain (`:27-32`), and only a clearly one-sided failure rate is evidence that further attempts this cycle are wasted.
  - `MinimumThroughput => 10` (`:41`): the minimum publish attempts inside the window before the ratio is evaluated at all. Ten keeps a quiet host, where two attempts an hour is normal traffic, from opening the circuit on a single unlucky pair (`:35-40`).
  - `SamplingDuration => TimeSpan.FromSeconds(30)` (`:48`): the rolling window. Thirty seconds is a few outbox cycles at the default polling interval, long enough that a batch's worth of attempts lands inside one window, short enough that a resolved outage ages out of the statistics quickly (`:43-47`).
  - `BreakDuration => TimeSpan.FromSeconds(15)` (`:57`): how long the circuit stays open before one trial publish is allowed through. Deliberately short, because a rejected publish costs one outbox row a wait of about this long and no retry, not a customer-facing error, so recovery latency matters more here than protecting the broker from one probe (`:50-56`). The probe is the exception to "no retry": a failed probe did reach the broker, so it counts against the probed row's retry budget like any other failed publish (`:54-55`).
- **Why it's built this way**: expression-bodied `static` properties rather than fields or constants, for the same reason as its sibling: `TimeSpan` cannot be a `const`, and a property is evaluated at call time instead of being inlined into the consuming assembly. Living in `MMCA.Common.Shared` rather than next to the processor is what makes the four numbers reviewable as a policy: a reader sees the whole breaker in one screen, with each value's justification attached to the member it justifies, without opening a 900-line background service. [ADR-009](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/009-resilience-and-recovery-objectives.html) is the governing decision: resilience is a framework invariant, not a per-call choice.
- **Where it's used**: [OutboxProcessor](group-04-events-outbox.md#outboxprocessor) builds its publish pipeline from all four (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Infrastructure/Persistence/Outbox/Processing/OutboxProcessor.cs:1077-1088`, values at `:759-762`), holds it in an instance field (`:99`) rather than a static one so parallel test processors cannot open each other's circuit (field doc comment `:87-98`), and logs an opening through `LogBrokerCircuitOpen` (`:664`). The pipeline also excludes `OperationCanceledException` from the handled set (`:763-764`), so a host shutdown cancelling a batch mid-flight is not counted as broker unhealthiness. Circuit openings surface as telemetry through the `MMCA.Common.Broker` meter that [Common.Aspire `Extensions`](#extensions-1) subscribes by literal name (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.Telemetry.cs:314`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the four values are not configurable. There is no settings class and no configuration key, so tuning them is a framework release. The breaker guards the broker-publish call **only**, never the database calls, which the processor's field doc states outright (`OutboxProcessor.cs:106-111`): a breaker on those would open exactly when the processor most needs to persist retry state.

### GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway.Transforms` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Transforms/GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider.cs:20` · Level 0 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: a second YARP request transform that strips the edge rate limiter's two bypass headers, the trusted-caller key and the synthetic-traffic key, from every proxied request, so the secrets they carry stop at the gateway (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Transforms/GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider.cs:7-11`).
- **Depends on**: YARP's `ITransformProvider`, `TransformBuilderContext`, the two validation contexts and the `AddRequestTransform` extension (`:2-3`), plus `Microsoft.Extensions.Configuration.IConfiguration` (`:1`). No first-party type: the header names belong to [GatewayRateLimitingSettings](#gatewayratelimitingsettings) in `MMCA.Common.Aspire`, which this package does not reference, so they are read from configuration by key (`:13-16`). Registered by [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions) beside [GatewayTraceHeaderTransformProvider](#gatewaytraceheadertransformprovider), which introduced the transform-provider concept.
- **Concept introduced, a secret that ends at the edge.** `[Rubric §11, Security]` assesses how credentials and trust boundaries are handled; here the boundary is the gateway itself. The edge limiter lets a caller presenting the right key skip rate limiting (a trusted internal caller, or a load test marked as synthetic traffic). Those keys are shared secrets, and a reverse proxy forwards inbound headers by default, so without this transform every downstream service, sidecar and header log would receive them. The class doc records why removing them is safe: the limiter reads both headers before the proxy runs, so its decisions are unaffected (`:10-11`). It is the mirror image of the trace-header transform: that one guarantees a header can only have come *from* the gateway, this one guarantees a header never goes *past* it. [ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html) is the gateway charter both transforms sit under.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Three `internal const` names (`:23`, `:26`, `:29`): the limiter's section name `GatewayRateLimiting`, and the default header names `X-Internal-Caller-Key` and `X-Synthetic-Traffic-Key`, "the same default the limiter uses" (`:16`).
  - Primary constructor with an optional `IConfiguration` (`:20`, `null` uses the defaults per the param doc at `:19`). The field `_headerNames` is resolved once at construction (`:31`).
  - `ResolveHeaderNames` (`:66-78`): reads `TrustedCallerHeaderName` and `SyntheticTrafficHeaderName` from the section (`:74-75`), each through `NameOrDefault` (`:80-81`), which falls back to the default on a missing or whitespace value and trims a configured one. The pair is passed through `Distinct(StringComparer.OrdinalIgnoreCase)` (`:76`), so a deployment that configures the same name for both removes it once.
  - `ValidateRoute` and `ValidateCluster` (`:35`, `:43`) are empty by design: the transform applies to every route and reads nothing from a route's own transform list (`:37-38`).
  - `Apply` (`:50-64`): null-guards the context (`:52`), copies the array into a local (`:54`), and adds a request transform that calls `ProxyRequest.Headers.Remove` for each name (`:55-63`). There is no enable switch: unlike the trace headers, removal always runs.
- **Why it's built this way**: reading the names by configuration key rather than through the settings type keeps `MMCA.Common.Gateway` free of a reference to `MMCA.Common.Aspire` (`:13-16`), at the cost of a string coupling. Resolving the names once in the constructor keeps the per-request delegate to one `Remove` per header.
- **Where it's used**: registered in the private `Wire` body shared by both `AddMmcaGateway` overloads as `.AddTransforms<GatewayBypassHeaderTransformProvider>()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:120`), so every gateway built on the kit strips the two headers.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the section name and the two key names are duplicated literals of the limiter's own configuration shape (`:22` names `GatewayRateLimitingSettings.SectionName` only in a doc comment), so a rename on the limiter side would not fail the build here. Not determinable from source alone: which test, if any, pins the two packages' names against each other.

### HttpResilienceDefaults

> MMCA.Common.Shared · `MMCA.Common.Shared.Resilience` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/HttpResilienceDefaults.cs:12` · Level 0 · class (static)

- **What it is**: fourteen `static` properties that are the single source of truth for every resilience and socket-handler value the framework's HTTP-based clients use: the outbound-HTTP Polly window (attempt timeout, breaker window, total timeout, retry count), the client-side `Retry-After` budget, the `SocketsHttpHandler` connection hygiene (pool lifetime, idle timeout, keep-alive ping delay and timeout), and five `Grpc`-prefixed values that are the east-west gRPC path's own timeouts and breaker shape (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/HttpResilienceDefaults.cs:12-94`). The class doc's own test of the design is that one file answers "what timeout does this client get" (`:3-11`).
- **Depends on**: nothing first-party and nothing outside the BCL (`TimeSpan`, `int`, `double`). All the arrows point *at* it: the Common.Aspire [`Extensions`](#extensions-1) service-defaults bootstrap (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:70-73,109-112`), the gRPC typed-client [DependencyInjection](group-13-grpc-contracts.md#dependencyinjection) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.Grpc/DependencyInjection.cs:127-130,144-164`), the UI's [AuthenticatedServiceBase](group-15-common-ui-framework.md#authenticatedservicebase) and `APIClient` registration, and [RetryAfterHeader](#retryafterheader), which reads `MaxRetryAfterWait`.
- **Concept introduced, the shared-constant class as a drift gate.** Most of this chapter is about behavior; this type is about *agreement*. Packages that ship separately have to configure the same Polly and socket numbers, and nothing in the type system makes them match, so the codebase turns the numbers into a third artifact all of them are obliged to read. `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]` assesses graceful degradation under partial failure, and the Polly values *are* the shape of that degradation: how long one attempt gets, how wide a window the breaker judges failures over, how long the caller waits in total, and how many times a single hop retries. [ADR-009](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/009-resilience-and-recovery-objectives.html) is the governing decision, and its first clause is exactly this posture: resilience is a framework invariant, not a per-call choice. `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]` shows up in the retry arithmetic below, because a retry policy is a load multiplier and multipliers compound per hop rather than adding. `[Rubric §31, Cost/FinOps]` is why the socket-handler block is here at all instead of being left at the BCL defaults: those four values are tuned for an idle Azure Container Apps replica (`:42-55`). And `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]` is the file's entire reason to exist: one edit moves every transport, so separately shipped packages cannot drift apart on a number.
- **Walkthrough**: fourteen expression-bodied `static` properties in four conceptual blocks.
  - *The outbound-HTTP Polly window*, the values handed to `AddStandardResilienceHandler`:
    - `AttemptTimeout` = 30 s (`:15`): the budget for one individual HTTP attempt.
    - `CircuitBreakerSamplingDuration` = 60 s (`:18`): the rolling window the breaker computes its failure ratio over, so a burst is judged against a minute of traffic rather than against the last handful of calls. The gRPC path shares this one value.
    - `TotalRequestTimeout` = 90 s (`:21`): the ceiling on the whole logical call including retries. The three numbers nest: a 30 s attempt inside a 90 s total leaves room for the initial attempt, the one retry, and the backoff between them.
    - `MaxRetryAttempts` = 1 (`:32`): retries *beyond* the initial attempt, deliberately one, shared by the HTTP and gRPC paths, and carrying the longest comment in the file (`:23-31`).
  - *Why one retry, the part worth internalizing.* The Blazor UI already owns user-facing retries: [AuthenticatedServiceBase](group-15-common-ui-framework.md#authenticatedservicebase) holds a static Polly policy that retries 3 times with exponential backoff (2 s, 4 s, 8 s) plus up to a second of jitter on `HttpRequestException` or a retryable response status (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI/Services/Api/AuthenticatedServiceBase.cs:21-37`), which is up to 4 attempts per user action. If every hop underneath also spent a full retry budget, the attempts would multiply instead of adding: the property doc puts the worst case at an up-to-16x request storm (`:25-27`). That is a retry storm, the failure mode where the load a struggling backend receives goes *up* precisely because it is failing. One transient-fault retry per hop still absorbs a dropped connection while bounding the multiplier.
  - *The one retry never replays a mutation* (`:29-30`). The value itself is a count; what keeps it safe is how each reader scopes it. The HTTP service defaults call `options.Retry.DisableFor(HttpMethod.Post, HttpMethod.Patch)` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:82`), because a timed-out attempt may still be running server-side and most hops carry no idempotency key, while GET, PUT, DELETE and the other idempotent verbs keep their retry (the call-site comment at `Extensions.cs:75-81` explains why it names two verbs instead of using `DisableForUnsafeHttpMethods`, which would also switch off PUT and DELETE). The gRPC path cannot use a verb filter at all, since every gRPC call is a POST, so it sets `Retry.ShouldHandle` to retry only an `HttpRequestException` whose `HttpRequestError` is `NameResolutionError`, `ConnectionError` or `SecureConnectionError`: a failure to reach the service before the request was sent, such as an ACA replica rollover (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.Grpc/DependencyInjection.cs:148-160`). An exception raised after the request frames were written is not retried, because the call may already have run.
  - *The `Retry-After` budget*: `MaxRetryAfterWait` = 8 s (`:40`), which equals the longest step of the UI backoff above. A `429` whose `Retry-After` asks for more than this is not retried at all and surfaces to the caller at once, so a refused user action costs one request rather than a held connection waiting out the rest of a rate-limit window (`:34-39`). [RetryAfterHeader](#retryafterheader) applies the comparison; both the Aspire pipeline and the UI policy read it.
  - *The socket-handler block*, the values handed to `SocketsHttpHandler`:
    - `PooledConnectionLifetime` = 10 min (`:46`): a pooled connection is recycled on this cadence, which forces DNS to be re-resolved, so an ACA replica rollover is picked up without an app restart.
    - `PooledConnectionIdleTimeout` = 5 min (`:49`): idle connections stay pooled that long, so low-traffic inter-service calls skip the TCP plus TLS handshake.
    - `KeepAlivePingDelay` = 60 s (`:52`) and `KeepAlivePingTimeout` = 30 s (`:55`): the HTTP/2 socket-level keep-alive ping interval and the timeout waiting for its acknowledgement.
  - *The east-west gRPC block*, introduced by a comment that explains why it exists (`:57-65`): an in-cluster gRPC call answers in well under a second (production p99 peaks below 800 ms), so the outbound-HTTP 30 s / 90 s budget would hold a caller on a hung peer for a minute and a half. The breaker shape is stated explicitly rather than left at library defaults because an east-west call addresses a peer directly and bypasses the Gateway's active health checks, so the breaker is the only thing that notices a peer going bad.
    - `GrpcAttemptTimeout` = 8 s (`:72`): about ten times the slowest measured east-west p99 (784 ms). The doc records the library constraint that `CircuitBreakerSamplingDuration` must stay at least twice this value (`:67-71`).
    - `GrpcTotalRequestTimeout` = 20 s (`:75`): two attempts plus the retry backoff.
    - `GrpcCircuitBreakerFailureRatio` = 0.5 (`:81`): an in-cluster peer is healthy or hard-down, so a tighter ratio would trip on ordinary replica-rollover blips (`:77-80`).
    - `GrpcCircuitBreakerMinimumThroughput` = 10 (`:87`): keeps a single failed call against a low-traffic service from opening the breaker (`:83-86`).
    - `GrpcCircuitBreakerBreakDuration` = 10 s (`:93`): about one container-replica restart, so recovery is not gated on the breaker (`:89-92`).
- **Why it's built this way**:
  - *The layer rules chose the address.* `MMCA.Common.Aspire` (Hosting), `MMCA.Common.Grpc` (Presentation) and the UI packages sit where none may reference the others; `Shared` is the one layer all are allowed to depend on, which the class doc states outright (`:9-10`). Both the Aspire and gRPC project files carry that `ProjectReference`, and the Aspire one names this type in a comment as the reason it is there at all (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/MMCA.Common.Aspire.csproj:57-59`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.Grpc/MMCA.Common.Grpc.csproj:21`).
  - *One file for both paths.* The `Grpc`-prefixed members give the east-west path deliberately tighter values than the outbound-HTTP ones, but they live in the same file beside the values they diverge from, and the comment introducing them says which values are the gRPC path's own and which are shared (`:57-65`). The divergence is therefore a stated decision a reviewer sees in one screen, not drift between packages.
  - *Properties, not fields or constants.* `TimeSpan` cannot be a `const` at all, and an expression-bodied `static` property is evaluated at call time rather than inlined into the consuming assembly the way a `const int` is at compile time. The flat one-line-per-value shape also keeps the file readable as a ledger. [BrokerResilienceDefaults](#brokerresiliencedefaults) is the same pattern applied to the outbox's broker-publish breaker.
- **Where it's used**: four production readers.
  - `AddServiceDefaults` routes it through `ConfigureHttpClientDefaults` (`Extensions.cs:57`), so *every* `HttpClient` in the process inherits it (per the comment opening at `Extensions.cs:54`): the four Polly values go into `AddStandardResilienceHandler` (`Extensions.cs:70-73`), with the POST/PATCH retry opt-out at `:82` and a `ShouldHandle` wrapper that refuses a 429 over `MaxRetryAfterWait` through `RetryAfterHeader.ExceedsRetryBudget` (`:90`), and the four socket values go into `ConfigurePrimaryHttpMessageHandler(() => new SocketsHttpHandler { ... })` (`Extensions.cs:107-115`).
  - `AddTypedGrpcClient<TClient>(serviceName)` re-applies the socket block (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.Grpc/DependencyInjection.cs:124-132`) and builds its resilience handler from the `Grpc`-prefixed members plus the shared `MaxRetryAttempts` and `CircuitBreakerSamplingDuration` (`:142-164`), then invokes the caller's optional `configureResilience` callback last so a consumer whose edge is legitimately slower can override any of them (`:167`, parameter at `:101`). It has to restate the socket values: forcing HTTP/2 means overriding the primary handler, which bypasses the wrapper the global `ConfigureHttpClientDefaults` installed, so the connection-hygiene values must come from the same source of truth (the reasoning is spelled out at `DependencyInjection.cs:115-123`).
  - [AuthenticatedServiceBase](group-15-common-ui-framework.md#authenticatedservicebase) does not retry a 429 over `MaxRetryAfterWait` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI/Services/Api/AuthenticatedServiceBase.cs:164`, doc at `:105-112`).
  - The UI's `APIClient` registration pins `HttpClient.Timeout` to `TotalRequestTimeout` instead of the BCL's 100 s, so the resilience budget rather than the transport decides when to give up (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI/DependencyInjection.cs:146-150`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**:
  - *Only the gRPC numbers are pinned by a test.* The ADR-009 fitness function [ResilienceHandlerTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) asserts that `AddTypedGrpcClient` wires its handler from these properties (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Presentation/MMCA.Common.Grpc.Tests/ResilienceHandlerTests.cs:68-76`), pins the gRPC literals and the shared retry and sampling values (`:178-184`), and checks the relationships between them, including the sampling window being at least twice `GrpcAttemptTimeout` (`:190-193`, `:210`). Runtime breaker behavior is covered separately by [ResilienceCircuitBreakerFaultInjectionTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), which drives its own short values. Not determinable from the cited tests: a test that pins the outbound-HTTP `AttemptTimeout`, `TotalRequestTimeout` or socket values at the Aspire call site.
  - *Two handler settings are still duplicated literals* rather than centralised here: `KeepAlivePingPolicy = HttpKeepAlivePingPolicy.WithActiveRequests` and `EnableMultipleHttp2Connections = true` (`Extensions.cs:113-114`, `DependencyInjection.cs:146,151`). The first one matters when reading the ping values: under `WithActiveRequests` the pings apply to connections that have outstanding requests, so what holds a genuinely idle pooled connection open is `PooledConnectionIdleTimeout`, not `KeepAlivePingDelay`.
  - *The latency figures are measurements recorded in comments.* The 784 ms p99 and "below 800 ms" (`:70`, `:60`) are production observations the source states but cannot demonstrate. Not determinable from source: whether they still hold for a given deployment.
  - *The billing claim is a platform statement.* "Does not count as user traffic to the ACA platform" (`HttpResilienceDefaults.cs:51`), expanded at the call site to idle-vCPU billing roughly 8x cheaper than active (`Extensions.cs:102-104`), describes how Azure Container Apps meters a replica. Not determinable from source: the code can set a ping interval, it cannot demonstrate how the platform bills it.

### MailDevDefaults

> Linked source (compiled into MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting and MMCA.Common.Testing.E2E) · `MMCA.Common.MailDev` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Build/MailDev/MailDevDefaults.cs:17` · Level 0 · class (internal static)

- **What it is**: two `const int` values, the host ports the local MailDev container is published on: `HttpPort = 1080` for the web UI and REST API (`MMCA.Common/Source/Build/MailDev/MailDevDefaults.cs:20`) and `SmtpPort = 1025` for the SMTP listener (`:23`).
- **Depends on**: nothing at all. It is read by the Aspire hosting [`Extensions`](#extensions-1) (`AddMailDev`, which re-exposes the pair as `MailDevHttpPort` and `MailDevSmtpPort`) and by the E2E package's `MailDevInbox`, which reads the captured inbox from the web port (class doc `:3-9`).
- **Concept introduced, a linked source file instead of a shared reference.** `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`, `[Rubric §33, Developer Experience]`. Two packages must agree on two integers, and the obvious fix (one package references the other, or both reference a third) would drag a dependency along: the E2E package would depend on the Aspire hosting SDK, or the hosting package on Playwright, "for two integers" (`:13-14`). So the file sits under `Source/Build/` and is compiled as an `internal` type into each package through a `Compile Include ... Link` item (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting.csproj:12`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.E2E/MMCA.Common.Testing.E2E.csproj:27`), the same pattern as `Source/Build/RateLimiting` (`:11-13`). A port move still lands in one edit, and neither package references the other (`:15`).
- **Walkthrough**: `internal static class` (`:17`) with two documented `public const int` fields (`:19-20`, `:22-23`). Nothing else.
- **Why it's built this way**: the ports are fixed rather than dynamic because a developer opens the MailDev UI by hand and every consumer's SMTP settings are written against the pair (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire.Hosting/Extensions.cs:156-168`). `const` is safe here despite compile-time inlining, because the type is `internal` and compiled into each consuming assembly from the same file, so no assembly can hold a stale copy of another's value.
- **Where it's used**: `Extensions.MailDevHttpPort` and `Extensions.MailDevSmtpPort` alias the two values (`Extensions.cs:163`, `:169`), and `AddMailDev` publishes the container on them as its `http` and `smtp` endpoints (`Extensions.cs:208-209`). `MailDevInbox` builds its default base address `http://127.0.0.1:{HttpPort}` from the web port (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Testing.E2E/MailDev/MailDevInbox.cs:33`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: because the type is `internal` in two assemblies, a consumer outside Common sees only the hosting package's `Extensions.MailDevHttpPort` / `MailDevSmtpPort` aliases. Not determinable from this file: whether a consumer's own `Smtp:Port` setting actually matches `1025`; the hosting doc states it does (`Extensions.cs:165-166`), but nothing here enforces it.

### GatewayConnectRetryHandler

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway.Forwarding` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayConnectRetryHandler.cs:20` · Level 1 · class (internal sealed partial)

- **What it is**: a `DelegatingHandler` on the client YARP builds for each cluster that retries a request only when it provably never reached the destination: a body-less GET or HEAD whose attempt failed with a name resolution, connection or TLS failure (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayConnectRetryHandler.cs:5-15`).
- **Depends on**: [GatewayRetrySettings](#gatewayretrysettings) (the attempt budget and the delay), `TimeProvider` for the delay, and `ILogger` for one Warning per retry (`:20-23`). BCL `DelegatingHandler`, `HttpRequestException` and `HttpRequestError`. Built by [GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory](#gatewayretryforwarderhttpclientfactory), the only constructor of it in source.
- **Concept introduced, retry only what cannot have run.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §11, Security]`. A gateway that retries freely can turn one client action into two writes at the destination, because a timed-out or reset attempt may already have been processed. This handler draws the line at the one failure class that proves the destination never saw the request: the connection itself was not established. It is the same rule the gRPC typed client applies with its `ShouldHandle` filter (see [HttpResilienceDefaults](#httpresiliencedefaults)), narrowed further at the edge to GET and HEAD with no body, "the only requests a replay cannot change the meaning of" (`:35`). A response of any status, any other exception, and a cancellation pass through untouched (`:9`). When the budget is spent the last exception propagates unchanged, so YARP still answers its usual 502 and the passive health policy still counts one failure (`:12-14`): the retry absorbs a replica rollover blip without hiding a destination that is really down. [ADR-088](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/088-gateway-edge-responsibilities.html) is the gateway charter this sits under.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `SendAsync` (`:26-33`): null-guards the request (`:28`), then routes a retryable request to `SendWithRetryAsync` and everything else straight to `base.SendAsync` (`:30-32`), so a POST pays nothing for the handler's presence.
  - `IsRetryable` (`:36-38`): `request.Content is null` and the method is GET or HEAD.
  - `IsConnectFailure` (`:41-44`): `HttpRequestError` is `NameResolutionError`, `ConnectionError` or `SecureConnectionError`.
  - `SendWithRetryAsync` (`:46-75`): a `while (true)` loop around `base.SendAsync` (`:53`). The `catch` is an exception filter (`:55-58`) that matches only a connect failure, while `retries < settings.MaxRetryAttempts`, and while the token is not cancelled; any exception the filter rejects is never caught, so it leaves the handler with its original stack. On a match it increments the count and logs (`:60-67`), then waits `settings.Delay` on the injected clock through `Task.Delay(TimeSpan, TimeProvider, CancellationToken)` when the delay is positive (`:70-73`).
  - `LogRetrying` (`:77-86`): a source-generated `[LoggerMessage]` at `Warning`, naming the method, the destination authority (or `(no destination)`), the `HttpRequestError`, and the retry number out of the budget.
- **Why it's built this way**: an exception filter rather than catch-and-rethrow keeps the final failure byte-identical to what YARP would have seen without the handler, which is what lets the 502 mapping and the passive health count stay unchanged. The injected `TimeProvider` makes the delay testable without real waiting.
- **Where it's used**: wrapped as the outermost handler of every cluster client by [GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory](#gatewayretryforwarderhttpclientfactory) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory.cs:64-68`), which exists only when `MmcaGateway:Retry:Enabled` is true. Covered by [GatewayConnectRetryHandlerTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway.Tests/Forwarding/GatewayConnectRetryHandlerTests.cs`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the retry is opt-in and off by default (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettings.cs:60-64`), and neither the ADC nor the Store gateway's `appsettings` sets a `Retry` block, so in those apps the handler is not installed. Not determinable from source: whether a deployment enables it through environment configuration.

### RetryAfterHeader

> MMCA.Common.Shared · `MMCA.Common.Shared.Resilience` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/RetryAfterHeader.cs:15` · Level 1 · class (static)

- **What it is**: both ends of the `Retry-After` contract on a `429 Too Many Requests` in one static class (`MMCA.Common/Source/Core/MMCA.Common.Shared/Resilience/RetryAfterHeader.cs:15-79`). The server half, `Format(TimeSpan)`, turns a rejected rate-limit lease's hint into the header value; the client half, `TryGetDelay(...)` and `ExceedsRetryBudget(...)`, reads the header back and decides whether a retry is worth waiting for.
- **Depends on**: [HttpResilienceDefaults](#httpresiliencedefaults) (`MaxRetryAfterWait`), plus BCL only: `HttpResponseMessage`, `HttpStatusCode`, `TimeProvider`, `CultureInfo`. Read by the rate limiters' `OnRejected` writers in `MMCA.Common.API`, `MMCA.Common.Aspire` ([GatewayRateLimitingExtensions](#gatewayratelimitingextensions)) and `MMCA.Common.UI.Web`, and by the retry policies in Aspire's [`Extensions`](#extensions-1) and the UI's [AuthenticatedServiceBase](group-15-common-ui-framework.md#authenticatedservicebase). [GatewayRetryAfter](#gatewayretryafter) carries a pinned copy of the server half.
- **Concept introduced, one rule for both sides of a throttle.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §12, Performance & Scalability]`, `[Rubric §15, Best Practices & Code Quality]`. A rate limiter that answers 429 is telling the caller when to come back; a client that retries on its own schedule either hammers a limiter that has not reset or, if it honors a long `Retry-After`, holds a connection and a user waiting out a full window. The class doc names the fix (`:6-14`): the server rounding and the client budget live in one place, on pure BCL types, so the API, Aspire and UI.Web limiters and the UI and Aspire retry policies all apply one rule. The budget itself is a numeric policy and so lives with the other numbers in [HttpResilienceDefaults](#httpresiliencedefaults); this class is the code that applies it.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - `Format(TimeSpan retryAfter)` (`:28-32`): `Math.Max(0d, Math.Ceiling(retryAfter.TotalSeconds))`, cast to `long` and printed invariantly (`:30-31`). Whole seconds, rounded up so a client never retries before the window has moved on, never negative. It takes the hint rather than the lease, and the doc gives the packaging reason (`:17-25`): `System.Threading.RateLimiting` ships in the ASP.NET Core shared framework, which this package, also loaded by Blazor WebAssembly, does not reference. So each `OnRejected` callback reads the lease metadata itself, and a lease without the hint (a concurrency-limiter rejection) sends the 429 with no header.
  - `TryGetDelay(HttpResponseMessage, TimeProvider, out TimeSpan)` (`:43-64`): null-guards both arguments (`:45-46`), then reads `response.Headers.RetryAfter` in either form. A delta is clamped at zero (`:49-53`); a date is measured against the supplied `TimeProvider` and a date already past becomes a zero delay (`:55-60`). No usable header returns `false` with a zero delay (`:62-63`). Taking a `TimeProvider` rather than reading the clock keeps the date branch deterministic under test.
  - `ExceedsRetryBudget(HttpResponseMessage?, TimeProvider)` (`:75-78`): `true` only when the response is a `TooManyRequests`, carries a usable `Retry-After`, and that delay is greater than `HttpResilienceDefaults.MaxRetryAfterWait` (`:76-78`). A `null` response (an exception outcome), a 429 without the header, or one at or under the budget is not over budget (doc `:66-74`).
- **Why it's built this way**: the predicate returns "do not retry" rather than computing a wait, so each caller keeps its own retry engine and only adds one refusal to it. Aspire wraps the standard handler's existing `ShouldHandle` and short-circuits to `false` when the budget is exceeded (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Extensions.cs:88-92`, reasoning at `:76-79`: the library's on-by-default `ShouldRetryAfterHeader` would otherwise hold the request for up to a minute); the UI policy treats the same response as non-retryable (`MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI/Services/Api/AuthenticatedServiceBase.cs:164`). Keeping it in Shared is what lets the WebAssembly-loaded UI and the server-side hosts call the same method.
- **Where it's used**: server half in three `WithRetryAfterHeader` wrappers: `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.API/Startup/WebApplicationBuilderExtensions.RateLimiting.cs:36`, `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Aspire/Gateway/GatewayRateLimitingExtensions.cs:98` and `MMCA.Common/Source/Presentation/MMCA.Common.UI.Web/Hardening/UiRateLimitingExtensions.cs:62`. Client half at `Extensions.cs:90` and `AuthenticatedServiceBase.cs:164`. Covered by [RetryAfterHeaderTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup), and pinned against the gateway copy by [GatewayRetryAfterTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) (`MMCA.Common/Tests/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway.Tests/RateLimiting/GatewayRetryAfterTests.cs:28`).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: three of the four limiter packages each carry their own near-identical `WithRetryAfterHeader` wrapper around `Format` (the Gateway copies `Format` too); only `Format` is shared, so the wrappers are kept alike by convention. The budget compares against one fixed 8 s value; a caller that wants a different ceiling has no parameter for it.

### AdcOutageAppHostFixture

> MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests · `MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests` · `MMCA.ADC/Tests/Integration/MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests/AdcOutageAppHostFixture.cs:17` · Level 3 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: the collection fixture that boots the real ADC AppHost (SQL Server with four databases, Redis, RabbitMQ, MailDev, the four services, the gateway and the UI) for the dependency-outage tier, and names every resource and endpoint the outage tests address (`MMCA.ADC/Tests/Integration/MMCA.ADC.AppHost.OutageTests/AdcOutageAppHostFixture.cs:9-17`).
- **Depends on**: [AppHostFixtureBase](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) from `MMCA.Common.Testing.Aspire.Fixtures`, closed over `Projects.MMCA_ADC_AppHost` (`:17`), and the precondition types `AppHostEnvironmentRequirement` and `AppHostReadinessBudget`. Aspire's testing and application-model types (`IDistributedApplicationTestingBuilder`, `ContainerResource`, `DotnetProjectResource`, `ContainerLifetimeAnnotation`, `EnvironmentCallbackAnnotation`). Shared through [AdcOutageCollection](#adcoutagecollection) with [AdcDependencyOutageTests](#adcdependencyoutagetests).
- **Concept introduced, an outage test needs a stack it is allowed to break.** `[Rubric §14, Testability]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`. Resilience claims (a cache or broker outage degrades health but keeps readiness, the outbox holds events until the broker returns) are only proven by stopping a real container under a running stack. That makes the fixture's main job safety: the AppHost declares its containers persistent so a developer keeps their data, and an outage test that stopped those would stop the developer's own containers. `UseSessionLifetimeContainers` (`:112-128`) moves every persistent container to `ContainerLifetime.Session` through Aspire's own `WithLifetime` (`:114-117`), then re-checks and throws if any still reads as persistent (`:119-127`), so the tier refuses to run rather than touch a developer's data. [ADR-117](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/117-apphost-integration-test-base.html) is the AppHost integration-test base this builds on.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Ten `public const` names (`:23-50`): `sql`, `redis`, `rabbitmq`, the Identity database `adc-identity` whose outbox carries `UserRegistered`, the `identity`, `notification` and `gateway` resources, Notification's cleartext `http` endpoint (it runs `Http1AndHttp2` there, so the plain HTTP/1.1 probe client gets a real answer while the other services are h2c-only, `:41-44`), and the gateway's `https` endpoint. One `private const` holds the full name of Aspire's experimental persistence annotation (`:20`).
  - The constructor clears `ADC_BROKER` (`:57`) so the AppHost selects RabbitMQ (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosting/MMCA.ADC.AppHost/Program.cs:98`); the doc gives the reason, the Service Bus emulator has no stop-and-start story this tier can assert (`:52-56`). The base restores the variable on dispose.
  - `RequiredEnvironment` (`:60-63`) demands the opt-in variable, Docker and a developer certificate, so a missing precondition makes every test skip rather than hang. `Budget` (`:69-70`) allows twelve minutes to start and eight to turn healthy. `ResourcesToAwait` (`:76-77`) waits only on Identity, Notification and the gateway, each liveness-gated in the AppHost, so the wait cannot deadlock on a dependency an outage takes away (`:72-75`).
  - `ConfigureBuilder` (`:86-100`) applies the session lifetimes (`:90`), then shortens the waits: Identity's outbox gets 20 retries, a 1 s backoff base, a 10 s lease and a 1 s polling interval (`:92-96`), and Notification's health report cache drops to one second (`:98-99`), each through an `EnvironmentCallbackAnnotation` added by `AddEnvironment` (`:154-155`).
  - `IsPersistent` (`:137-148`) checks both annotations Aspire consults: the last `ContainerLifetimeAnnotation` (`:139`), and the last persistence annotation, found by full type name and read by reflection because it is an experimental type; any `Mode` other than `Session` counts as persistent (`:144-147`). `ProjectNamed` (`:150-152`) throws when the AppHost declares no project with the name.
- **Why it's built this way**: reading the experimental annotation by name rather than by type avoids taking a compile-time dependency on an Aspire API that is still marked experimental, while still catching a container made persistent through the newer API (`:130-136`). The tuned outbox values are what keep a broker-outage scenario inside the test budget: without them a parked event would wait out production backoff before draining.
- **Where it's used**: the collection fixture of [AdcOutageCollection](#adcoutagecollection), injected into [AdcDependencyOutageTests](#adcdependencyoutagetests), which reads its resource-name constants for every scenario.
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the tier needs a container runtime and the opt-in variable, so it does not run in a default local test pass. The fixture asserts nothing about SQL Server outages; see [AdcDependencyOutageTests](#adcdependencyoutagetests) for why that scenario is absent.

### GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway.Configuration` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Configuration/GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter.cs:25` · Level 3 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: a YARP `IProxyConfigFilter` that resolves each cluster's forwarder request profile from three sources, per property, and rewrites the cluster only when the resolution actually differs from what it already carried.
- **Depends on**: [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings) via `IOptions<T>` and [GatewayClusterRequestProfile](#gatewayclusterrequestprofile). Externals: `Yarp.ReverseProxy.Configuration.IProxyConfigFilter` / `ClusterConfig` / `RouteConfig` and `Yarp.ReverseProxy.Forwarder.ForwarderRequestConfig`, plus `Microsoft.Extensions.Options`. Registered by [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions).
- **Concept introduced, the proxy config filter as a defaults layer.** `[Rubric §2, Design Patterns]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience, Reliability & Business Continuity]`. YARP loads its route table from a config provider and then runs every registered `IProxyConfigFilter` over each route and cluster before the table goes live. That hook is a decorator over configuration: it lets a package add behavior to a table it did not author and does not own, which is precisely what makes [ADR-089](https://ivanball.github.io/docs/adr/089-gateway-topology-owned-by-configuration.html)'s "the host owns the route table" rule compatible with a shared gateway package. **Precedence is per property, not per block**, and the class doc calls that out as the point of the type (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Configuration/GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter.cs:9-17`): a cluster that states one value keeps inheriting the rest. Per-block precedence would force a cluster that only needs a different timeout to also restate its version pair, which is the copy-paste this kit exists to remove.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Primary constructor `(IOptions<GatewaySettings> options)` (`:25`) with the settings snapshotted into a readonly field and null-guarded in the initialiser (`:27`). The filter closes over the value for the process lifetime, which is why the settings type is init-only.
  - `ConfigureClusterAsync(ClusterConfig, CancellationToken)` (`:33-43`): null-guards (`:35`), calls `Resolve` (`:37`), then returns the **same instance** when nothing changed and `cluster with { HttpRequest = resolved }` otherwise (`:39-42`).
  - `ConfigureRouteAsync(...)` (`:50-51`): returns the route untouched. Routes carry no request profile; only clusters do (`:45`).
  - `Resolve(ClusterConfig)` (`:59-74`), `internal` so the precedence rules are unit-testable through the filter's own type (`:58`): reads the cluster's own `HttpRequest` (`:62`), looks up the per-cluster override by id (`:64`), takes `ClusterRequestDefaults` as the fallback (`:65`), and builds one `ForwarderRequestConfig` from four independent resolutions (`:67-73`).
  - The four private resolvers are one expression each and all read identically, `own ?? over ?? fallback`: `ResolveVersion` (`:82-87`), `ResolveVersionPolicy` (`:95-100`), `ResolveActivityTimeout` (`:107-111`), `ResolveAllowResponseBuffering` (`:118-122`). The two version resolvers call the profile's `ParseVersion` / `ParseVersionPolicy` with the cluster id, so a bad string fails with the cluster named.
  - `IsSameAs(ForwarderRequestConfig?, ForwarderRequestConfig)` (`:131-142`) is the churn guard and the most easily missed subtlety. It substitutes `ForwarderRequestConfig.Empty` for an absent block (`:136`) because, as the inline comment says (`:133-135`), an absent block and an all-null block mean the same thing to the forwarder, so a cluster that had no profile and gained no defaults must come back untouched rather than acquiring an empty one. It then compares all four properties (`:138-141`).
- **Why it's built this way**: returning the identical instance when nothing changed is what keeps YARP's config-change detection quiet on every reload (`:124-127`). A filter that always returned a new record would make YARP believe the topology changed on every configuration refresh and churn its route table for nothing. The second doc paragraph (`:18-22`) records the deliberate absence of a downgrade switch: a resolved HTTP/2 version pair is forwarded as stated, and dropping a cluster to HTTP/1.1 is a per-cluster decision expressed in its own profile.
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddMmcaGateway` through `builder.AddConfigFilter<GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter>()` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:117`). In ADC it replaced the host's own hand-written filter of the same purpose (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/Program.cs:146-148`). Covered by [GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilterTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the settings are captured once from `IOptions<T>`, not `IOptionsMonitor<T>`, so a configuration reload changes the route table YARP reloads but not the defaults this filter applies. Restarting the host is the only way to move them.

### GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway.Configuration` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Configuration/GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter.cs:17` · Level 3 · class (sealed)

- **What it is**: the second YARP `IProxyConfigFilter` in the kit. It fills in destination health-check defaults for clusters that declare none, so the proxy actually ejects a failing destination instead of continuing to balance onto it.
- **Depends on**: [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings) via `IOptions<T>`, and through it [GatewayHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewayhealthcheckdefaults) with its [GatewayPassiveHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewaypassivehealthcheckdefaults) and [GatewayActiveHealthCheckDefaults](#gatewayactivehealthcheckdefaults) children. Externals: YARP's `IProxyConfigFilter`, `ClusterConfig`, `HealthCheckConfig`, `PassiveHealthCheckConfig`, `ActiveHealthCheckConfig`. Registered by [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions). It shares the config-filter concept introduced by [GatewayClusterProfileConfigFilter](#gatewayclusterprofileconfigfilter).
- **Concept introduced, additive-only defaulting.** `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`, `[Rubric §34, Architecture Governance & Documentation]`. The class doc states the invariant (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Configuration/GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilter.cs:9-14`): a cluster that already states a `Passive` block keeps it verbatim, and the same holds for `Active`. **The filter never edits an operator's explicit choice, it only supplies one where the configuration was silent.** That rule is what makes a defaults filter safe to add to an existing gateway: the worst case for a fully configured cluster is that the filter does nothing. Note it is per block, not per property: a cluster that states a partial `Passive` block does not have the missing properties filled in.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Primary constructor and null-guarded settings field (`:17`, `:19`), the same shape as its sibling filter.
  - `ConfigureClusterAsync` (`:25-47`): reads the existing `HealthCheck` (`:29`), then `existing?.Passive ?? BuildPassive()` (`:30`) and `existing?.Active ?? BuildActive()` (`:31`). If both resolved to the very same references the cluster already held, it returns the cluster unchanged (`:33-36`), the same churn guard the profile filter uses, expressed here with `ReferenceEquals`. Otherwise it rebuilds `HealthCheck` with the two blocks and **carries `AvailableDestinationsPolicy` across unchanged** (`:44`), so an operator's destination-selection choice survives the rewrite.
  - `ConfigureRouteAsync` (`:54-55`): routes carry no health-check configuration; returns the route unchanged.
  - `BuildPassive()` (`:59-70`): returns a `PassiveHealthCheckConfig` from the settings when `Enabled`, else `null`. Returning `null` matters: it means "no passive block", which is different from a block with `Enabled = false`.
  - `BuildActive()` (`:74-87`): the same shape, copying `Policy`, `Interval`, `Timeout` and `Path`.
- **Why it's built this way**: two independent filters rather than one, so each owns exactly one YARP config property. `AddMmcaGateway`'s own doc records the consequence (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:46-50`): the profile filter owns `HttpRequest`, the health-check filter owns `HealthCheck`, and neither reads what the other wrote, so their relative registration order carries no meaning. That is a real simplification: ordered filters would be a hidden coupling nobody would notice until someone reordered them.
- **Where it's used**: registered by `AddMmcaGateway` (`GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:118`). With both consumer gateways enabling active probes in configuration, this filter is what turns that flag into a real `ActiveHealthCheckConfig` on every cluster (`MMCA.ADC/Source/Hosts/MMCA.ADC.Gateway/appsettings.json:42-47`, `MMCA.Store/Source/Hosts/MMCA.Store.Gateway/appsettings.json:26-31`). Covered by [GatewayHealthCheckDefaultsConfigFilterTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: `ReferenceEquals` on the two blocks is a correct no-op check only because `BuildPassive` / `BuildActive` construct new objects whenever they run at all, so a rebuilt block is never reference-equal to an existing one. It is an identity check, not an equivalence check, which is a deliberate difference from the profile filter's property-by-property `IsSameAs`.

### GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory

> MMCA.Common.Gateway · `MMCA.Common.Gateway.Forwarding` · `MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory.cs:12` · Level 3 · class (internal sealed)

- **What it is**: YARP's own `ForwarderHttpClientFactory`, subclassed to install [GatewayConnectRetryHandler](#gatewayconnectretryhandler) as the outermost handler of every cluster client, so a retried attempt runs through the whole inner pipeline again (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/Forwarding/GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactory.cs:7-11`).
- **Depends on**: YARP's `ForwarderHttpClientFactory` and `ForwarderHttpClientContext`, `ILoggerFactory` / `ILogger`, `IOptions<GatewaySettings>` from which it keeps only the [GatewayRetrySettings](#gatewayretrysettings) block, and `TimeProvider` (`:14-18`, `:27-39`). Registered by [GatewayReverseProxyExtensions](#gatewayreverseproxyextensions) only when [GatewaySettings](#gatewaysettings)`.Retry.Enabled` is true.
- **Concept introduced, extend the proxy's client factory rather than replace it.** `[Rubric §2, Design Patterns]`, `[Rubric §29, Resilience & Business Continuity]`. YARP builds one `HttpMessageInvoker` per cluster through `IForwarderHttpClientFactory`, and its default factory already carries the socket and activity-tracking setup a proxy needs. Overriding the one hook `WrapHandler` and delegating to `base.WrapHandler` first (`:64-68`) keeps all of that and adds a single decorator on the outside, so the retry sees each attempt exactly as YARP would have sent it.
- **Walkthrough**:
  - Three readonly fields: the retry logger, the retry settings and the clock (`:14`, `:16`, `:18`).
  - The `public` constructor (`:27-39`), the one the container uses: it passes `base` a logger created for `ForwarderHttpClientFactory` through `CreateYarpLogger` (`:31`, `:70-75`), so YARP's own factory logging keeps its category, and creates the retry logger under the `GatewayConnectRetryHandler` category (`:36`).
  - The `internal` constructor (`:49-61`) takes one non-generic `ILogger` and leaves YARP's factory logging off. It is `internal` on purpose: the container must see exactly one public constructor, because a host that also registers a non-generic `ILogger` would otherwise make the choice ambiguous (`:41-45`).
  - `WrapHandler` (`:64-68`) returns a new `GatewayConnectRetryHandler` whose `InnerHandler` is whatever `base.WrapHandler` produced.
- **Why it's built this way**: registration is guarded so it can never silently displace a factory the host composed. `AddConnectRetry` (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:130-155`) collects every non-keyed `IForwarderHttpClientFactory` registration, throws an `InvalidOperationException` naming the foreign one (or "a factory delegate") when anything other than YARP's default or this type is present (`:137-146`), returns early when this type is already registered so a second call is idempotent (`:148-151`), and otherwise adds `TimeProvider.System` with `TryAddSingleton` and this factory with `AddSingleton` (`:153-154`). The doc explains the plain `AddSingleton`: it runs after `AddReverseProxy`'s own `TryAdd`, so the last registration wins (`:123-126`).
- **Where it's used**: the private `Wire` body shared by both `AddMmcaGateway` overloads calls `AddConnectRetry` when `settings.Retry.Enabled` is true (`GatewayReverseProxyExtensions.cs:108-111`). Covered by [GatewayRetryForwarderHttpClientFactoryTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup) and by [AddMmcaGatewayTests](group-29-testing-infrastructure.md#per-project-test-rollup).
- **Caveats / not-in-source**: the retry budget is bounded by validation attributes on the settings, one to three extra attempts (default one) and a delay of at most five seconds (default 200 ms) (`MMCA.Common/Source/Hosting/MMCA.Common.Gateway/GatewaySettings.cs:66-72`). Neither the ADC nor the Store gateway `appsettings` file sets `MmcaGateway:Retry`, so this factory is not registered in either app as configured in source.
